95aa336da4cadbef6e83fdb2f0cedc0d4603cc92
2026-08-15
fabric: raise FAB_LR_AMIN to 0.15
An expert at the envelope floor should oscillate between 5% and 19% of peak,
roughly where a cosine run's late rate sits, not between 5% and 10%. The
degenerate case this guards is not subtle: if FAB_LR_CYCLE is short relative
to how often the router selects, every expert reaches a vanishing envelope
early and the population trains at ~0 for most of the run. `cycle min..max`
on the [lr] line says which regime a run is in.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 <noreply@anthropic.com>

914613658d9a82f849bb2774058d7083e1d24864
2026-08-15
fabric: per-expert Smith triangular2 on a USE clock; use-based grace; balance floor
The expert lifecycle ran on a wall clock, which asks the wrong question of
every reader. Grace wants "has this expert had enough chances yet"; the
per-expert LR wants "how far through its own learning is it". Under a wall
clock an expert the router rarely calls reads as fully-aged after FAB_GRACE
steps of receiving almost no gradient -- culled for failing at a job it was
never given, and annealed to the floor before it was ever trained.

  - fab.uage: selections since birth or rescue. One bump_use() credits both
    `use` (fitness, comparative) and `uage` (the expert's own clock), which
    were incremented at three separate sites. Reset on grow, spawn and
    rescue; carried through remove()'s swap-with-last; checkpointed, and
    backfilled to the grace threshold on resume so a restored population is
    cullable rather than handed a fresh grace period and a fresh high-LR
    cycle.
  - Per-expert LR is Smith's triangular2 clocked in SELECTIONS, phase-shifted
    so a newborn starts at the peak rather than the base. Two experts at the
    same step are at different points in their cycle, which is the point:
    the population is never all exploring or all consolidating at once.
    FAB_LR_CYCLE (half-cycle), FAB_LR_GAMMA (envelope, 0.5 = triangular2
    exactly), FAB_LR_AMIN (envelope floor). Verified: peaks 100/52.5/28.7/
    16.9% of LR, troughs at LR_MIN_FRAC.
  - FAB_LR_AMIN exists because gamma^(cyc-1) goes to zero and use-age has no
    horizon: the smoke reached cycle 90 on six experts, i.e. every survivor
    permanently pinned at the floor and unable to respond to a shift. Age
    should lower the ceiling, not close it.
  - Grace is now a use-age, and the cull ranks WITHIN the eligible set. That
    is not cosmetic: the bottom of a raw utilization ranking is by definition
    the population with the least use-age, so taking the bottom cull_frac
    globally and then skipping the ungraced spends the whole budget on
    entries it is guaranteed to skip and removes nobody, ever.
  - FAB_CULL_FRAC 0.08 -> 0.02, FAB_GRACE 3000 steps -> 48 selections,
    FAB_LR_OWN 0 -> 1 (per-expert rates were off by default), FAB_LR_BOOST
    1.0 -> 2.0 so the bottom of the eligible ranking actually gets room.
  - BAL_FLOOR: the load-balance weight decayed to exactly zero at BAL_WARM,
    after which nothing pushed routing mass outward and an unselected expert
    had no route back -- no traffic, no gradient, no improvement, still no
    traffic. Under the use clock it is also frozen at its use-age, so the
    cull can never reach it either. A small permanent floor (0.15 x
    FAB_BALANCE = 0.0015) keeps every expert accruing experience without
    preventing specialization. Balance and use-age are one mechanism.
  - manage() counts eligibility BEFORE the capacity-pressure gate. It was
    counted after, so a below-pressure population reported "0 past their
    grace" in the same line as a successful cull from the sustained-error
    route, while experts sat at use-age 4287.
  - Guard against remove()'s renumbering invalidating a precomputed ranking.
  - FAB_LR_SPAN retired: it scaled a per-expert cosine across a wall-clock
    lifetime and has nothing left to denominate.

daf9f89a2c8add3b0864265b6ee74f6fccc179b9
2026-08-15
memory: give eviction a real signal -- retrieval during training, LRU on use
The eviction rules ranked a constant. mem.read() was called from exactly two
places, generate() and bpb_true(), both eval-only, so during training `use`
stayed 0 for every entry and `last` was never written at all on the global
store (read() stamped it only when n_own > 1, and _store() only on the
partitioned path). EVICT=usage therefore broke ties arbitrarily and every
other path evicted by write order, whatever the knob said.

That is the mechanism behind the vanished English domain: English was not
less useful after the Python run, it had merely stopped being WRITTEN, and
nothing in the training loop could observe that its entries were still
being retrieved.

  - MEM_PROBE_EVERY / MEM_PROBE_N: cadenced read probe in the training loop,
    real retrievals against the text being trained on. Deterministic stride,
    not a random draw -- consuming stream RNG would make the probe cadence
    change the trajectory. Nothing feeds the forward pass or the loss.
  - read() stamps `last` unconditionally; _store() stamps it on every path.
  - EVICT=lru: least-recently-RETRIEVED dies. Use-based recency, so a quiet
    domain that still answers queries survives and a loud one nothing asks
    for does not. Now the default.
  - RESUME restores `last`/`tick` on the global store. Without it every
    restored entry is the oldest thing in the store and is evicted before
    anything written after the resume -- the same failure at the boundary.
  - The epoch line reports retrieved-count and probe-count, and shouts if
    the probe ran and nothing was ever retrieved. A selection rule with no
    signal was invisible for the whole project; it is not invisible now.
  - _SPEC: MEM_PER_EXPERT default 0 (the previous commit changed the call
    site but not the declaration, which would have been a SystemExit at the
    read), EVICT default lru, and the two new knobs.

mem_evict_test.py drives a store two ways -- a domain retrieved but not
written vs neither -- and asserts it can tell them apart, with EVICT=recency
as the control that cannot. Measured: lru keeps 70/100 of a read domain and
0/100 of an unread one; recency keeps 0 either way.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 <noreply@anthropic.com>
Claude-Session: https://claude.ai/code/session_01PksNp6ECrYm34zjV2rbm4x

e25d9b5ab152d20a426b59f563f13f5a80a1759b
2026-08-15
memory: honor the documented MEM_PER_EXPERT=0 default; gate LR boost on grace
MEM_PER_EXPERT's comment recorded a measurement and a decision ("DEFAULT OFF")
that the code never implemented -- it read _i("MEM_PER_EXPERT", 1), so every
run in this project used the partitioned store. The partition is also the
mechanism behind the vanished English domain: owners are experts folded mod
MEM_OWNERS, both domains route to overlapping experts, and intra-block
eviction is LRU on `last`, which is write-recency -- so a domain that stops
being written is evicted oldest-first by construction.

Also gate FAB_LR_BOOST on fab.age(i, step) >= FAB_GRACE. A newborn sits at
the bottom of a utilization ranking because it has not had a chance yet, not
because it is failing, and it already carries the high newborn rate from its
own schedule. The boost is for experts that have had their safe phase and
spent it badly -- the same population soft_cull ranks.

f8599b71d587b7d10174ee8fbc7fe82cc3114a08
2026-08-15
mask_dead missed retired ids, which are not a suffix
The mask covered [vocab_size:] -- the never-minted tail -- and nothing else. But
probation RETIRES a token by popping it from seq2id while leaving id2bytes
intact, deliberately, so ids stay positional and old checkpoints keep loading. A
retired id is therefore BELOW vocab_size, can never be a target again, and went
straight through the mask.

Not a rounding error on the arms that use it: prob_use and prob_emb retired 217
and 224 of 256 minted tokens. Those rows sat in the softmax denominator for the
whole run, which is the exact condition LOSS_MASK_DEAD exists to remove.

Now a cached boolean over the full width -- the tail plus the holes -- keyed on
(vocab_size, retired count), both of which only grow, so it rebuilds when the
vocabulary moves rather than per call. Verified directly: with vocab_size=300
and retired={256,271} out of width 512, exactly 214 ids mask (212 tail + 2
retired), 256 and 271 among them, and live id 257 is untouched.

Found by the tokenizer-literature agent, which also pointed at Picky BPE
(EMNLP 2024): its IoS criterion is algebraically the same statistic as this
repo's p(b|a) read in the opposite direction -- it deletes the part where we
mint the pair -- and we compute it for both halves and use only one.

Also landing notes/research_{experts_routing,continual_memory}.md and the
finished DOC_PLAN.md. All four research agents are unreviewed, and WebFetch was
blocked for every paper host this session, so almost nothing in them was read in
full; each carries per-claim evidence tags and the tags are mostly "memory,
unverified". Treat as leads.

Two of their findings are verified against this code and are not leads:
  holdout_bpb() calls _eval_logits, which does not consult memory -- so ACROSS
    THE RUN BOUNDARY, "the ONLY number that spans the run boundary", is a
    WEIGHTS-ONLY retention number. bpb_true is the only path that interpolates
    the store. The eng-HELD result therefore measured the weights, which is
    consistent with every English memory entry having been evicted, but it is
    not what the line's wording implies.
  HEAD's fabric defaults are arm D. FAB_GROW=1, FAB_N0=3, FAB_NMAX=4096 is the
    configuration the 2x2 measured at 3.384 with spread 2.074, not arm B's
    1.999/0.080. Nothing was changed after that result.

    runs at different EPOCHS stop being comparable.

752b1ff1e383b0b70296f5185e939e411ae14ad3
2026-08-15
stop a sweep without killing it, and give failing experts room to move
_stopped: `touch runs/<dir>/STOP` and the loop finishes the run it is on, then
stops before starting the next. Ctrl-C kills the CURRENT run too, losing however
many hours it is into it, and killing the shell orphans the python -- neither is
what "stop after this one" means, and there was no third option. Wired into
seeds, grid and repeat; STOP_FILE overrides the path.

FAB_LR_BOOST: the bottom of the utilization ranking gets a HIGHER own-rate, not
just a shorter life. An expert in the cull-eligible fraction is already failing,
and annealing it on the same curve as a thriving one spends its remaining life
confirming that. The boost applies to the bottom FAB_CULL_FRAC by utilization --
the same ranking the cull uses, so the two agree on who is in trouble, and the
response to trouble is exploration before removal. Pairs with FAB_RESCUE, which
does the same thing in weight space at the moment of the cull: this acts
continuously and earlier, that one is the last chance.

Verified: with FAB_LR_BOOST=3, experts span 1.00e-04..4.24e-03 while the global
rate has annealed to 1.54e-04 -- the failing ones running above the nominal peak
while the settled ones sit at the floor.

Both off by default (FAB_LR_BOOST=1.0, no STOP file).

a5cc7ea37b3e05ea84be3301e2e0a715e219cb8f
2026-08-15
a missing birthday made an expert immortal; now it makes it cullable
91fd815 stamped the founding population in __init__, which closed the fresh-run
case and left two open.

PERSISTENCE. fab_cfg recorded how MANY experts a checkpoint had and not one
birthday, so every RESUME rebuilt the bug on the path continual learning depends
on: 2048 experts restored, ages all reading 0, none of them ever cullable. The
checkpoint now carries fab_born, and a checkpoint written before it existed gets
its experts backfilled to step 0 -- with a line saying how many, because "these
are old because we know they are" and "these are old because we know nothing"
should not look the same in a log.

DIRECTION OF FAILURE. `s.born.get(i, step)` returns `step` for a missing entry,
i.e. reports the expert as newly born, and everything downstream protects the
young: soft_cull skips anything inside FAB_GRACE, the FAB_NEW_FRAC budget counts
recent births, per-expert rates hand newborns the high rate. So the fallback was
the dangerous one in all three. fab.age(i, step) defaults to born-at-0 instead,
so an unrecorded expert reads as maximally OLD -- cullable, established, mature
rate. No future creation path that forgets to stamp a birthday can bring this
back.

Measured, same config and seed, 24 founders with FAB_GRACE=100:
  ac79e92 (before)  0 culls, population stays 24 -- nothing was ever removable
  HEAD    (after)   6 culls, population 24 -> 8
That is what "arm B ran with no selection at all" meant, and this is the run
that shows it rather than infers it.

ExpertRouter has its own born dict and the same idiom, but every expert there is
created through _new(), which stamps one -- there is no founding population to
miss. Left alone: EXPERTS=0 by default and changing it would add risk for no
defect.

91fd815e0324fc6ff6fa6e613e3ec13ba3964aa5
2026-08-15
a decaying envelope, per-expert rates, and the founders had no birthday
LR_DECAY: the fluctuation stays, its ceiling comes down. The repeating cosine
returned to 100% of peak at every restart forever, and an 18-epoch run showed
what that costs -- 1.00e-04 -> 2.00e-03 at step 201925, after which the held-out
curve swings 1.5 b/B and never resettles. LR_DECAY scales each cycle's PEAK by
the run's overall progress, so every cycle keeps its own high phase to move in
and its own anneal to consolidate, while the envelope falls monotonically.
Measured over three cycles: peaks 100/100/100% at LR_DECAY=0 (unchanged),
100/88/64% at 0.5, 100/76/29% at 1.0.

FAB_LR_OWN: each expert on its own schedule, clocked from its own birth, so an
expert born at step 40000 gets a real exploration phase instead of being born
into whatever the run has decayed to and never moving.
  NOT via param_groups: fab.A and fab.B are single (cap,d,r) tensors -- the whole
    population is two parameters, so a group cannot carry a per-expert rate.
  NOT via gradient scaling either, and this is the trap: Adam's update is
    m_hat/(sqrt(v_hat)+eps), invariant to a constant factor on the gradient, so
    the obvious implementation does exactly nothing.
  Instead the UPDATE is rescaled: keep the pre-step weights, let the optimizer
    step at the global rate, move each row back along its own delta. Exact for
    any optimizer. Costs one clone of the live rows per step.
Verified: global 9.65e-04 with experts spanning 1.00e-04..1.99e-03 by age.

AND A PRE-EXISTING BUG IT SURFACED. s.born was written only by grow(), so the
initial FAB_N0 experts had no entry and every reader fell back to `step` --
reading their age as 0, forever. Three things ran on that:
  soft_cull skips anything younger than FAB_GRACE, so the FOUNDING POPULATION
    WAS PERMANENTLY IMMUNE TO CULLING. At FAB_N0=2048 that is all of it: arm B,
    the best result on record, ran with no selection whatsoever.
  the FAB_NEW_FRAC budget undercounted recent births by exactly the founders.
  per-expert rates handed every founder the newborn rate for the whole run.
Found because the diagnostic printed "experts 2.00e-03..2.00e-03 (x4.00..x4.00)"
-- every expert identical and clamped, which is not a schedule.

The diagnostic itself was also printing on step//20000, which never advances in a
short run, so it fired once at step 3 when everything genuinely IS newborn and
every ratio IS clamped -- the one moment it cannot say anything. On RATE_EVERY now.

All three off by default: LR_DECAY=0, FAB_LR_OWN=0.

ac79e92a3d25568d29a403cf2784d08431b556b4
2026-08-15
the warmup ramp is not a cosine restart
The detector fires on the rate RISING, which is right for a restart and wrong
for the warmup: it climbs from 0, so the first few steps multiply the rate by
far more than 1.5. An 18-epoch run reported three "cosine restarts" -- at step
15 (2% of peak), step 31 (3%), and step 201925 (100%). Only the third is one.

Harmless in effect, since nothing has grown by step 15, but it puts two false
entries above the one that matters, and a log that cries restart is a log nobody
greps for restarts. A real restart returns the rate to a large fraction of peak,
so require that too.

The one at 201925 is the reason this is worth fixing now: 1.00e-04 -> 2.00e-03
deep into training is a 20x jump back to full peak, and the held-out curve after
it swings by 1.5 b/B and never resettles (3.82 3.29 3.04 4.01 3.39 4.04 4.08 ...
3.92 3.19 2.58 3.85 3.63). The two seeds whose base model reads 5.612 and 5.268
are the ones that ended near a restart; the one at 3.023 landed better in the
cycle. That is the entire 0.364 spread.

Recorded ep18_big_s{0,1,2}. Note for anyone reading those rows: the corpus was
re-fetched larger for this run and got HARDER (order-1 3.440 -> 3.747), so the
raw held-out is not comparable to the 8-epoch arm B. Against each run's own
order-1 anchor it is 1.411 vs 1.441 -- 18 epochs bought nothing, and did not
cost the 0.34 the raw numbers suggest either.

6d5e6d775e55fd84ca05c9c54394cd7d51cd3310
2026-08-15
FAB_BURST=1 and a 4% newborn cap, against an 8% cull
FAB_CULL_FRAC=0.08 is the share of the population considered for removal per
manage event, so a growth allowance at half of it leaves selection strictly able
to outpace growth. That is what makes the population a population rather than a
queue -- previously growth could add 10% per event while the cull considered 8%,
so pressure ran the wrong way. The burst floor drops to 1 for the same reason:
the smallest honest growth step is one expert, and with it the FRACTION is in
charge from n=10 up instead of n=30.

  n       max grow/event   % new    after the 4% cap
      3                1   33.3%    1
     12                1    8.3%    1
     25                2    8.0%    1
     64                6    9.4%    2
   2048              204   10.0%    81

The cadences are NOT matched and the comment says so: growth is capped per
FAB_NEW_WIN (400 steps) while the cull runs every MANAGE_EVERY (50). Culling is
gated on capacity pressure and skips anything inside FAB_GRACE so it does not
fire 8x more often in practice -- but if a population trends DOWN, that
asymmetry is the first place to look.

Also --min-score for fetch_big.py. I checked the corpus before changing it and
the seed passages are genuine prose ("...can hear sounds as low as 20 Hz",
"...an ideal chronicler in Ross King", "...inactivate antibiotic-resistant
S. enterica"), documents are separated with a blank line, and sub-200-character
documents are already dropped -- so the pipeline was not the problem. What was
missing was a way to ask for a cleaner slice: fineweb-edu carries its own
educational-quality classifier score, and --min-score filters on it. The count
of skipped documents is printed, and a gate that skipped NOTHING says so
explicitly -- "every document passed" and "this dataset has no such field" look
identical otherwise, and only one of them is what was asked for.

f4b2e9bafb7e62d87cb36b0042e2c21addc05564
2026-08-15
cap the newborn FRACTION, not just the growth rate
There was a rate and no cap on percent-new, and the difference is the largest
effect measured here. max(FAB_BURST=3, FAB_RAMP_RATE=0.10 * n) is 10% only once
n >= 30; at n=3 it is 100%, at 6 it is 50%, at 12 it is 25%. And 10% every
FAB_COOLDOWN//8 = 50 steps COMPOUNDS -- over 400 steps the population doubles, so
"10% per event" permits ~114% new per cooldown window.

Same ramp, same destination of 4096 experts, differing only in where it started:
  ramp 3    -> 4096   4.327 / 3.572 / 2.253   mean 3.384, spread 2.074
  ramp 2048 -> 4096   1.994 / 2.097 / 1.937   mean 2.009, spread 0.160
From 2048 no event exceeds 10%; from 3 the first ten events are 25-100%. The
damaging quantity is the FRACTION newborn at once, not the count -- 4096 experts
are fine if they arrive slowly enough.

FAB_NEW_FRAC caps exactly that: at most this fraction of the live population may
have been born within FAB_NEW_WIN steps, which binds the burst floor and the
compounding together in a way a per-event rate cannot. Growth takes whatever is
left of the budget rather than being refused outright, and the decline is logged
once per window rather than per event.

ON BY DEFAULT at 0.10. It leaves the asymptotic ramp rate untouched -- already
10% -- and removes only the small-n blow-up and the compounding. FAB_NEW_FRAC=0
restores the old behaviour.

A DEADLOCK THE TEST CAUGHT: int(0.10 * 3) is 0, so the first version blocked the
bootstrap completely -- a population starting at FAB_N0=3 reached 7 (via spawn)
instead of 256, every ramp event declined. max(1, ...) makes it slow rather than
stuck, and the fraction takes over once n >= 1/FAB_NEW_FRAC. After: 256 uncapped
with a 100% worst event, 15 capped with a 33% worst event -- 33% being 1-of-3,
the best achievable at n=3, and honest about it.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 <noreply@anthropic.com>

c9099188d51e09284e6751d7d49b081a92f8478d
2026-08-15
the config audit could only catch knob families it had been told about
Six knobs were set deliberately -- GROW_CAP, GROW_CAP_FAB0, GROW_CAP_VOCAB0,
GROW_CAP_EVERY, GROW_CAP_PLATEAU, LOSS_MASK_DEAD -- on a build (e9f2e58) that
predated all of them. Every one was ignored, the run said nothing, and an hour
of GPU produced a different experiment from the one requested. The net built for
exactly this ("you set a knob that does nothing") did not fire, because it was an
ALLOWLIST of prefixes -- FAB_, ROUTE_, TOK, ... -- and a brand-new family is
precisely when the mistake is most likely.

Families now come FROM _SPEC: every knob's first underscore-delimited token is a
family, so registering a knob extends the net automatically. GROW_EVERY already
existed, so GROW_CAP* would have been caught.

Deny-listing all non-system variables instead was tried first and is worse: on
this box it flagged AI_AGENT, AWS_*, BUN_* and thirty more, and a warning that
cries wolf is a warning nobody reads. Generic tokens (MAX/MIN/USE/NEW/...) are
dropped for the same reason -- MAX_DOMAINS made MAX a family, which collected
MAX_THINKING_TOKENS -- as is _plumb, whose members are read conditionally by
design (PROBE_WAIT only when PROBE=1). Verified: FAB_TYPOO and GROW_CAPX flagged,
nothing else.

The message now names the commit, since "a knob from a different commit" is what
actually happened and reads nothing like a typo.

Recorded the accidental runs as rampfrom2048_s{0,1,2} -- they measure something
worth having, just not what was asked for.

41d2c5dffbafbfdbea35136c96a190fe6d118904
2026-08-15
the soft cap has to bind both growth doors, not one
spawn_from creates an expert whenever a mid-chain router query finds no near
match. It is independent of PlateauGrowth, so it ignores FAB_GROW as well as the
soft cap -- which is why a FAB_GROW=0 run still drifts 3 -> 6 experts, and why
"population frozen at FAB_N0" was never quite true. Now clamped by the same soft
cap as the fabgrow path. A cap that binds one of two doors is not a cap.

Verified the gradual-growth path end to end, which is what arm B needs to scale:
  [capacity @ 2156] experts pinned at 16 and the loss has stalled
                    (improving -0.0021 < 0.002) -> soft cap 16 -> 32
  [fabric @ 2184]   stall -> grew 1 -> 17/64 experts
Cap lifts only on pinned AND stalled; growth then fills it one expert at a time;
ZERO ramp events across the run. Starting the population AT the soft cap latches
the ramp off on the first check (n >= ramp_to * cap), so the 377 -> 4085-in-600-
steps injection that destroyed arm D cannot occur -- which is the whole point of
routing growth through a cap that has to be earned.

cc0a377a95eae3d36debfc66bd593a10b59a2e80
2026-08-15
the 2x2: size was never the problem, ramping to size is
Four arms, three seeds each, one knob apart.

  arm                                seeds                 mean   spread
  A  FAB_GROW=0, N0=3   (~6)         2.047 2.315 1.989     2.117    0.326
  B  FAB_GROW=0, N0=2048             1.998 1.960 2.040     1.999    0.080
  C  FAB_GROW=1, NMAX=64             2.163 2.127 1.983     2.091    0.180
  D  FAB_GROW=1, NMAX=4096           4.327 3.572 2.253     3.384    2.074

Read it along the two axes and it is not ambiguous:
  growth OFF, 6 -> 2048 experts:  2.117 -> 1.999   a large population is FINE
  growth ON,  64 -> 4096 experts: 2.091 -> 3.384   a large population is fatal
  at 4096, growth OFF vs ON:      1.999 -> 3.384   the entire effect

So it is the interaction, not either term. Ramping to 64 costs nothing. Holding
2048 from step 0 costs nothing. RAMPING to 4096 is what destroys the base model
-- consistent with the mechanism the seed logs showed: the ramp injects ~4000
mutated clones into the path between the base representation and the loss over
~600 steps, and HALT MASS is 0.0000 so the base head has no direct path out.
Born-at-once experts are exact identities (B is zero-initialised), so arm B
inserts nothing; it differentiates from a no-op.

B IS THE BEST ARM ON RECORD, and by the margin that matters: spread 0.080 across
three seeds, against 2.074 for the current default. seed1's 1.960 is the single
best number this project has produced. For the first time a configuration is
reproducible enough that a 0.1 b/B difference between two arms would mean
something.

The fabric also only earns its keep in B: contribution +0.225/+0.293/+0.106,
against +0.022/+0.001/-0.006 in C (64 experts contribute nothing at all) and a
meaningless +6.183 in D seed0, where it is only large because the base model it
is compensating for reads 10.338.

Recorded as popB_n2048_s{0,1,2} and popC_nmax64_s{0,1,2}.

e2db890497685595c6650949ddeb976d038ea053
2026-08-14
capacity that is earned: rescue-before-cull, and soft caps that lift on plateau
Three mechanisms, all off by default, all verified to fire.

FAB_RESCUE -- selection pressure raises the MUTATION RATE, it does not only
prune. An expert at the bottom of a utilization ranking is not necessarily bad;
it may sit in a basin the router never sends anything to, and culling replaces it
with a fresh random expert no more likely to escape. It now gets one large jump
from where it already is (the same heavy-tailed mutation grow() gives offspring,
scaled to its own weights) and a restarted grace clock. If it comes back up for
culling having still earned nothing, it goes. The rescued set is renumbered
alongside remove()'s swap-with-last -- without that a survivor inherits the
removed expert's "already had its chance" and is culled on sight.

GROW_CAP -- FAB_NMAX and VMAX are numbers chosen before the run, and are then
either wasted or binding, with no way to revisit. The soft cap lifts by
GROW_LIFT only when BOTH the population is pinned against it AND the loss has
stalled. Pinned-but-improving means the capacity in hand is still paying;
stalled-but-not-pinned means the limit is not what is stopping it. The
conjunction is the rule. Hard ceilings do not move -- the fabric preallocates
FAB_NMAX slots (unused rows are zero-B identities, exact no-ops) and the model
allocates VMAX rows -- so a lift hands out capacity that already exists and no
tensor is reallocated.

The vocabulary lift is only honest with LOSS_MASK_DEAD=1, since reserved rows
otherwise sit in the denominator; the banner says which case it is in.

TWO BUGS THE TEST CAUGHT, both mine:
  The rescue path fired zero times on the first attempt and I nearly reported it
  as working -- FAB_GRACE=3000 on an 1800-step run means nothing is ever old
  enough to cull. It also had no counter in the log, which is the same shape as
  retire_stale and fuzzy_segment: a maintenance path with no counter cannot be
  told from one that silently stopped. It reports now.
  The plateau gate lifted at step 7 on `improving +0.0000` -- fast and slow are
  both seeded from the FIRST loss, so "not improving" was an initialisation, not
  a reading. Now requires GROW_CAP_EVERY observations first.

Verified firing: 8 rescues logged with counts; experts 6 -> 12; vocabulary
320 -> 640 -> 1024 across two separate plateaus. Quality effects at this toy
scale (3.554 / 3.650 / 3.447) are noise and are not evidence for any of it.

e9f2e58a952359e9e095f9c5eef4df488a5da9cb
2026-08-14
mask never-minted ids out of the distribution (LOSS_MASK_DEAD, off by default)
V is VMAX -- the softmax WIDTH, fixed before training -- while the vocabulary is
whatever the tokenizer has minted. Ids in between index no byte sequence, can
never be a target, and take only the push-down half of the cross-entropy
gradient while still taking mass off the tokens that can occur. Masking them is
not a reweighting; it is the correct denominator, and it also makes bits/byte
comparable across VMAX, which it currently is not.

I got the placement wrong first, and the measurement caught it. Masking at the
LOSS ONLY is worse than not masking: the model is never taught to push the dead
rows down, and every eval path then scores it with those untrained rows still in
the denominator. On a config with 86.7% of the width never minted:
  unmasked                     4.746 +/- 0.043
  masked at the loss only      6.100 +/- 0.074      <- worse
  masked everywhere            4.686 +/- 0.034
mask_dead() is now applied wherever logits become a distribution -- the loss
(which training reaches directly) and fab_logits (which every eval path reaches
through), so both are covered and neither is masked twice.

AND THE RESULT IS NOT WHAT THE RECORD IMPLIES. +0.060 against a combined SE of
0.055 is 1.1 sigma -- a hint, not a finding. The "dead rows are catastrophic and
monotone" curve this repo has been quoting (0% -> ~2.2, 41% -> 3.561,
75% -> 6.114) came from comparing arms that differed in far more than their dead
fraction, measured through the instrument that was editing runs. This is the
first CONTROLLED test of it -- same seed, same config, one knob -- and it does
not reproduce that magnitude. The correlation may still be real at pilot scale
and on a longer run; it is not established, and I have been repeating it as
though it were.

Off by default: it changes every number, and it should be adopted as a measured
arm rather than assumed. It is also the enabling change for a raisable VMAX --
with the mask on, reserved headroom costs nothing, so a vocabulary that can grow
mid-run stops being a tax on the domain already being learned.

40de03d30a8c27000d9d859c64673911dd9505d7
2026-08-14
pilot-add never created $OUT, so tee had nowhere to write the report
`pilot` runs `mkdir -p "$OUT"`; `pilot-add` never did. tee opens its output file
at process start, before python writes a byte, so on any machine that has run
`seeds` but not `pilot` the directory does not exist, tee fails instantly, and
the whole report goes to a closed pipe. The worst version of a failure: hours of
GPU, a valid checkpoint written, and no record of what it measured.

Three fixes, all cheap and all about not losing a result again:

  mkdir -p "$OUT" before the run.
  ONE reserved name for the checkpoint and its log. _reserve was called twice,
    independently, so a second add could put the checkpoint at pilot_gru_py-2
    and its log at pilot_py.log -- a result filed under a name that does not
    match the model that produced it.
  PRINT both paths before spending the GPU. The log lands under $OUT, which is
    not where the checkpoint being RESUMED lives, and nothing on the command
    line hints at that.

And holdout.py, because the measurement was never actually lost. Every
checkpoint stores `holdout` (per-domain bits/byte with its standard error) and
`holdout_step`, so the number the run exists for survives in the model file:

  python3 holdout.py <parent-ckpt> <child-ckpt>

reconstructs ACROSS THE RUN BOUNDARY, applying the same 2-sigma test the in-run
report applies so the two cannot disagree. mmap'd so reading a small dict does
not materialise a GB of memory keys. It reports what was stored AT EACH SAVE,
which under CKPT_EVERY is not necessarily the end of the run -- stated in the
output rather than left to be assumed.

b92f35869825c14df518c3478776ee543517d123
2026-08-14
fetch_big.py could not open a gated dataset, and would not have read the-stack
Three separate things would have failed on `pilot-add py bigcode/the-stack`,
and only the first is about the token.

1. NO AUTH AT ALL. load_dataset was called with no token, so it worked only if
   the ambient huggingface-cli credential happened to be there -- "works on my
   machine", an opaque 401 anywhere else. --token now takes it explicitly and
   falls back to $HF_TOKEN / $HUGGINGFACE_HUB_TOKEN, then to the cached login.
   `token=` replaced `use_auth_token=` in datasets 2.14, so both are tried
   rather than dying on an unexpected keyword under an older pin.

2. WRONG FIELD. the-stack keeps its text in `content`; the fallback preset uses
   `text`. That fails with a KeyError AFTER authenticating, which reads like an
   auth problem and is not one. Presets added for the-stack and
   the-stack-dedup with the right field.

3. NO WAY TO SELECT A LANGUAGE. the-stack is organised as directories, not
   configs, so one language needs data_dir="data/python" and there was no
   argument for it. --data-dir added, and FETCH_ARGS passes it (and --token)
   through pilot-add.

A gated repo and a bad token also raise indistinguishable errors here, and the
fixes differ, so a 401/403/gated failure now prints both: accept the terms in a
browser (per-dataset, impossible from the CLI) AND make a token visible. It also
states whether this process saw a token at all, because "no token set" and
"token rejected" are one message otherwise.

Verified separately, since I left it as an open caveat last turn: a
command-prefix assignment does reach self_organize.py through pilot-add's `env`
line, so `FAB_GROW=0 bash longrun.sh pilot-add ...` does keep the ramp off.

Untested against the network: this sandbox reaches GitHub and PyPI only, so the
streaming path itself is unexercised, as the module docstring already notes.

2ba3ac17f0565aaec0f23fe6020781eb7931a7ff
2026-08-14
everything is kept; nothing is used unless you ask -- and asking was unsafe
Answering "is anything saved and possibly used by a following run", exhaustively.

WRITTEN, and kept: ckpt.pt (model, fabric, memory + provenance, domains, world
model, optimizer moments, held-out probe), ckpt.prev.pt, source.bin, probe.pt,
the .best copy, and the tokenizer JSON.

READ BACK: two sites in the whole tree, both gated on RESUME --
  self_organize.py:3321  torch.load(...)               the only torch.load
  self_organize.py:653   DynamicTokenizer.load(_tp)    guarded by
                         `os.path.exists(_tp) and (not TOK_ONLINE or RESUME)`
With TOK_ONLINE=1 and RESUME unset, neither fires. So nothing leaks by accident,
and the lever for doing it ON PURPOSE already exists and has never been used.

Taking that lever was unsafe, which is probably why. A checkpoint's embedding and
head are indexed BY ITS TOKENIZER. The checkpoint has always recorded tok_path
and nothing ever read it back, so resume paired the saved model with whatever
TOKENIZER_PATH the new command carried. Both failure modes are silent, because
VMAX fixes the row count so every shape matches and load_state_dict is happy:
  WRONG FILE -- resume runs/seeds/..._seed2.ckpt without setting TOKENIZER_PATH
                and you get data/dyntok.json: seed2's weights, another run's merges.
  NO FILE    -- that path missing sends the setup down the else branch, which
                mints a FRESH 512-token vocabulary; 2048 trained rows are then
                read with 512 ids' meanings.
The checkpoint now records tok_vocab and tok_merges -- a filename does not
certify contents -- and resume refuses on mismatch instead of loading a model
into a vocabulary that is not its own.

And pilot-add can now reach the checkpoints that exist. It hardcoded
RESUME="$OUT/pilot_$PA", so everything written by seeds/grid/repeat was
unreachable and continual learning could only be attempted from a run shape
nobody uses. RESUME_FROM=<dir> points it anywhere, it finds the .dyntok.json
saved beside that checkpoint, and its outputs go through _reserve so a second
add does not overwrite the first.

42d8686b00aacef022706511166c3ffb6b660a75
2026-08-14
"already complete, skipping" was not asking whether the run matched the config
An exhaustive audit of cross-run state (39 candidate channels, each adversarially
verified) found exactly one real channel, and it is not model state -- it is the
REPORTED NUMBER.

longrun.sh:567  if _done "$LOG"; then ... continue; fi
longrun.sh:57   _done() { grep -aq "SIG_MODE=learned -- ..." "$1"; }
longrun.sh:563  TAG=$(echo "${ARMFLAGS:-default}" | tr ' =' '__' | ...)

TAG is derived from ARMFLAGS alone. It is blind to EPOCHS, STREAM_LEN, D_MODEL,
SIG_WIN, MEM_QUOTA, DEVICE, PILOT_DIR and the commit -- every one of which
`seeds` reads from the environment. So the skip asks "did a log with this name
finish", which is not "is that run interchangeable with the one I want":

  EPOCHS=8  bash longrun.sh seeds 3     # runs, writes default_seed{0,1,2}.log
  EPOCHS=18 bash longrun.sh seeds 3     # SKIPS ALL THREE

and the SEEDS SUMMARY then globs those logs and prints the 8-epoch held-out
numbers under the 18-epoch banner. The wrong answer is not merely kept, it is
reported. Same shape in `grid` and `repeat`.

Each completed run now stamps a .cfg beside its log, and the skip compares it.
Matching -> skip as before. Different -> stop and print both configurations,
rather than adopting the stale result or silently overwriting a real one. A log
with no .cfg (everything predating this commit) also stops, naming the reason.

This did NOT affect the 3-seed pilot: fresh SEED_DIR, and all three logs carry
the same commit and a complete report. The audit's other 38 candidates were all
ruled out -- within one sweep every writable path is keyed by $SEED (log,
TOKENIZER_PATH, SAVE_CKPT), the corpus is mmapped read-only, _pilot_corpus runs
once outside the loop, and each seed is a separate process. Four guards do most
of the work: self_organize.py:652 (tokenizer load needs not-TOK_ONLINE or
RESUME), :3320 (the only torch.load is gated on RESUME), :3694 (SAVE_CKPT=0
returns before writing) and datastream.py:31 (ACCESS_READ).

runs.py could not ingest any post-fix log
Adding the error bar to the headline line changed `train X | held-out Y` into
`train X +/- a | held-out Y +/- b (n train / m held-out windows of W)`, and the
registry's regex predates it. Every run since that commit failed to add with
"has no 'train ... | held-out ...' line -- did the run reach its report?", which
reads as a broken RUN rather than a broken parser. A registry that silently
stops accepting new runs is worse than no registry: the gap only shows up later,
as an absence.

The optional group matches both spellings, so the existing rows still parse, and
the standard error is now carried as its own column -- it is the number that
says whether two rows differ.

8bdeca43e9a77da826885dca4fd357e615c9623f
2026-08-14
the memory store was queried in a language it was not written in
mem.ctx holds KW TOKEN IDS captured under whatever segmentation was in force
when the entry was written, and it is the input to the whole drift-survival
machinery: _rekey_amortized re-encodes keys from it on a cadence precisely so
keys track the model. It was re-encoding a STALE TOKEN SEQUENCE. A query builds
its key from the CURRENT segmentation, so the same text on the two sides
produced different id sequences and therefore different keys, and the gap
widened at every mint. The rekey pass could not close it -- it was faithfully
re-encoding the wrong input.

Measured on the probe corpus, growing the vocabulary 647 -> 1024, which is ONE
growth step where a pilot does about sixteen: 82.3% of stored contexts no longer
match what the tokenizer produces for the same bytes.

remap_mem_ctx() re-segments every active entry into the current vocabulary at
each retok and once more after the final one, before any of the report battery
reads the store. Exact in id space, because minting is APPEND-ONLY: id2bytes
never changes meaning, so an entry's bytes can always be recovered. 200,000
entries in 2.0s -- about 30 seconds across a 48k-step run. The in-flight rekey
snapshot is reset so the whole store is re-keyed from the new contexts, and the
count is printed on the tokenizer line, because an unmeasured maintenance pass
is indistinguishable from one that silently stopped working.

WHAT IT DOES NOT FIX, and the report now says so with a number. An entry's VALUE
-- "the next token was X" -- cannot be remapped: under the current vocabulary
the stream would emit a longer merge covering X and whatever followed it, and
what followed is not in the entry. Those entries vote for a target the model was
retrained away from. The [vocab] instrument already computes exactly the set of
ids the final stream carries, so it now also reports how many live entries
predict outside it.
Nor is the SPAN: a better vocabulary covers the same bytes in fewer tokens (75%
of windows re-segment shorter), so a remapped window spans less text than a live
query's does and is left-padded to width. Fixing that means storing the BYTES
instead of the ids, which changes the checkpoint format -- and RESUME is how
continual learning is meant to work here, so not on the way into a test run.

asm.tokc decays by TOKC_DECAY (0.5) at each segmentation change for the same
reason: counts banked under an older segmentation are observations of a
different distribution over the same text, not stale observations of this one,
and the histogram is read at the end against text tokenised with the FINAL
vocabulary. TOKC_DECAY=1 restores the cumulative behaviour.

None of this touches the training loss -- memory is written during training and
read only by the report -- so it was never a source of run-to-run drift in the
model. It decided whether "memory contributes +0.698" was measuring anything.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 <noreply@anthropic.com>
Claude-Session: https://claude.ai/code/session_01PksNp6ECrYm34zjV2rbm4x

8a8fb6920974f7e52f3f86a8c7d90a560f53379d
2026-08-14
the unweighted bytes/token bias flips sign with vocabulary size
The previous commit said the vocabulary-mean bytes/token "overstates, and the
overstatement grows with VMAX". The regression sweep says otherwise at small
vocabularies: at 512 tokens it reads 1.50 against 1.85 as actually used, because
the 256 single-byte seeds dominate the entry count while the stream
preferentially carries the longer merges. It reads HIGH only once the tail of
long rare entries outweighs those seeds, which is the pilot regime.

A bias whose SIGN depends on vocabulary size is worse than a constant one, and
it is the same conclusion by a better argument: the figure is not comparable
across VMAX, which is the axis these runs were being compared along.

The fix itself is unchanged and was already right. Only the reasoning in the
comments was wrong, and it is the reasoning that gets read next time.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 <noreply@anthropic.com>
Claude-Session: https://claude.ai/code/session_01PksNp6ECrYm34zjV2rbm4x

37100fb7f7cea02addd4a2d8bbe5e389acc66954
2026-08-14
the epoch roll carried a stale batch, and two figures that flattered large VMAX
Four fixes from the outstanding list, ahead of the next pilot.

1. THE PARTIAL BATCH SURVIVED THE EPOCH BOUNDARY. The roll sits above the
   accumulator, so on any epoch whose last window lands mid-batch -- at
   BATCH_W=16, fifteen times in sixteen -- up to BATCH_W-1 windows of the OLD
   stream were still queued. _bp holds (bpos, i) where `i` indexes the OLD
   tok_bs, and _resample() replaces tok_bs, so _posv read the NEW table at the
   old stream's offsets: those entries went into memory with provenance pointing
   at unrelated text. The short-slice padding hid it -- no IndexError, just wrong
   bytes -- and prompt.py's grounded recall reads a 220-byte span around that
   position, so the passage it quoted was not the passage stored. The batch is
   now dropped at the roll: <=15 windows of ~15,000 per epoch.

2. BYTES PER TOKEN WAS A MEAN OVER THE VOCABULARY, NOT OVER THE TEXT. Every
   number in the [signature] banner is about how far the loop strides through
   the text, and the loop strides through tokens in the proportions the stream
   uses them. sum(bytes_per_id)/vocab_size weights a token minted once and never
   seen again as heavily as one in every window -- and the tail is long and rare
   BY CONSTRUCTION, which is why those tokens were minted late. The figure
   overstates, and the overstatement GROWS WITH VMAX: the arm with the larger
   vocabulary got the larger claimed signature coverage for free, along the one
   axis these runs were being compared on. Now len(byte_stream)/len(stream) --
   measured, not estimated. SIG_WIN=614 was chosen off the old figure.

3. The composition report printed the same unweighted mean as "bytes each". It
   now prints both, labelled: bytes per ENTRY (a property of the vocabulary) and
   bytes per token AS USED (the compression figure).

4. THE FINAL RE-TOKENIZATION INVALIDATED NOTHING. The in-loop retok re-points
   ENC_SEQ and clears _VALT/_BL because the segmentation moved; the final one did
   neither. Under SIG_SPACE=tokens every signature in the report battery indexed
   a stale table at the new stream's offsets, and the end-of-run held-out probe
   scored validation text tokenised with the pre-final vocabulary. Inert on the
   default path (SIG_SPACE=bytes), which is why it sat there.

c76dc74842c547d52811b6fa7ad2a708518ed135
2026-08-13
the instrument was wired into the circuit: diagnostics were editing the run
"Why are we trying to measure the noise? Let's fix the issue that's coming up,
or first find it." Found it. It is not chaos and it is not the seed.

MECHANISM. build_stream() picks every segment's length with a draw from the
GLOBAL random stream, and seg_from turns that length into a read cursor
(_CUR[p] += L). The stream is rebuilt every epoch. So the bytes epoch 2 trains
on are a function of where the global generator happened to be standing when
_resample() was called -- and every diagnostic in the file was drawing from that
same generator on its own cadence. Two runs with the same seed, the same code
and the same corpus, differing only in HOW MUCH THEY MEASURED, trained on
different text.

Measured, on a 3-epoch smoke: 250,027 global draws, 23,835 of them (9.5%) taken
inside evaluation passes. Changing HOLDOUT_N from 4 to 16 -- a knob that must
not touch training -- moved 48 report lines, including "model ALONE 3.494" to
"4.306" and a sign flip on the domain-provenance verdict.

FIVE LEAKS, each an eval pass writing training state:

1. Fabric.society / Fabric.forward explored during eval. learn_regions=False was
   applied to the centroids alone. Exploration is a GRADIENT device -- an eval
   pass has no backward -- so it bought nothing and cost twice: it drew from the
   global stream, and it routed 15% of every scored window to a deliberately
   sub-optimal cold expert, so every held-out number in this project's record
   was read through a randomly degraded router.
2. The chaining path recorded UTILIZATION during eval. `use` ranks the cull,
   seeds the cold set, and names the expert discovery hands novel material to,
   so how often we measured decided which experts died. The counterfactual walk
   was already excluded here; the eval pass never was.
3. The TIMING PROBE called .backward() and cleaned up by naming the modules it
   thought it had touched. That enumeration went stale when WORLD_FEEDBACK began
   wrapping model.encode: 29 world-model parameters entered the loop holding
   gradients computed from random tokens. Bisected -- forward-only matches
   PROBE=0 exactly; with backward the runs split at the second logged step
   (6.1199 vs 6.1125) from byte-identical weights, stream and memory, and never
   rejoin. Now torch.autograd.grad, which returns gradients instead of
   accumulating them and so has no cleanup to keep in sync.
4. halt_ema and _mass_ema averaged eval passes into a figure the report prints
   as "during TRAINING".
5. The remaining probes draw directly (window shuffles, the coherence pair,
   generation). frozen_rng()/@no_rng_drift saves and restores both generators
   around them, which also covers the ones added later by someone who does not
   know this rule exists.

AND THE STREAM GETS ITS OWN GENERATOR, seeded from (SEED, epoch). Even with the
leaks closed, two arms differing in a training knob take different numbers of
draws in epoch 1, so epoch 2 handed them different text -- the knob under test
and the training data both changed. This is the levers-not-isolated problem at
its root.

VERIFIED, six runs, same seed, one knob each:
  rerun                  0 differing lines (deterministic)
  SAVE_CKPT 0 -> 1       3 lines, all checkpoint filenames. This is the knob
                         that produced the documented 1.594 b/B same-seed spread
  PROBE 1 -> 0           training bit-identical on every PHASE/manage/fabric/
                         world-model line; report differs in the 4th decimal
  HOLDOUT_N 4 -> 16      2 lines, the probe's own sample size
  EVAL_N 4 -> 16         training bit-identical
Before: 48, 104 and 115 differing lines on the same comparisons.

Also: the headline `train | held-out` line now carries an error bar. It is a
mean over at most min(24, EVAL_N) windows -- ~15 kB of text at the pilot's
WIN=256 -- and was printed bare, so part of what has been read as a difference
between arms was the sampling error of the instrument. EVAL_N 4 vs 16 moves it
0.35 b/B on a run that is otherwise byte-identical.

And an empty corpus now says so at startup instead of dying 700 lines later
inside build_stream on "Cannot choose from an empty sequence".

No result in the record predates these fixes safely: every arm comparison was
measured through an instrument that was changing the thing it measured.

79dac6c27354d09a853d2cbdd0cef2e2da26c0df
2026-08-13
per-module seeds, the restart marked self-inflicted, and a crash I had armed
FIVE FIXES, three of them in code I wrote in the last hour.

1. THE VMAX RE-ROLL FIX DID NOT WORK. Seeding once before the construction
line was not enough: build_lm() draws V*d + d*V + V for the embedding and
head, V is VMAX, and enc is built on the SAME LINE after it -- so the encoder
still moved with the vocabulary size. Measured after that attempt: encoder
step-0 loss 7.20 at VMAX=2048 against 6.93 at 4096, on a module that is not
VMAX-shaped and must not move at all. Now a seed per module (model, enc,
world, fabric), so no module's init depends on how much RNG another consumed.

2. A CRASH I ARMED 30 MINUTES AGO. Renaming probation's cadence to
_due("probation", ...) was right, but _fired is a plain dict indexed
UNGUARDED, initialised with only grow/retok/ckpt/lmcurve. The first run with
TOK_PROBATION>0 would have died on KeyError('probation') with no try/except
around it -- i.e. the fix for the armed grow bug was itself armed. _fired is
now a defaultdict, so a new cadence can never crash a run.

3. RETOK_EVERY=0 SILENTLY DISABLED SIGNATURE BATCHING. _due returns False on
n<=0 BEFORE recording, so _fired["retok"] never advances and the lookahead
clamp evaluated to about -step, flooring _H to 1 for the whole run. Every
RETOK_EVERY=0 arm therefore differed from its comparator in TWO ways. The
frozen 4.364 / frozen_nr 2.175 pair -- quoted in this file as the largest
single effect on record and used to justify the retok guard -- is not a clean
single-knob comparison. Now skipped entirely when there is no retok to bound.

4. THE CosINE RESTART WAS NOT MARKED SELF-INFLICTED. note_shift exists for
"the jump is OURS, not the data's" and is called for retok and resample, but
not for the restart -- which takes the rate from the LR_MIN_FRAC floor to full
peak in ONE step, a 20x jump, the largest self-inflicted loss jump in a
multi-cycle run. Unmarked, PlateauGrowth reads the regression as unexpected,
fires a growth burst and can enter a RECOVER lockout of up to FAB_RECOVER_MAX
steps; maybe_deepen resets dp_wait on the same spike. Detected by the rate
RISING, which only a restart does.

5. THE GUARD USED THE WRONG INVARIANT. retire() pops from seq2id and leaves
id2bytes alone, so vocab_size is unchanged while the match table -- and the
segmentation -- is not. Verified: retire() x10 left vocab_size at 321 and
moved the segmentation 3478 -> 3765 tokens. The guard now stamps
(vocab_size, len(seq2id)). Latent, and wrong in the direction that produces a
stale held-out cache.

0f967846a602e72bc5190935155b8ee04ce34feb
2026-08-13
VMAX was silently re-rolling every weight in the system
FROZEN = torch.randn(V, D) sat at module scope, unconditional, with V == VMAX.
It drew V*D numbers from the global generator BEFORE anything else was built,
so changing VMAX shifted the RNG stream for every module constructed after it
-- including the ones that are not VMAX-shaped at all. Verified directly:

  VMAX=2048  enc.weight_ih[0,:3]=[-0.03309,-0.02115, 0.02092]  cent=[-0.04928,...]
  VMAX=4096  enc.weight_ih[0,:3]=[-0.02035, 0.00378, 0.03229]  cent=[ 0.21240,...]
  VMAX=8192  enc.weight_ih[0,:3]=[ 0.02650,-0.00554,-0.02035]  cent=[ 0.02198,...]

The signature encoder and the fabric's 4096 routing centroids are not sized by
VMAX and should have been identical across those arms. Three runs "differing
only in VMAX" were three different random initialisations of the whole system.

That matters because of what this file already documents at ground_update:
two runs with byte-identical code and the same seed, differing only in whether
SAVE_CKPT was set, read 3.694 and 2.100 -- a 1.594 b/B spread from a 0.05%
perturbation. The VMAX field spans 2.132 to 3.989 = 1.857. A full re-roll of
every weight is vastly larger than 0.05%, so the non-monotonic ordering needs
no further explanation and the comparison cannot be attributed to VMAX.

FROZEN is now allocated lazily -- it is also dead weight by default, since
key_frozen is reached only under KEY_SRC=frozen and the default is "model", so
at VMAX=8192 it was an 8192xD tensor allocated on device and never read -- and
torch.manual_seed(SEED) is re-applied immediately before build_lm(), so the
encoder, fabric and world model are VMAX-invariant. This does not make the
system insensitive; it removes one large unintended perturbation.

ALSO, ARMED AND WAITING: the _due double-call fixed for retok in d0728fe was
still live for grow.

    4682: if ONLINE and TOK_PROBATION > 0 and TOK.prov and _due("grow", ...)
    4710:     if _due("grow", GROW_EVERY):        # -> TOK.maybe_grow()

_due records the step and returns True, so the probation block would have
consumed the grow event and minting would never have run: the vocabulary stops
filling VMAX and the run fills with dead rows. Inert only because
TOK_PROBATION defaults to 0 and short-circuits before _due is reached -- i.e.
it would have fired on the first prob_use or prob_emb run. Probation now uses
its own cadence key.

e2001782e8a5f4647a537f5d267bca42fc8dec32
2026-08-13
the signature-coverage projection was pinned at the VMAX=2048 value
SIG_PROJ_BPT defaulted to 2.4 -- the end-of-run bytes/token for a VMAX~2048
byte-BPE -- and was used for every vocabulary size. The projected stride came
out 256*2.4 = 614 B, exactly SIG_WIN, so projected coverage was 100% whatever
VMAX was and the warning clause was suppressed.

Three 18-epoch runs therefore printed 'covers 100% now' and nothing else while
ending at:
    VMAX=2048   2.91 B/tok   stride  745 B   82% of the loop window
    VMAX=4096   3.41 B/tok   stride  872 B   70%
    VMAX=8192   3.93 B/tok   stride 1006 B   61%
The signature encoder labels only what fits in its window; the rest of each
loop window is material it never read. At 8k it was reading three fifths.

Now projected from VMAX: 0.5*log2(V) - 2.59 fits those three measured points
to within 0.02 B/token. An estimate from three runs on one corpus, not a law
-- SIG_PROJ_BPT still overrides it, and the value only affects this warning,
never the run.

This does NOT explain why 4096 beat 2048. The defect is MONOTONE in VMAX, so
it penalises larger vocabularies and predicts 2048 > 4096 > 8192, which is the
opposite of what was measured. It makes vmax4k's win more impressive and part
of vmax8k's loss artifactual, and it is worth fixing either way.

d0728fe52a6eb036181fb840108a9cd9d7270798
2026-08-13
_due is not a predicate: my retok guard killed re-segmentation entirely
_due RECORDS the step and returns True. Asking it twice in one if/elif
consumes the event: the first call fired, the vocabulary test failed, the
second returned False because _fired had just been set. So the retok never
ran. And _last_vsz is written only inside the retok body, so it stayed at the
seed value forever and the SKIP branch could never fire either. Both paths
dead, silently.

Three 18-epoch runs at 04cbe89 trained with NO mid-epoch re-segmentation and
reported nothing about it: zero '[tokenizer @ N]' lines and zero 'retok(s)
skipped' lines in base_5 and vmax8k_5.

It also corrupted their held-out CURVE. _VALT caches the tokenized validation
text and is invalidated only by a retok; with no retok ever firing, a growing
vocabulary drifted away from a frozen reference. That is exactly the bug the
comment above _VALT.clear() describes, reintroduced. It shows up as a 1.6 b/B
disagreement between the curve's final value and the end-of-run memorization
check (base_5: 3.764 vs 2.182). frozen_nr is unaffected on both counts -- it
sets RETOK_EVERY=0, so _due returns False on n<=0 before any of this, and its
vocabulary never moves so the cache stays valid: its curve and check agree
(2.405 vs 2.356).

One _due call, nested branches.

04cbe897ee902a30b180d6e5051488227f2e150b
2026-08-12
base_nr: does re-segmenting mid-epoch earn its side effects on a GROWING vocabulary?
frozen_nr answered the question for a FIXED vocabulary, where re-segmentation
is a provable no-op: 2.175 b/B and 94% real words against frozen's 4.364 and
26%, on an identical vocabulary. That says nothing about the growing case,
where a retok genuinely does carry newly minted tokens into the stream.

RETOK_EVERY=0 does not stop re-segmentation. _resample() rebuilds the stream
at every epoch boundary and calls _retok itself (self_organize.py:2844, called
from :3922), firing the same _sigq flush and fabgrow.note_shift. So base_nr
moves re-segmentation from every 3000 steps to once per ~6000-step epoch. New
tokens still reach the stream, up to one epoch later.

Note the baseline moved under us: at HEAD the retok guard already skips every
retok after VMAX saturates, which for base@8ep is ~10 of ~16 (2048 fills after
~2.8 epochs at ~540 mints/epoch = step ~17100 of 48133). So base at HEAD is
NOT the base that scored 2.239, and re-running it measures the guard.

046fd817f1f1a09c5345a665c38cf2e5f2725fa4
2026-08-12
a retok on an unchanged vocabulary is pure damage: 2.189 b/B of it
Two arms with IDENTICAL vocabularies -- 512 minted, 441 used, 0% dead -- and
one knob between them:

    frozen     RETOK_EVERY=3000   held-out 4.364   26% real words   best at
                                                   step 2000, then +1.533
    frozen_nr  RETOK_EVERY=0      held-out 2.175   94% real words   best IS
                                                   final, still improving

frozen_nr is the best result in this project's records. It beats base (2.239,
75% words) on held-out while using more of its headroom -- 54.9% against
40.8% -- on a 512-token vocabulary against base's 2048. Its text reads as
English; frozen's is character soup.

THE CAUSE. With the vocabulary frozen, re-segmentation is provably a no-op on
CONTENT: same tokens, same greedy longest-match, byte-identical stream. frozen
fired 23 retoks and 22 of them added zero tokens. Each one still discarded the
lookahead queue, dropped the held-out token caches, and blacked out fabric
growth for FAB_COOLDOWN steps. All cost, no content change, 22 times. That is
the whole 2.189 b/B.

The [config] COUPLING banner has been DESCRIBING this for weeks -- "each retok
rebuilds an identical stream while still clearing the lookahead queue and
blacking out fabric growth" -- without anyone knowing the price.

THE FIX. A retok is refused when nothing has been minted since the previous
one. This is not coupling two knobs: RETOK_EVERY still sets the cadence
whenever the vocabulary is moving. It refuses only the case where
re-segmentation cannot change anything, and says so once, then counts the rest
for the report.

It is not only the frozen arms. EVERY run enters this state once minting
saturates VMAX -- base fills 2048 partway through and every retok after that
is the same pure-cost operation. If that costs base what it cost frozen, the
main path has been paying it in every run on record.

End-to-end verification of the guard was still running when this was pushed;
the container has rolled back three times today and unpushed work is the only
thing at risk. Static gates (parse, levers, longrun syntax) pass. I will
report the run and fix on top if it fails.

25c37eb931a542238bca52992918802fa216bac4
2026-08-12
pin SEED_VOCAB too: VMAX alone assumed a default the harness overrides
The fix for the frozen arms set VMAX to match the vocabulary, which quietly
assumed SEED_VOCAB was 512 -- true of self_organize's default and not of the
smoke harness, which sets 256. So `frozen` under smoke was straight back to
50% dead rows with a different pair of numbers.

Caught immediately, by the predictor added in the same commit:

  [config] COUPLING  TOK_MINT_UNTIL=1 freezes the vocabulary at 256 while
           VMAX=512 sizes the softmax, so 256 rows (50% of the width) can
           NEVER be a target

which is the whole argument for moving that check out from behind its
`not TOK_MINT_UNTIL` guard: it found an incomplete fix on the first run after
being written, before any GPU time.

frozen, frozen_nr and frozen2k now state SEED_VOCAB and VMAX together. An arm
has to specify the whole configuration it tests, or a harness default silently
redefines it -- which is the same failure as the arm inheriting VMAX=2048 from
the grid, one level up.

freeze6k keeps VMAX=1024 without a SEED_VOCAB pin on purpose: its vocabulary
depends on how many mints 6000 steps buy, so it cannot be stated in advance.
The predictor now warns on it instead.

The first clean pilot: base 2.239 (2048/2048 minted, 0% dead, best == final,

    frozen_nr   TOK_MINT_UNTIL=1 RETOK_EVERY=0      75% dead -> VMAX=512

TWO SYSTEMIC FIXES, because patching six arms invites a seventh:

The pre-flight shortfall predictor was guarded `not TOK_MINT_UNTIL` -- it
skipped exactly the case where the shortfall is known EXACTLY in advance,
since minting stops at a fixed step and nothing after can change the total. It
now reports the frozen case and names the VMAX that would fix it.

An unknown arm name returned "" from _flags_for, so a typo ran the DEFAULT
configuration under the misspelled arm's log name -- a result filed against an
experiment that never happened. The wildcard now returns a sentinel and both
callers refuse it: "!! unknown arm 'frozn' -- not in _flags_for. Nothing run."

Note on the audit itself: it also reported _flags_for as nested inside grid),
which was true of the checkout it read but not of the repo -- the container
rolled back to 2c705c7 twice today and I restored from origin both times. Every
other finding was re-verified against the restored file before being acted on.

2026-08-11
the mint gate starved the vocabulary in the first real pilot; fail open
I defaulted TOK_MINT_PMIN=0.10 on the strength of a 400 kB test where it
filled the vocabulary, and flagged only that it was untested at VMAX=8192.
It broke at VMAX=2048, on the project's standard pilot config:

    epoch  2: 878   3: 1091   4: 1211   5: 1284   6: 1331   7: 1379   8: 1439
    [vocab] never minted 609 (29.7% of width)
    held-out 3.600, best 2.829 at step 6000, +0.910 past its own minimum

Minting decelerated and asymptoted ~600 short of the cap. Against an ungated
baseline near 1.96 that is a whole failure mode, and a known one: dead rows
are what made vmax8k@8ep (41% dead) score 3.561 and the frozen arm (75% dead)
4.672. The median p(b|a) of everything judged was 0.029 against a 0.10
threshold, so once the top-1024 window held no passing candidate the gate
minted nothing at all, permanently.

Two changes:

FAIL OPEN. The gate may REORDER what gets minted and may never PREVENT a
mint. If nothing in the window passes, it takes the most frequent candidate
clearing min_pair -- what the ungated path would have chosen. Verified over 8
passes at VMAX=2048: pmin 0 / 0.10 / 0.15 all now reach 2048/2048, with 734
and 1103 forced mints respectively. gate_forced is reported, so a gate that
has stopped discriminating shows up in the log rather than being inferred
later from a vocabulary that came up short.

DEFAULT BACK TO 0. A filter that produced 30% dead rows at the standard
config has not earned a default. It is an arm (pgate/pgate_t) to be measured.

The [vocab] never-minted instrument is what caught this, in one line, in the
first pilot that used the new default -- which is the argument for it.

2026-08-11
smoke duplicated the arm definitions, and they had already drifted
Asked whether the smoke test was even up to date, the answer was no, within
the hour. It repeated _flags_for's contents instead of calling it, and the two
already disagreed:

    arm        smoke ran                grid runs
    prob_use   TOK_PROBATION=150        TOK_PROBATION=200
    prob_emb   TOK_PROBATION=150 ...    TOK_PROBATION=200 ...
    compose    TOK_COMPOSE=1            TOK_COMPOSE=1 TOK_MINT_NOVEL=0

Three of seven arms were configurations the grid will never run. A smoke test
that greenlights something nobody executes is worse than no smoke test.

Calling _flags_for exposed a second, larger problem: it was defined INSIDE the
grid) case branch, and a function defined in one branch does not exist in
another. smoke would have resolved every arm to the empty string -- seven
identical runs reported as seven passing arms, which is the same failure in a
worse form. Verified minimally:
    case smoke in grid) f(){...};; smoke) type -t f  ->  UNDEFINED
_flags_for and the arm documentation now live at top level, above the case, so
grid and smoke cannot describe different runs.

TOK_PROBATION_STEPS=1500 moves into smoke's COMMON env rather than an arm's
flags: it is a scale adaptation, not part of what the arm is. At 3 epochs the
5000-step default deadline never elapses, so probation would silently never
judge anything and the arm would pass while testing nothing.

Also worth recording, since it cost me a diagnosis: the smoke run that appeared
to fall through into watch) had not. I edited longrun.sh while a background
bash was executing it; bash reads a script by byte offset, so the hoist shifted
the file under the running interpreter and it resumed in another branch. Both
arms had already passed.

2026-08-11
longrun.sh smoke: does every pilot arm still reach its report?
There was no smoke entry point -- the one used earlier in this project was
ad-hoc and did not survive. Every failure this session has been of one shape:
a knob that crashes a diagnostic (the ragged-window ValueError that killed a
run AFTER its metrics printed), a name collision that swallowed the
memorization block, a gate that starved the vocabulary to 256 of 1024. None
of those need a long run to catch, and all of them cost one.

`bash longrun.sh smoke` runs every arm the pilot will use at 40 kB / 3 epochs
and asserts only that each REACHES ITS REPORT, using the same _done marker the
grid uses. Minutes on a GPU. SMOKE_ARMS scopes it to a subset.

It says out loud that the held-out numbers at this size are noise. A smoke
test that reports plausible-looking metrics invites being read as a result,
and that is its own way of wasting a day.

904742c541a2c71b3d49cdf7bf6b92ccff31fe48
2026-08-11
three findings from the pilot-matrix audit
BREAK ON A LIST THAT IS NO LONGER FREQUENCY-ORDERED. maybe_grow's candidate
loop exits at the first entry below min_pair, commented "the list is
frequency-ordered: none below". After a TOK_MINT_NOVEL re-sort it is not: the
score is (c - seen)/(1+seen)^novel, so a rarely-seen pair at count 30 can
outrank a worked-over one at count 500, and the break would discard every
viable candidate below it. Latent until now and reachable by DEFAULT since
TOK_MINT_PMIN=0.10 -- the mintnovel and composenov arms are exactly
novel>0 AND pmin>0. I could not trigger it on the corpus to hand, but the
premise the comment states is demonstrably false, so it is fixed rather than
left to fire on data that does invert the order. Frequency order still takes
the early exit; novelty order continues past.

TOK_MINT_GATE_K WAS DECLARED AND NEVER READ THROUGH _env. tokenizer.py reads
it from os.environ directly, so _ENV_READ never saw it and the config audit
reported a registry knob nobody reads. Mirrored from self_organize the way
TOK.pmin already is.

pgate WAS AN ALIAS FOR base. With 0.10 the default, "TOK_MINT_PMIN=0.10" is
an arm that changes nothing while reading as though it tests something. The
informative arm is the one that turns the gate OFF, so pgate becomes `nogate`
(TOK_MINT_PMIN=0) -- which is also the arm that reproduces pre-gate
vocabularies. pgate_c drops its now-redundant PMIN. Added prob_use and
prob_emb for the probation mechanism.

The audit also confirmed, independently, that TOK_MINT_PMIN=0 reproduces the
old vocabulary EXACTLY -- same id2bytes, 758 tokens on its corpus -- which is
what makes `nogate` a valid reproduction of every pre-gate result. Measured
here on 400 kB: pmin=0 -> 1024 tokens, pmin=0.10 -> 1010, but only 307 shared.
The gate does not trim the vocabulary at this scale, it REPLACES most of it.

9f8412b735fecdb36a26a79280f2e2965452859f
2026-08-11
probationary minting: mint provisionally, judge on evidence, un-merge on failure
A token is now minted ON PROBATION. TOK_MINT_PMIN judges a merge from
co-occurrence BEFORE the model has seen it once, which is the most statistics
alone can do and less than we can do: the token can be minted, TRAINED, and
then judged on what it turned out to be worth.

BRANCHING ENTROPY CANNOT BE THE POST-PROBATION TEST, and the reason is
structural rather than a tuning problem. Minting DESTROYS the evidence that
criterion reads: greedy longest-match consumes a+b into the merged token, so
the pair never occurs again. Measured -- mint 't'+'h', then read the pair
count after forty more passes of the same text: 0, and 0 at the instant of
the merge. A re-test can only ever fail, and did: 0 kept, 8 un-merged, 100%.
Entropy is a PRE-mint criterion by nature, and that is where it already is.

The two tests that CAN see something after the merge:
  use   -- reach TOK_PROBATION appearances within TOK_PROBATION_STEPS of being
           minted. A merge taken on a transient burst never gets there. This
           is the evidence retire_stale was written for and never given.
  embed -- ||delta|| / ||composite||: each token's vector is composite(its
           bytes) plus a residual starting at ZERO, so that ratio is exactly
           how much the token had to become that its parts did not already
           say. Near zero means the parts explain it. Needs TOK_COMPOSE=1.
           It also requires the token to have been TRAINED first -- a residual
           near zero because nothing was ever seen says nothing about a merge.

Retirement is SOFT and has to be: ids are positional, merges[] is replayed in
order by load() and every later token is built on this one's index, so
removing an id would renumber the vocabulary and invalidate every embedding
row and every checkpoint. TOK.retire() drops it from the match table only; the
text re-segments to its parts and the row goes unused.

That made the stream get FINER for the first time in this system, and it broke
a report that assumed a fixed window length: the domain-genuineness reservoir
raised ValueError on a ragged stack and killed the run after the metrics had
printed. Fixed at both ends -- the reservoir refuses a window that does not
match what it already holds, and the report keeps the modal length and counts
what it dropped rather than dying.

Default off. Verified end to end in all three modes (off / use / embed), each
reaching the full report, each giving a discriminating verdict (7 kept, 1
un-merged) rather than the degenerate 0-or-all the entropy re-test gave. The
held-out numbers at this scale are not evidence either way -- 30 kB and 3
epochs, where a single retirement re-segments much of the stream.

8103a8a50ec11ae63939354eb45ee027ddc7da63
2026-08-11
runs.csv: results in a table that can be re-checked, not in comments that cannot
Measured results were written into source comments beside the code they were
about. Right place, wrong form: a comment cannot be sorted, cannot be diffed
against a new run, cannot say which knobs produced it, and above all cannot be
CHECKED WHEN A DEFAULT MOVES. Several recorded numbers here were measured
under a learning-rate schedule that no longer exists, and read as current.

runs.py parses a finished log -- every column comes out of the log, never
typed, so a row cannot claim a configuration the run did not have:

    runs.py add <log> [--tag X]     append a row
    runs.py list [--arm X]          the table
    runs.py stale                   what no longer matches today's defaults

`stale` is the point. It reads the CURRENT defaults out of _SPEC (so it cannot
drift from the code) and reports, per row, exactly which overrides reproduce
it. Two things it gets right that a naive diff would not:

  - a BLANK column on a parsed row means the run PREDATES the knob, which is
    not the same as matching it -- that is the case that reads as current and
    is not. Reported as "<predates this knob>".
  - EPOCHS <= the default wavelength is NOT stale on the schedule, because
    LR_EPOCHS clamps to min(default, EPOCHS) and restarts fit whole cycles, so
    there is one cycle and no wrapping: max |restarts - hold| = 0.000e+00 over
    a whole 8-epoch run. Flagging those would be false, and expensively so --
    the 8-epoch baselines are the ones worth reproducing.

`manual` records a row whose log is gone, demands a --source, and stamps
"(no log)" in the commit column so it is never mistaken for a parsed run. The
707f1af pilot (base/frozen/frozen_nr) and the FABRIC on/off pair are in on
that basis, with the comments replaced by a pointer.

11 rows seeded: every run from this session plus those five. The comment that
survives in self_organize.py is the one worth keeping -- WHY freezing ever
looked good (a frozen vocabulary made _total_steps accurate, so it was the
only arm whose cosine annealed) -- which is an explanation, not a measurement.

the never-fired audit covers TOK_ANCHOR; restarts replicate at 8 epochs; gate on
THE AUDIT THAT SHOULD HAVE CAUGHT IT NOW DOES. [config-audit] !! knob was ON
and its loss term NEVER FIRED covered DIV_W, IND_W and CHAIN_SUP, and not
TOK_ANCHOR -- which is exactly how TOK_ANCHOR=0.05 came to print on the
EFFECTIVE line of every run in this project while contributing nothing. Any
weight reaching the loss through _term belongs in that list. It also names
the reason when it can: TOK_ANCHOR is gated on TOK_COMPOSE.

IS the run, _prog reaches 1.0 and 1.0 % 1.0 == 0.0 -- the rate jumped back to
PEAK on the final steps of every 8-epoch run, i.e. the one configuration that
has to reproduce earlier results was the one it broke.

The schedule now fits a WHOLE NUMBER of cycles to the run, rounding to the
nearest and stretching the period to divide it exactly. Truncating instead
left a 30-epoch run with 2 cycles and a third of its length parked at the
floor -- the same wasted tail restarts exist to remove. The period moves by at
most ~1/(2n) from nominal, a few percent, against the 11x that
EPOCHS-stretching caused.

  (a) REPLICATE  8-epoch run: max |restarts - hold| = 0.000e+00, bit-identical
  (b) IMPROVE    18 ep -> 2 complete cycles, 30 ep -> 3, every one ending
                 annealed at the 5% floor rather than mid-cycle, with only

TOK_MINT_PMIN defaults to 0.10. Measured, it fills the vocabulary rather than
starving it (1024/1024 at pilot scale, 783 merges, 279.8 candidates examined

successor distributions are sparser; the [vocab] gate line reports the
rejection rate so a starved run is visible rather than inferred.

c341921fb621ede6d79fcbc851a12a1b7033aeeb
2026-08-11
LR_RESTARTS: the cosine repeats instead of holding at the floor
LR_EPOCHS is the WAVELENGTH; a run longer than it now restarts the cosine
rather than saturating. Holding spent every later epoch at 5% of peak, and
that is measured: 12 extra epochs at the floor bought 0.009 b/B. Each cycle
now gets a fresh high-rate phase to move in and a fresh anneal to consolidate.

It keeps the property that made a fixed wavelength worth having. The rate at
a given step depends only on where that step falls inside a cycle, so it is
identical in an 8-, 18- or 30-epoch run -- EPOCHS no longer sets the learning
rate, which is what dragged E18.

Two implementation points that are easy to get wrong:
  - warmup is paid ONCE, at the start, not per cycle. The reason for warmup is
    that the optimizer state is cold, which is only true the first time.
  - the period is read from the LATCHED projection, not from _project's
    max(step+1, latched) return. That return exists so the HOLD form saturates
    once the horizon passes; under restarts it would make the wavelength grow
    with the step and each cycle longer than the last.

Verified against _lr_at lifted from the source, wavelength 45391 steps:
    step    epoch   RESTARTS=1  RESTARTS=0
    44000     7.8          5%          5%
    46000     8.1        100%          5%
    90000    15.9        100%          5%
   133000    23.4          5%          5%
The first cycle is identical under both, so an 8-epoch run is unchanged and
LR_RESTARTS=0 reproduces the 2.023 run exactly.

Also: the mint gate now verified end to end. TOK_MINT_PMIN=0.10 minted 783
merges and FILLED the vocabulary (1024/1024, 0% never minted), examining
279.8 candidates per mint against a median p(b|a) of 0.034 -- it reorders
which merges happen rather than starving minting, which is what the two
earlier broken versions did.

the registry guard caught the LR_EPOCHS default I only half-changed
Moving the declared default from 0 to 8 left two read sites still asking for
0 -- the EFFECTIVE line and the COUPLING banner -- and _env refused the run:

  [config] LR_EPOCHS is read with default 0 here but the registry declares 8.
           Change one of them; they cannot both be right.

That is the guard doing exactly its job, at config time, before any GPU was
spent; without it the banner would have printed one horizon while the
schedule used another, which is the class of lie this whole thread has been
about. Both sites now compute the same min(_i("LR_EPOCHS", 8) or EPOCHS,
EPOCHS) the scheduler does, so the banner reports what will actually run.

The "LR_EPOCHS=0 was set explicitly" branch now keys off the raw environment
rather than the resolved value, which is 2 at EPOCHS=2 and would never equal 0.

Verified at EPOCHS=2: default and explicit LR_EPOCHS=0 both reach the banner
with an effective LR_EPOCHS=2 -- the clamp keeping a short run annealing over
its own length rather than over 8.
