93c17339700e7a63c408ddfac8ee32bd89e81a93
2026-08-11
the mint gate: p(b|a), not an entropy threshold, and it filters rather than aborts
The entropy version I shipped an hour ago was wrong in two ways, both caught
by running it end to end rather than on the constructed case that motivated it.

WRONG STATISTIC. An absolute H(next|a) cut-off does not survive real text.
Measured over 400 kB of English at the byte level: H has median 3.48 bits,
p90 4.39, max 5.33 -- so TOK_MINT_HMAX=1.5 rejected 81% of left tokens. Worse,
H is ANTI-correlated with frequency: a common left token is common precisely
because many things follow it, so an entropy gate rejects the most useful
merges FIRST. The top pair in that corpus (b' ' + b' ', x31432) sits at
H=4.39. p(b|a) asks the same question -- does `a` reliably predict `b`? --
scale-free and without the frequency bias. H is still computed and REPORTED,
because it is the right diagnostic for choosing a threshold; it is not the
gate. TOK_MINT_HMAX is replaced by TOK_MINT_PMIN.

WRONG CONTROL FLOW. A rejected candidate returned None, which ends the grow
burst, and self_organize.py breaks its loop on None -- so ONE blocked pair
stopped minting for that event. Since the highest-frequency pair is the one
most likely to straddle a boundary, that stopped minting almost entirely: the
end-to-end run reached vocabulary 256 of 1024 with 100% of candidates blocked
and the run degenerated (75% of the softmax never minted). The gate now walks
down the candidate ranking until one passes, which is what makes it a gate
rather than an off switch. A rejected pair is not zeroed -- it stays at full
count and is reconsidered as the segmentation settles.

TOK_MINT_GATE_K (how far down the ranking it may look) defaults to 1024, kept
generous so TOK_MINT_PMIN is the only lever that decides. At 64 the WINDOW was
deciding rather than the threshold: at pmin=0.10 the vocabulary reached 419 of
1024 at gate_k=64 against 1010 at gate_k=1024.

Measured on 400 kB, 1024 cap, 4 passes:  pmin 0.10 -> 1010,  0.15 -> 623,
0.25 -> 353. Arms pgate / pgate_t / pgate_c. Still default off.

The report counts CANDIDATES EXAMINED, not vocabulary denied -- the gate walks
the ranking, so one mint can reject many -- and says so, plus the median
p(b|a) of everything it judged, so a threshold can be picked from data.

c214c212a7f736b74595360227f3fafb457de7a1
2026-08-11
a meaning gate on minting, appearances by default, and a fixed LR wavelength
THE MERGE-QUALITY GATE NEVER EXISTED. Not in the code and not in history --
`git log -S` over tokenizer.py finds nothing. The only re-ranking is
TOK_MINT_NOVEL, which scores by RECENCY of growth, (c-seen)/(1+seen)^novel,
not by whether the merged unit means anything, and it defaults to 0.
retire_stale is the nearest relative and is unreachable.

TOK_MINT_HMAX adds one. Frequency cannot tell a UNIT ("th"+"e") from a pair
straddling a boundary everything crosses ("e"+" "); right-branching entropy
    H(next | a) = -SUM_b p(b|a) log2 p(b|a)
can, and `pair` already IS that joint distribution -- it only had to be read
by left token instead of as a flat ranking. A merge is allowed when H(next|a)
<= TOK_MINT_HMAX AND b takes at least half of a's successor mass, so the pair
cannot ride a low entropy that some other successor is carrying. Candidates
on either side may themselves be minted tokens, so the gate composes and is
recomputed over the CURRENT segmentation. A blocked pair is NOT zeroed: it
may become predictable as the segmentation settles.

Measured on a constructed case where frequency and meaning disagree:
    H(next|'t') = 1.315 bits  ('h' 157 of 208)          -> PASS
    H(next|'e') = 2.052 bits  (' ' 82, 'a' 43, 'o' 40)  -> BLOCK
Entropy is cached per change in the tally size, so a grow burst pays one pass
over `pair`, not one per candidate. Default 0 = off: it changes which tokens
exist, so it wants measuring against base rather than switching on silently.
Arms hgate / hgate_t / hgate_c.

TOK_ANCHOR_USES now defaults to 400 rather than 0, so release is by
APPEARANCES by default. Steps release a token on a clock unrelated to whether
it was ever trained on, and the two are anti-correlated: a token minted late
is rare BY CONSTRUCTION, which is why it was minted late.

LR_EPOCHS defaults to 8 -- a fixed cosine WAVELENGTH -- instead of following
EPOCHS. Defaulting to EPOCHS made the cosine stretch with the run, so EPOCHS
silently set the lr at every step (11x apart between an 8- and an 18-epoch run
at step 44000, which is what dragged E18). Fixed wavelength makes any two runs
of at least 8 epochs share the same lr at the same step; longer runs spend
longer at the floor rather than turning back up. Clamped to min(8, EPOCHS):
without that a 3-epoch pilot would anneal over 8 and end near 76% of peak,
reintroducing the original never-anneals bug as a default. LR_EPOCHS=0
restores the old EPOCHS-following behaviour for reproducing earlier results.

d05d91964b3b405d4e136fc977cf425d9a5fcee0
2026-08-11
two bugs an adversarial audit of the anchor path turned up
TELLING THE COMPOSER THE VOCABULARY GREW WAS NESTED INSIDE WARMSTART.
`if _i("WARMSTART", 1):` sits at indent 20; the `if TOK_COMPOSE:` that calls
set_vocab and note_born sat at indent 24, inside it. With WARMSTART=0 and
TOK_COMPOSE=1 the mint still happened -- maybe_grow appends to id2bytes and
seq2id, so the id becomes emittable and the next retok feeds it into training
-- but the composer was never told. The new token got an all-zero byte mask,
so its composite was proj(length(0)), IDENTICAL for every token minted that
way: the indistinguishable fresh row ByteComposer exists to abolish,
reintroduced by an ablation flag about something else. note_born went with
it, so born stayed -1e9 and the anchor held nothing either.

Hoisted out of the guard. Telling the composer a token exists is correctness,
not warm-starting.

_V == VMAX IS NOT AN INVARIANT. DynamicTokenizer.load restores the vmax saved
IN THE FILE, not env VMAX, so resuming against a tokenizer written by a
larger-VMAX run gives len(id2bytes) > delta.size(0). table() and anchor()
then index delta[:_V] on a shorter tensor -- a bare shape error thousands of
lines from its cause. Not clampable: the LM head is VMAX-wide too, so the
extra ids have nowhere to be predicted. set_vocab now fails with a message
that names the mismatch. Per-arm TOKENIZER_PATH made these files persistent,
which is what raised this from theoretical to reachable.

Verified by lifting ByteComposer out of the source: the guard raises
SystemExit rather than a shape error, and born/seen both survive the
reallocation that every mint triggers.

Found by an adversarial audit that also CONFIRMED, empirically, that
TOK_ANCHOR is inert without TOK_COMPOSE: three end-to-end runs at identical
seed differing only in TOK_ANCHOR (0.05/tau=4000, 25.0/tau=1.0, 0) produced
byte-identical reports apart from the banner line echoing the knob.

3464ba7ce22ef5de746f39767fc8a2c4d5356ac1
2026-08-11
TOK_ANCHOR_USES: release a new token on APPEARANCES, not on the clock
The anchor held a freshly minted token near its composite with a weight
decaying over TOK_ANCHOR_TAU STEPS of its own life, and its docstring claimed
this made the token "free once it has seen enough of its own material" --
which steps do not measure. A token minted early appears constantly and is
thoroughly trained inside tau; one minted late is rare BY CONSTRUCTION, which
is why it was minted late, and may appear a handful of times in the same tau.
Both were released on the same clock, so release was anti-correlated with
readiness. Unit-tested on two tokens of identical age, one seen 500x and one
seen 2x: weights 0.905/0.905 under steps, 0.007/0.980 under appearances.

TOK_ANCHOR_USES=N weights by exp(-seen/N), where `seen` counts appearances in
the batches actually trained on, incremented once per optimizer step from the
input batch. 0 keeps the old step rule, so nothing moves unless it is set.

This also separates the anchor from re-segmentation BY CONSTRUCTION. Under
the step rule TOK_ANCHOR_TAU=4000 and RETOK_EVERY=3000 overlap: a token is
re-segmented underneath itself while still anchored. `seen` only advances
when the token is in a training batch, which a retok cannot do, so the two
schedules cannot interfere and there is no ordering to get right.

set_vocab reallocates on EVERY mint, so `seen` is carried forward exactly
like `born`; without that copy each mint would zero every count and no token
would ever finish its anchor. Never-minted rows are masked out of the term --
under the step rule born=-1e9 already gave them ~0 weight, but seen=0 would
have held them at FULL weight, and "minted but not yet seen" and "does not
exist" have to be told apart.

Two banner lies fixed with it: TOK_ANCHOR/TOK_ANCHOR_TAU are printed on the
EFFECTIVE line but do nothing unless TOK_COMPOSE=1 (they have done nothing in
every run of this investigation, since it defaults to 0), and the
[tokenizer] TOK_COMPOSE line stated "~TAU steps" regardless of which release
rule was running. Both now say what is actually the case.

e7ae8850856937b74da4dc932b7b024b9177d2f0
2026-08-11
vocab.py --tree: read token quality out of the merge tree, without a corpus
The cohort table needs text to say what a cohort covers, and a tokenizer file
travels on its own. The merge tree carries a different signal and, for the
question actually being asked -- are the tokens minted LATE any good -- a
sharper one: where a token sits relative to word boundaries, how deep a merge
it is, and whether the vocabulary is spending slots walking a single word out
one byte at a time.

On the vmax8k vocabulary (7936 minted, 8 cohorts by mint order):

  cohort   meanB  depth prefix-of  ·word  word· ·word·  multi interior
  1-992     3.29   1.69       72%    16%    18%     2%    12%      52%
  ...
  6945-7936  4.71   2.37       10%    15%    17%     2%    16%      51%

Boundary structure is FLAT across the whole run -- the last cohort carries
the same share of leading/trailing word boundaries as the first -- and the
multi-word share RISES, 12% to 16%. prefix-of-another, the waste measure,
is concentrated EARLY (72% -> 10%): the scaffolding is at the start, where
BPE puts it and where it gets used constantly, not at the end.

So the late tokens are structurally indistinguishable from the early ones
apart from being longer and rarer. Two hypotheses about late-token quality
have now failed against this file: "minted from pairs that had stopped
recurring" (unused is 0% in every cohort) and "shape degrades once the good
pairs are exhausted".

max_tok is not binding either: the longest token is 14B against a cap of 16.

---
ec9813effb04a7456583f81eb60da7504d938807
2026-08-11
vocab.py: read the mint log the runs were already writing
TOKENIZER_PATH holds {"merges": [(a, b), ...]} and ids are handed out in
order, so id 256+k IS the (k+1)th token ever minted. Every run has been
writing a complete mint LOG and nothing could read it, so "are the tokens
minted late any good?" had no answer short of guessing from generated text.

    python3 vocab.py <path> --list 40               newest 40, in mint order
    python3 vocab.py <path> --list 40 --from 4823   a window of mint order
    python3 vocab.py <path> --corpus data_pilot     what each cohort covers

Grouped by mint order, because that is the axis that differs: a token minted
early had the whole run to be used and trained, one minted late arrived near
the end and -- on an annealed schedule -- with almost no learning rate left.
The cohort table reports mean length and shape (word-initial vs word-interior
fragment), and with --corpus the share of the stream each cohort produces,
the share of BYTES it covers, and how many of its tokens never appear at all.
Shape alone is suggestive; %bytes is what says whether a cohort pays.

EVERY ARM WAS OVERWRITING THE SAME FILE. longrun.sh never set
TOKENIZER_PATH, so grid/seeds/repeat all wrote data/dyntok.json and each run
destroyed the previous arm's vocabulary -- the same class of overlap as the
levers, with the arm's identity not reaching its artifact. Each subcommand
now writes beside its own log. Safe: under TOK_ONLINE without RESUME the
loader never reads the file back.

The live [tokenizer @ step] line now samples the newest ids as they are
minted, so drift is visible during a run rather than only afterwards.

Verified by lifting the print out of the source and running it against a real
vocabulary: leading spaces marked, non-UTF8 replaced, "newest:" suppressed on
a +0 retok. vocab.py checked against a tokenizer file a run actually wrote.

  model finished.

  the line above had just said to ignore. When a unit-stable bits/byte curve

f279fd0252529d22f6875d07b9fa303c9aa8deb3
2026-08-11
levers: declare every knob that another knob decides, and refuse the one override
Derived from the AST rather than from the comments, and the registry had a
hole: 4 of 279 knobs were not in _SPEC at all, and all four were derived --
the class where drift is most likely and least visible.

DERIVED (unset -> follows another knob; an explicit setting still wins):
    MAX_DOMAINS     <- FAB_NMAX          was not in the registry
    ENC_EVERY_IDLE  <- ENC_EVERY         was not in the registry
    D_MODEL         <- D_MODEL_B         was not in the registry
    PHASE_W         <- PHASES            was not in the registry
    SIG_LOOK        <- ENC_EVERY_IDLE <- ENC_EVERY   (two hops)
    FAB_MIN_STEPS   <- SOCIETY
    LAYERS          <- MODEL
    ENC_POS_MAX     <- WIN
    SEG_CONTIG      <- DOMAINS

FAB_NMAX silently setting the DOMAIN cap is a cross-subsystem tie nothing
stated: the expert population's size knob also sizes domain assembly.

OVERRIDE, and the one that is a bug rather than a design: CHAIN_VOTE=1 --
the default -- forces FAB_MIN_STEPS to 0 inside Fabric.__init__. An explicit
FAB_MIN_STEPS=2 was accepted, printed in the banner, written to the
checkpoint, and discarded. It is now refused on the same contract as a
registry default mismatch. Nothing that has run sets both, so no
configuration anyone has used is refused.

_SPEC_FREE stops being a hand-maintained list and derives from _DERIVED, so
"exempt from the literal-default check" and "declared to follow another
knob" cannot disagree.

levers.py re-derives all of it from the AST and fails if the declarations and
the source drift apart in either direction -- an underived declaration or an
undeclared derived default. Registry coverage is 279/279.

Every log now carries a [config] DERIVED line separating knobs that are
following another from knobs that were set explicitly.

Verified: the refusal fires under CHAIN_VOTE=1; with CHAIN_VOTE=0 the run
proceeds and FAB_MIN_STEPS=2 takes effect; the DERIVED banner reports both
groups correctly; levers.py exits 0.

9fabba41d99d5d4ee33006dacfe44bdd402bd0cc
2026-08-11
LR_EPOCHS: separate the schedule horizon from the run length
EPOCHS set two things: how many steps the run takes, and the horizon the
cosine is shaped over. So it changed the LR at EVERY step, and two runs
differing only in EPOCHS were two different schedules rather than one run
measured at two lengths.

Measured on the vmax4k pair -- identical config, identical vocabulary
trajectory, both reaching 4096 near step 40k:

    step      E8 lr      E18 lr     ratio
    20000  1.263e-03  1.807e-03    1.4x
    40000  1.683e-04  1.275e-03    7.6x
    44000  1.046e-04  1.148e-03   11.0x

Step 44000 is where the 8-epoch run posted its best held-out (2.059) at 5%
of peak, while the 18-epoch run was at 56% and moving away from its own
minimum. "8 epochs beat 18" and "a low LR beat a high one" were the same
observation. The 18-epoch run filled its vocabulary completely (4096/4096,
0% never minted), so dead rows do not explain it.

LR_EPOCHS is the horizon in epochs, defaulting to EPOCHS so nothing moves
unless it is set. EPOCHS=18 LR_EPOCHS=8 reproduces the 8-epoch LR at every
step and then holds at the LR_MIN_FRAC floor -- verified by lifting _lr_at
and _project out of the source and comparing: 19 overlapping sample points,
zero mismatches, tail pinned at 5%.

_proj_steps served both the ETA and the LR horizon from one monotone clamp,
so they could not take different horizons without one silently inheriting
the other's. Split into _proj_steps (work remaining) and _lr_total (schedule
horizon) over a shared _project, with separate clamps.

The LR appeared in no log line anywhere, which is how a lever moving the
rate 11x stayed invisible across every comparison made so far. It is now on
the epoch line as a fraction of peak, and the [config] COUPLING banner
states the EPOCHS/schedule tie on every run.

vmax4k filled its width and is the best pilot on record: it beats base@8ep
(2.239, 75% real words) on both metrics at the same epoch budget. A larger
vocabulary helps when it is actually filled.

The previous commit reached for GROW_BURST=24 to buy the mints. That is a
second knob compensating for a first: GROW_BURST changes the SHAPE of minting
-- how large a segmentation shift each grow event is -- while EPOCHS changes
only how much of the identical process runs. Reverted; the arms carry no
growth knobs. ~14 epochs fills 8192 at the measured ~540 mints/epoch.

    mints/epoch and epochs-to-fill. Measured at the seed vocabulary, so it ran
    ~25% optimistic on this data: it reports a floor on the shortfall.

    denominator all run) from minted-then-unused (trained while in use, lost

    of the second and is fine.
  - the CHAINING report hardcoded "depth is 0.00 of 4" as a fact, and the

frozen1k / frozen2k: separate "fixed vocabulary" from "tiny vocabulary"
The `frozen` arm is TOK_MINT_UNTIL=1, which freezes at SEED_VOCAB=512. That conflates two different ideas.
At 512 the model has almost no whole-word units and has to spell everything -- measured 3.07 tokens per
generated word against base's 2.52, and 24% of generated words appearing in the training text against base's
89%. Its base model alone reads roughly twice base's bits/byte before the fabric or memory contribute
anything. So the arm answers "is a 512-token vocabulary enough", not "does freezing help".

frozen2k freezes at 2048 -- the size base ENDS at -- so the comparison is fixed-versus-growing with the
vocabulary size held constant. frozen1k sits between them.

SEED_VOCAB is not in grid's hardcoded env list, so an arm flag reaches it.

c6f54e6a41f7de07b0ede0571a0b0c22bfea13dd
2026-08-10
equiv.sh: a noise baseline, because the GPU is nondeterministic in exactly one subsystem
A CUDA self-test came back DIFFERS on 14 lines. Where it differs is the useful part:

    model ALONE (weights only) 3.889  ->  3.889      IDENTICAL
    model ALONE 3.889 -> + FABRIC 3.325              IDENTICAL
    model + MEMORY 3.427   vs   3.431                differs
    flagged 5100  vs  5113 implausible               differs

Training is bit-reproducible -- every model-only and model+fabric figure matches exactly. The memory store's
RETRIEVAL is not, and the three numbers derived from it move with it. So the earlier byte-identical repeat
runs were not luck, and the nondeterminism is confined to one subsystem rather than the optimiser.

A tool that just says DIFFERS because a known-noisy subsystem moved is useless. Now: the self-test WRITES the
set of line shapes this machine varies on (numbers masked, so 3.427-vs-3.431 and 5100-vs-5113 collapse to one
pattern each) to runs/equiv_noise_<device>.txt, and a later comparison SUBTRACTS them and judges on the
remainder. Differing only in known-noisy lines now reports INERT and exits 0, printing the diff anyway so the
claim is checkable.

Run 'equiv.sh HEAD HEAD' once per machine to establish the baseline before trusting a comparison.

Also adds vmax4k / vmax8k arms. VMAX is the model's vocab dimension and the tokenizer's ceiling and nothing
has run above 2048; these are reachable as arm flags only since the precedence fix, which is why the earlier
'grid base VMAX=8192' would have run at 2048 and named the log 8192.

9c59a844e91db5a2c0f9d59cc37b8e5a67c2a81c
2026-08-10
revert the main() split: the seam was 136 values wide, not 39
I said at the start that splitting main() was a rewrite rather than a refactor, then did it as a refactor.
Four bugs followed, each caught by execution rather than review:

  1. the SimpleNamespace import replaced the first "import os" and split a multi-import line, so the file
     could not be imported at all
  2. the context missed the six FUNCTIONS main() defines (_retok, _save_ckpt, _config_audit, encpos, encwin,
     report_holdout) -- my analysis walked assignment targets and never looked for defs
  3. it also subtracted every name _report writes, which is exactly the case that breaks: a statement that
     reads and writes the same name makes it local, so the read is unbound
  4. correcting for that took the context from 39 values to 136, including a, b, c, i, k, p, s, t, v, x

That last number is the finding. The report does not read 39 finished objects from the training half; it
reads 136, and eighty of them are leftover loop temporaries. There is no clean seam to cut along. main() at
1,645 lines plus a 136-value context is not clearer than main() at 2,930 -- it is the same tangle with a
function call in the middle.

Splitting it properly means first renaming the per-block temporaries so nothing crosses a boundary, and only
then cutting. That is a real piece of work with its own verification, not something to land while pilots are
waiting on the GPU.

KEPT, all verified inert: the 274-knob registry, the five canonicalised defaults, _units/_eval_logits, the
comment corrections, the COUPLING banner, and equiv.sh itself.

7ff2af0fd31beef040afee06df51dd15801b549e
2026-08-10
equiv.sh: same commit twice is a determinism self-test, not an error
The comparison assumes a run is a function of (config, commit, seed). On CPU that held. On GPU it is not
guaranteed -- cuDNN's GRU backward and atomic scatters are not bit-reproducible in general -- and if the
machine is nondeterministic then equiv reports DIFFERS for two commits that are actually identical, which is
worse than no test at all because it looks like a finding.

So the same-commit case, which used to exit 1, now runs the commit twice and answers 'is this machine
deterministic'. Run it first on any new device; believe no other verdict until it comes back IDENTICAL.

The verdict wording branches on which question was asked, and the two sides get _run1/_run2 log names so a
self-test does not collide with a comparison in the same directory.

7de4dafdc65a6d145a1afff6fc1a5707e8a74e0a
2026-08-10
split main(): the 1,297-line measurement battery moves to _report(R)
main() was 2,940 lines holding 658 locals in one scope. The seam is not arbitrary -- the end-of-run report
READS 39 finished objects from the training half and writes nothing the training half reads back, so it is
the only place main() cleanly divides.

R carries those 39 values and they are unpacked into locals at the top of _report, so the 1,297-line body is
BYTE-FOR-BYTE what used to sit in main(): no renaming, no threading, nothing to get subtly wrong at that
scale. The one bare 'return' in the region is the BENCH early-exit, and since the report is the last thing
main() does, returning from _report instead is equivalent. Ten other returns are inside nested defs.

main() 2,940 -> 1,645 lines. Verified by equiv.sh, not by this message.

2d93a3ea7c506582e26d794f81f9e7117f8e14a9
2026-08-10
equiv.sh: prove a code change is inert, instead of improvising a command line each time
`rerun.sh smoke` asserts every arm REACHES THE REPORT. That catches crashes, not changes. To claim a refactor
is inert you have to show the NUMBERS are the same, and the three one-off invocations I improvised to do that
were each broken in the same way -- they depended on a shell variable still being set, or they wrote their
output INSIDE a git worktree that the next line then deleted. One of them would have destroyed the result it
was asked to produce.

So: a script, tested before use.
  - every path derives from ${BASH_SOURCE[0]}, never from $(pwd) or a caller-set variable
  - the output directory is created AND write-checked before anything runs
  - logs live in runs/equiv_<A>_vs_<B>/, never inside a worktree, so cleanup cannot destroy them
  - a side that fails prints its last lines rather than vanishing
  - config is passed directly, not via _flags_for, which silently resolves to empty for an arm name that does
    not exist on the older commit -- the trap in the last command I sent
  - both sides normalised the same way, verdict printed, exit 0 identical / 2 differs

What it reaches that the 12 kB smoke does not: the vocabulary driven to its cap (GROW_EVERY=20 GROW_BURST=8),
the fabric driven to its cap (FAB_NMAX=64), retokenisation firing (RETOK_EVERY=300), and EPOCHS=3 forcing two
epoch resamples. Those are the paths a real run spends its time in and where a refactor could hide.

SCALE=fast (~2-4 min/side) can falsify inertness cheaply; SCALE=deep runs the same paths at pilot width. A
fast pass is strong evidence, not proof, and the script says so in its own output.

Guards verified before first use: missing arg, bad ref, same commit twice, unwritable output, and a corpus
under 50 kB -- which caught this checkout's data_pilot being a 2-byte stub.

707f1af431049c16f35dd85d63b66387478210cf
2026-08-10
the pilot bundle: one resumable block, with the two confounded knobs separated
Last round ran TOK_MINT_UNTIL=1 and RETOK_EVERY=0 together and came back 1.4 bits/byte worse than the
previous frozen run, with no way to tell which did it. They are not the same idea. TOK_MINT_UNTIL stops
MINTING. RETOK_EVERY stops RE-SEGMENTING -- and a re-segmentation that produces a byte-identical stream is
still not a no-op, because it clears the lookahead queue and blacks out fabric growth for FAB_COOLDOWN steps.
So they get one arm each.

  base       the control everything else is read against
  frozen     TOK_MINT_UNTIL=1                    vocabulary frozen, retok still fires
  frozen_nr  TOK_MINT_UNTIL=1 RETOK_EVERY=0      ...and re-segmentation off too
  drop       DROPOUT=0.1
  wdecay     WEIGHT_DECAY=0.01
  reg        DROPOUT=0.1 WEIGHT_DECAY=0.01

Ordered by information value, so stopping early leaves the most useful subset. Every run so far reports
UNDERFIT with a NEGATIVE gap, so the regularisation arms are expected to cost rather than help; they are
worth measuring anyway because DROPOUT perturbs the hidden state the router reads, which makes it an
expert-dynamics lever and not only a generalisation one.

Run as `GRID_CKPT=0 bash longrun.sh grid pilots`. GRID_CKPT=0 sets SAVE_CKPT=0, which matches the mode every
recent seeds/repeat run used -- checkpointing gates extra holdout_bpb passes and mixing the two modes is what
made the 3.694-vs-2.100 pair uncomparable.

343bfd73f9471633eb45569b04cef0343ff3717a
2026-08-07
two repeated expressions become named helpers
_units(TOK, USE_TOK, text) replaces ten copies of `TOK.segment(x, count=False) if USE_TOK else list(x)`.
count=False is the part worth naming: counting tallies the pair statistics that drive MINTING, so an
evaluation pass that dropped it would silently steer the vocabulary it was supposed to be measuring against.

_eval_logits(model, fab, FABRIC, x) replaces six copies of
`fab_logits(model, fab if FABRIC else None, model.encode(x))` -- the line that must never drift between the
six evaluation sites, because getting `fab if FABRIC else None` wrong scores the base model while claiming to
score the system.

CAUGHT IN THIS EDIT, worth recording: the regex that rewrote the call sites also rewrote the body of _units
itself, leaving `return _units(TOK, USE_TOK, text)` -- infinite recursion on first call. It compiled without
complaint. What caught it was an AST assertion that neither helper appears in its own body, not the compiler
and not reading the diff.

Two things deliberately NOT done, reversing what I proposed earlier:
  ExpertBank is NOT deleted. `expert_bank:EXPERTS=1 FABRIC=0` is one of the eleven arms in rerun.sh smoke, so
  it is an exercised ablation control, not dead code. Removing it would delete a test.
  The 51 redundant knob reads are NOT collapsed. The registry already made duplicate reads harmless -- _env
  now refuses a read whose default disagrees with the table -- so rewriting the call sites removes a hazard
  that no longer exists.

Smoke in progress at commit time: five arms complete, all clean, all reaching the report
(full 6.153, no_fabric 6.683, no_perexp 6.153, no_tok 5.329, no_world 6.431 held-out).

6f4c5341a665af06b362508d52336165aef8ace3
2026-08-07
one declared place for all 274 knobs, and five defaults that disagreed with themselves
Before this, every knob was read inline at its point of use across 5,500 lines. 211 of the 274 appeared in no
declaration at all, so there was nowhere to look to see the configuration surface -- and nothing stopped the
same knob from being read with a different default in two places. Five were:

  VMAX       the tokenizer targeted 4096 while ByteComposer sized delta/dbias to 2048, so an unset VMAX
             indexed past the end of both per-token tables. No run has hit it because the harness always sets
             VMAX=2048 and TOK_COMPOSE defaults off, but it is a crash waiting for the first direct invocation.
  DOMAINS    the CHECKPOINT recorded _env("DOMAINS", "") -- an empty domain list on any run that did not set
             it, and report_holdout keys its retention probe on exactly that field.
  RESUME     None in one place, "" in another. Both falsy; cosmetic.
  SAVE_CKPT  same, in four places.
  LAYERS     genuinely context-dependent (4 transformer / 1 gru) and is EXEMPT rather than forced.

_SPEC lists all 274 with type, default and subsystem group, in eleven groups. _env checks every read against
it and stops the run on a disagreement, so this class of bug cannot come back. Five knobs whose default is
computed from another (LAYERS, FAB_MIN_STEPS, SEG_CONTIG, SIG_LOOK, ENC_POS_MAX) are listed with None and
exempted, so the registry stays a complete inventory.

Verified: 274 registry entries, 274 distinct knobs read, zero dead entries, zero mismatches; the module
imports past every module-level read without tripping the check.

Behaviour: unchanged wherever the knob is set, which for VMAX/DOMAINS/SAVE_CKPT is every harness path. The
VMAX and DOMAINS canonicalisations change the unset case -- that is the point of them.

4e91275ffcb3769f46bae1e48b5e29e19ba474ed
2026-08-07
composite levers: say what you mean, without changing what anything does
Several ideas in this system need three or four primitives set consistently, and getting one wrong fails
silently. All three of these are measured, not hypothetical:

  TOK_MINT_UNTIL=1 freezes MINTING but leaves RETOK_EVERY firing -- 39 no-op re-tokenisations in one run,
    each dropping the lookahead queue and blacking out fabric growth for FAB_COOLDOWN steps.
  SOCIETY / CHAIN_ROUTE / CHAIN_VOTE pick ONE of three forward paths, and CHAIN_VOTE silently forces
    FAB_MIN_STEPS=0, so "which path am I on" is four flags and a hidden override.
  ROUTE_REGION_W and FAB_KEY_NORM together decide whether routing is weight-prediction only, two terms on
    wildly different scales (13%/87% measured), or two terms on comparable ones.

Four levers -- TOKENIZER_MODE, PATH_MODE, ROUTE_SCORE, POP_MODE -- resolve to those primitives. No primitive
changed, no default changed, and every lever is UNSET by default: unset writes nothing, so the primitives read
their own defaults and behaviour is byte-identical. An explicit setting always beats a lever, and every write
is printed, so a lever cannot quietly do something that was not asked for.

CONFIG_DUMP=1 prints the resolved configuration and stops before training, so two commits can be shown to be
configured identically by diffing text -- no GPU, no run. That is what a "nothing changed" claim needs and
what eyeballing a banner does not give.

Verified in isolation: no lever set leaves all ten primitives untouched; each lever resolves as documented;
TOKENIZER_MODE=frozen with an explicit RETOK_EVERY=3000 keeps 3000; an unrecognised mode is reported rather
than ignored; TOKENIZER_MODE=mint:N warns that retok keeps firing after the freeze.

c14f87643cca35e16103c98aaeb0dc442096dd97
2026-08-07
GEN_N: every text judgement in this project rested on one 200-token sample
GEN_PROCS caps how many DOMAINS get a continuation. This project runs one corpus, so it has always been
one domain and therefore exactly one sample -- and the composing check scored "% of generated words that
appear in the training text" on 64-91 words. 91% (83/91) against 71% and 31% were being read as a real
signal off a few dozen words.

GEN_N (default 4) draws several DISTINCT seed passages per process, via random.sample rather than repeated
random.choice, so a passage cannot be drawn twice and the samples are not silently correlated. Everything
downstream that reads _gen_keep -- the composing check and the coherence probe -- iterates it, so both get
~4x the sample for free.

Cost is GEN_N x 2 x GEN_LEN single-token forwards (1600 at the defaults), once, after training.

seeds and repeat never pulled the pilot corpus
`pilot` and `grid` each carried their own copy of the fetch-if-empty guard. `seeds` and `repeat` were added

a config error, after the harness had already said it was starting.

One _pilot_corpus() next to _reserve/_done, called by all four. It also does two things the copies did not:
prints what it is pulling before it starts, and re-checks afterwards, because fetch_big.py exiting 0 while
writing nothing is the same wasted setup one step later.

When `datasets` is missing it now prints the exact fetch_big.py command for the directory in question rather
than a bare install hint.

5f4f117653db34fad941cc8f31db208f4e666bd6
2026-08-07
three fixes: an eval pass no longer trains the router, and the LR horizon is live
1. GROUND_UPDATE ON A FABRICATED ZERO GIST.
   fab_logits is the eval path -- learning curve, holdout probe, bpb_true, generation, prompt.py -- and it
   invents `gist = torch.zeros(...)` so the routing arithmetic has the right shape. Fabric.forward and
   route_w then read that placeholder as a real signature and call ground_update, which does
   `F.normalize(gist).mean(0)` -- zero -- and drags the top-FAB_CENT_TOPK experts' region centroids toward
   the ORIGIN. Five times per forward on the soc path (entry + 4 hops).

   Measured, on two runs whose model code was byte-identical and whose seed was the same, differing only in
   whether SAVE_CKPT was set (which gates the extra holdout_bpb calls): held-out 3.694 vs 2.100. A
   diagnostic's sampling frequency was changing the final model by 1.594 bits/byte.

   route_w/society/forward take learn_regions=True; every non-training caller passes False -- fab_logits,
   the timing probe, and the four post-training report sections that were skewing the reports they precede.
   Training calls fab.society()/fab() directly with a real signature and is unchanged.

2. THE LR SCHEDULE READ A HORIZON THE RUN NEVER REACHES.
   _total_steps = EPOCHS x (tokens // WIN), measured ONCE at the seed vocabulary. Minted tokens are longer,
   so every later epoch is shorter. Four runs at one seed, everything else identical:

     E8  minting   projected  63,024   ran  48,130   31% over   cosine reached p=0.760, ended 18% of peak
     E12 minting   projected  94,536   ran  70,368   34% over   p=0.742
     E18 minting   projected 141,804   ran 103,805   37% over   p=0.730, ended 21% of peak
     FROZEN vocab  projected 118,776   ran 118,743    0% over   p=1.000, ended  5% of peak -- as designed

   The frozen-vocabulary run is the only one that has ever annealed, and only because a vocabulary that does
   not grow makes the projection exact. "Frozen tokenizer" and "schedule that anneals" were therefore the
   same experiment and neither could be credited -- the tokenizer conclusion I drew from those four runs was
   not supported. It also means EPOCHS was never just run length: at step 48,130 the E18 schedule was at
   1.52e-3 and the E8 schedule at 3.58e-4.

   _lr_at now reads _proj_steps(step), clamped to its running minimum so the rate falls and never steps back
   up mid-run.

3. ARM FLAGS WERE SILENTLY DISCARDED.
   `env A=1 A=2` keeps the last assignment, and $FLAGS/$ARMFLAGS came FIRST, so every knob hardcoded after
   them -- VMAX, WIN, BATCH_W, RATE_EVERY, CKPT_EVERY, GROW_*, SEG_*, DATA_DIR, LAYERS, MODEL -- overrode the
   arm. `grid 3 VMAX=512` ran at 2048 and named the log 512. Flags now come last in grid/seeds/repeat, with
   the loop's own SEED after them. Verified end to end: VMAX=512 RATE_EVERY=250 now reach the process.

f9d676c3778bbbec164acfb64c5b37454e855ba8
2026-08-06
yes, in every past run -- and minting picks the most damaging pair by construction
Second-half slope of the held-out bits/byte curve, every log in the project:

  FROZEN  TOK_MINT_UNTIL=6000   2.45 -> 2.19   -0.1020/10k   improving
  none    TOKENIZER=0           5.46 -> 4.34   -0.0354/10k   improving (from a
                                                             level that loses to order-1)
  all-run minting, 21 logs:     19 FLAT or WORSE, +0.0175 to +0.2170/10k
                                (both GRU and transformer, fabric and FABRIC=0,
                                 society, chaining and soc-loop)
  the 2 that read "improving" are the socloop-with-raw-dot runs whose curves
  oscillated to 7.76 -- the slope is fitting a recovery from spikes, not learning

So the flat second half is not new and not specific to anything in this branch.
It has been in every pilot since the first, on every architecture.

MINTING IS NOT THE PROBLEM; WHICH PAIR GETS MINTED IS. maybe_grow takes
most_common(1) -- the GLOBALLY most frequent pair -- which by construction
appears everywhere, so re-segmenting it rewrites the representation of ALL
existing material at once. That is the most disruptive mint available, chosen
every time. For a system whose point is continual learning it is backwards: a
new area arriving should buy vocabulary for ITSELF, not change how everything
already learned is spelled.

TOK_MINT_NOVEL re-ranks the top candidates by how much a pair has grown SINCE
LAST CONSIDERED, relative to how much was already seen: recent/(1+seen)^novel.
A pair common all along scores low however frequent; a pair that has just
started appearing scores high. Minting then follows NEW material and leaves the
spelling of fitted text alone. novel=0 reproduces the old behaviour exactly.

Verified the selection directly -- given (AB: 500 seen 460), (CD: 400 seen 380),
(PQ: 60 seen 0): novel=0.0 mints AB, novel=0.5 mints PQ.

Default 0, unvalidated end to end. The toy is 6.034 against 5.764, which is
meaningless here for the same reason as the rest of the tokenizer work: on 400
steps with one corpus there is no "new material" for novelty to find, so the
rule can only pick worse pairs than frequency does. It needs the run it was
designed for -- pilot, then `longrun.sh pilot-add` to bring a second area in.

2026-08-06
"is it still learning" is now answered in every report, not computed by hand
The curve reported "best", "final" and "since the minimum" -- all whole-run
descriptions. A run can show a good minimum early and then be completely flat
for its second half, and none of those numbers say so. The slope over the SECOND
HALF does, and it is the question that actually decides whether more steps at a
given setting are worth buying.

Replayed against the four real runs:

  frozen@6k  2.45 -> 2.19   -0.1427/10k   still improving
  chained    3.79 -> 3.76   +0.0175/10k   FLAT -- bought nothing
  society    3.47 -> 3.54   +0.0269/10k   getting WORSE
  bytes      5.46 -> 4.34   -0.3911/10k   still improving (from a level that
                                          loses to order-1)

So the architecture DOES learn -- but only where the vocabulary has stopped
moving. With minting running for the whole run, the second half of both the
society and the chained-society pilots bought nothing at all: not a sequence of
shocks with recovery in between, just flat. The disruptions are continuous
enough that there is no "between" to look at, and removing them is the only way
to see the underlying trend.

Verified live on a real run.

ed04aac0319bf5d2a867834832f070abbb57b151
2026-08-06
minted tokens DO get parameters -- they start at their composite and grow into themselves
I built the wrong thing and you corrected it. TOK_COMPOSE removed per-token
parameters entirely; the goal was the opposite -- keep them, but make the
TRANSITION from a mint to its composite easy. Rebuilt to that spec, and ON by
default as asked.

Each token is now:   vector = composite(its bytes) + a free residual, zero-init

So at the instant "ab" is minted its vector IS its composite -- and its bytes
are its parts -- and it becomes itself from there by moving the residual. The
mint is continuous rather than a jump onto a row somebody guessed at. Per-token
parameters exist and train normally; the composition is the starting point, not
a replacement.

TOK_ANCHOR (0.05, tau 4000) is the "trained with the loss, differently" part:
the residual is penalised in proportion to how RECENTLY that token was minted,
using per-token birth steps. A new token is held near its composite while it is
new and progressively released as it accumulates its own material, so the
handover is gradual instead of instant. Pre-existing tokens are unaffected --
their birth step is -inf so their weight is exactly 0.

Verified directly: delta starts at 0.0; anchor is 0.0 at birth, 0.25 once a
young token's residual moves, 0.0017 for the same residual at age 20k, and 0.0
for tokens that predate the composer. It fired 93 times in a real toy run.

Kept from the previous version because you said they were fine: no VMAX ceiling
on the composite, and tokens sharing bytes starting near each other.

The toy is uninformative here and I am not reading it as a result -- 5.764
against 5.100 for the old lookup table, on 400 steps where the composer has not
yet learned to build anything out of 256 byte embeddings and only ~126 tokens
have been minted at all. The anchor changes nothing yet either (identical to
TOK_ANCHOR=0) because a residual that has barely moved has nothing to penalise.
This needs a pilot.

---
e8df6fe11d2cf1d72036e6fc40daf7bb12574ca3
2026-08-06
TOK_COMPOSE: a token's vector computed from its bytes, so minting allocates nothing
Answering the id question. Literal integer-valued ids -- id = the token's bytes
read as an integer -- cannot index a table: max_tok=16 makes that a 128-bit
number, and no embedding has 2^128 rows. But the property that makes the idea
good does not need them. TOK.id2bytes already means the id determines the bytes,
so the token's representation can be COMPUTED from those bytes instead of stored
in a row somebody has to initialise.

ByteComposer: 256 byte embeddings + a within-token position embedding ("ab" is
not "ba") + a length term, pooled and projected, giving the (V, d) table. It
serves as the input embedding AND, tied, as the output head, with the per-token
output bias composed from the same vector. So:
  a minted token has NO parameters of its own -- nothing to initialise, no warm
    start, and the whole WARMSTART_MODE question stops existing
  no new parameters appear mid-run, so minting is no longer a moving target for
    the optimizer
  no VMAX ceiling -- the vocabulary can grow as far as the tokenizer wants
  a token sharing bytes with known tokens starts near them, automatically
The cost is that tokens can no longer be arbitrarily unrelated to one another,
which is the point rather than a limitation.

The table is sized to VMAX rather than the live vocabulary. Unused rows cost
nothing when there are no per-token parameters, and sizing to the live count
made any lag between a mint and the refresh an IndexError on the training
stream -- which is exactly what the first version did.

TOK_COMPOSE=0 by default. On a short toy it is behind the lookup table (5.765
vs 5.100), which is expected and uninformative: the composer starts from 256
byte embeddings and has to learn to build every token out of them, so it pays
its cost up front and earns it back over a run long enough for minting to
matter. That is precisely the regime a 400-step toy cannot see. Grid arm
`compose` added.

TOKENIZER=1 remains the default; the bytes run was an explicit override.

---
c92d10424882ce768cac268c76f1a25ff7d2b66a
2026-08-06
minted-token init is asymmetric, and averaging both sides loses most of the benefit
You said freezing the vocabulary will not help unless the way new tokens are
learned is fixed. Chasing that found a real defect in the initialisation, and a
result I am NOT defaulting on.

THE DEFECT. WARMSTART set both sides to the mean of the parents. They are not
symmetric:
  HEAD scores "the next token is ab" from the state at position t -- the same
       decision the model already made when it scored "next is a" there, since
       the contexts where ab now appears are exactly those where a appeared and
       b followed. head[b] is tuned for a DIFFERENT conditioning state, the one
       AFTER consuming a, so averaging it in mixes in the wrong row.
  EMB  is what the recurrence CONSUMES. After consuming ab the state should be
       where consuming a then b left it, and the last symbol dominates what is
       handed forward.
So: head[ab] = head[a], emb[ab] = emb[b].

Measured on the immediate post-mint loss -- what the model has to climb back
from at every mint, which is exactly what this mechanism exists to reduce.
6 pairs x 3 seeds = 18 trials:

    random              2.1699  sd 0.120
    mean/mean [old]     1.8222  sd 0.078
    mean/first          1.6252  sd 0.071
    last/first          1.4822  sd 0.011   -0.340 vs old, 31x its own sd
    sum/first           1.6518  sd 0.100

The old warm start beat random by 0.348; last/first beats the old warm start by
0.340. On that measurement it roughly doubles what warm-starting is worth.

WHY IT IS OFF BY DEFAULT ANYWAY. The only end-to-end check disagrees: a short
toy with minting on gave held-out 5.214 with last/first against 5.100 with mean.
One run, one seed, a gap well inside the 0.06-0.17 seed spread measured at pilot
scale -- so it does not refute 18 trials. But those 18 trials measure the
IMMEDIATE post-mint loss, and "cheaper to recover from" is not the same claim as
"better model at the end". Two measurements pointing different ways, neither
decisive, and defaulting on the one never checked end to end is the mistake this
branch has made repeatedly.

WARMSTART_MODE=last/first runs it; grid arm `mintinit` added. The pilot decides.

---
1e62effaa5e05b67d36d27fb473db003c49704c4
2026-08-05
minted-token handling: the weights were already inherited, the optimizer state was not
You suspected the problem is in how newly minted tokens are taught. Half of that
is already handled and half of my proposed fix was wrong; both worth recording.

ALREADY THERE: WARMSTART=1 initialises a minted token "ab" from its parents --
emb, head weight, head bias, and the signature encoder's embedding all set to
0.5*(a+b) rather than left at their random init. Toy check, minting on:
WARMSTART=0 gives held-out 5.667 against 5.100 with it on, so it is doing real
work.

NOT THERE, AND MY REASON FOR ADDING IT WAS WRONG: the Adam moments for that row.
The argument was that a never-gradiented row has v=0, so its first update is
lr*sign(g) -- Adam's maximum step -- landing on the weight the warm start just
placed. Checked it directly and it does not hold: Adam's step counter is
PER-TENSOR, not per-row, so by the time a token is minted the bias correction
already reflects thousands of steps and DAMPS a fresh row. Measured on a 5-step
toy, the new row's first update was 5.4e-4 with v=0 and 1.0e-3 with inherited
moments -- inheritance makes the first step LARGER, the opposite of the
motivation.

So it ships as WARMSTART_OPT, defaulting OFF, with the disproof next to it. On
the toy it happens to help (4.995 vs 5.100), which is one seed of one toy and
not a reason to default it on. Both flags are in the effective-config table.

MINIMUM TOKENIZER: TOKENIZER=0 is raw bytes, 256 ids, no minting, no
re-tokenisation ever -- the cleanest floor available, and the only setting where
bits/byte and bits/token are the same number so nothing can drift. Verified it
runs; grid arms bytes, freeze6k and freeze20k added.

Toy, same budget: bytes 4.720 | freeze 4.691 | mint 4.995-5.100. Suggestive of
the same direction as the pilot, on a run far too short to mean anything.

18fdd6c0e10bb69bab6c4ff0923017a90d6070e9
2026-08-05
"best at step 6k" is largely the yardstick moving, not the model stopping
You asked why the best lands at ~6000 when the tokenizer has not capped, the
embedder is still training and the system is still learning. It should not, and
the answer is that the curve measuring it is not comparable across time.

_VALT tokenises the held-out validation text ONCE and was never invalidated,
while the training stream is re-segmented at every mint. So after the first mint
the learning curve compares a model trained on the CURRENT segmentation against
validation text frozen in an OLD one, and the mismatch grows with every mint.

The shape follows from that exactly:
  - the curve degrades over the MINTING window (retok at 3008, 6016, 9024,
    12032, 15040, 18048, 21056)
  - and goes FLAT the moment minting stops (vocab caps at 21056; +0 tokens from
    24064 on; every sample after 22000 is 3.69-3.84)
A model that suddenly stops degrading at the exact step its vocabulary stops
changing is a drifting yardstick, not a model. And "best at ~6000", identical in
every arm at every seed, is the last sample where the cache still matched.

_VALT and _BL are now cleared with every re-tokenisation, so each sample is
measured against the segmentation the model is actually training on.

WHAT THIS DOES AND DOES NOT OVERTURN. The end-of-run held-out figures (2.058,
2.101, ...) re-tokenise before evaluating and are unaffected -- every
architecture comparison in this branch rests on those. What is now in question
is the per-process CURVE and everything I read off it: the "+1.220 bits/byte
since its own minimum" that I used to call the divergence REAL rather than a
units artifact, and the claim that model-alone quality degrades while memory
masks it. Those need re-measuring. The constant-LR arm oscillating 3.4 to 7.8
is far too large to be cache drift alone, so the LR result stands.

Verified by placement, not by execution: the clear sits inside the retok block
between the stream rebuild and the [tokenizer @ N] print, so it runs exactly
when and only when the segmentation changes. I could not reach that path at toy
scale -- minting needs more accumulated pairs than a short CPU run produces --
so it is unexercised until the next pilot, and the first thing to check there is
whether the curve still turns at 6000.

"experts serving none: 4053" was an instrumentation bug, and it disabled the breadth cap
WHY EXPERTS READ 0. The affiliation map recorded ONE expert per step:

    fab.note_dom(int(_w[0].argmax()), did)

row 0 of the batch, argmax only. At BATCH_W=16 that is fifteen of every sixteen
windows never recorded as serving anything, which is how a run that routed to
hundreds of experts reported "serving >1 domain: 16 | exactly 1: 14 | serving
none: 4053".

It was not only a reporting problem. dom_ban -- the percentage breadth cap --
reads exactly that table, so the cap could only ever ban the ~30 experts that
happened to land in the sample. It has been effectively inert since it was
added.

AND THE TWO PATHS WERE MEASURING UTILIZATION AT DIFFERENT RATES. note_dom also
bumped `use`, and society() records `use` NOWHERE ELSE -- while both chaining
paths record it per row inside forward(). So society's utilization table was one
sample per step and chaining's was BATCH_W per step, a factor of 16. Every
ROUTER SELECTION comparison in this branch (516 vs 202 vs 877 distinct experts,
top 3.7% vs 5.1% vs 25.2%) put those two against each other as if they meant the
same thing.

note_dom now records affiliation only, for EVERY row; note_use is separate and
is called on the society path to match what chaining already did.

FAB_GROW=0 freezes the population at FAB_N0 for the whole run -- no ramp, no
regression burst, no stall growth. Culling, routing, selection and replication
still run, so it isolates GROWTH from everything else the fabric does. The
2.4 -> 3.5 climb between steps 6k and 12k is the largest remaining loss in every
arm at every seed and it coincides with the ramp building the population; this
is the arm that tests whether those two facts are related.

Grid arms nogrow and nogrow_s added (FAB_N0=1024, a population that exists from
step 0 instead of being built during the run).

1593c70da834aa0da569fdd0e7603d6759d6459c
2026-08-05
there was no learning-rate schedule -- 2e-3 constant for 48,000 steps
You doubted the tokenizer explanation and you were right to. I could not test
it: the one arm with minting off (frozvocab, TOK_ONLINE=0) also builds the full
2048 vocabulary up front instead of seeding 512 and minting, so it is a
different run from the start -- and it went NaN from step 20000 anyway. The
hypothesis was untested and I presented it as a finding.

What the data does say, across all 17 pilots in this project:

  every one of them -- GRU and transformer, FABRIC=0 and every fabric variant,
  society, transition-chaining and soc-loop -- bottoms in held-out bits/byte at
  ~2.4 around step 6000 and rises to ~3.8-4.1 by 48000. Same shape, same place.

A cause common to all of them cannot be the fabric, the router, or the blend
rule. And it explains why nothing I changed in the routing ever moved it.

lr=2e-3, constant, no warmup, no decay, for the whole run. That is exactly this
shape on AdamW: fast early progress, then the optimizer bounces around a minimum
it cannot settle into and slowly degrades. It also covers the transformer arms,
which the tokenizer theory did not.

Added: linear warmup then cosine to LR_MIN_FRAC of peak. It anneals to 5% rather
than 0 -- this is a continual-learning system and a schedule that decays to
nothing cannot learn anything that arrives late. LR_SCHED=none reproduces the
old behaviour exactly, so this gets a clean A/B.

Warmup is clamped to a tenth of the run: at LR_WARMUP=1000 a short run never
leaves warmup and trains at a fraction of peak throughout, which reads as the
schedule hurting when it is the schedule never having run.

THIS IS A HYPOTHESIS. A 360-step toy cannot test a failure that takes 6000 steps
to appear -- the toy has cosine slightly behind, which is what decaying the rate
during the initial descent should look like and says nothing about step 40000.
The pilot is the test.

53fbae5116049217835b6bdddba83ba2a7107379
2026-08-05
the chained society is the default, and the model's own weights are degrading
DEFAULT is now CHAIN_ROUTE=soc + CHAIN_VOTE=1: the society, run N times over.
Each round re-routes from scratch with the society's own router with the current
state in the query, the round's experts vote on the output, and the state
carries into the next round. No transition matrix, no SRC. It is the only
configuration that has produced real multi-hop routing (H(hop1|hop0) = 0.533
bits over 202k transitions, against 0.005-0.058 for every transition-matrix arm)
at society-class stability. The PATH banner describes whichever one is running
rather than always describing the transition walk, which it was still doing
after the default flipped.

THE DIVERGENCE IS REAL, AND I HAD NOT ACTUALLY CHECKED. The DIVERGING verdict
was computed from the per-TOKEN loss curve, and the tokenizer mints throughout a
run (256 -> 2048), so each token comes to carry more bytes and per-token loss
rises MECHANICALLY. A unit-stable curve -- held-out bits/byte per process -- was
sitting in the same report the whole time. It now cross-checks, and says:

  per-token   best 3.62 -> final 4.23   +0.610
  bits/byte   +1.220 since its own minimum

So it is not units. It is worse than the per-token curve implied, and the
verdict is unchanged but now earned rather than assumed. Where it IS units, the
report now says NOT DIVERGING instead.

WHAT THAT CROSS-CHECK EXPOSES: model-alone held-out bits/byte bottoms at 2.40
around step 6000 and rises to 3.62 by 48000, while the end-of-run figure reads
2.058 because the memory store masks it. The WEIGHTS are getting worse; memory
compensates. The leading explanation is our own tokenizer: minting re-tokenizes
the stream, so the same text acquires new ids and every embedding and head row
learned for the old segmentation is invalidated, continuously, for the whole
run. That is the project's own continual-learning failure mode, self-inflicted,
and it has been present in every pilot including FABRIC=0.

TOK_MINT_UNTIL=<step> freezes the vocabulary after a warmup -- keep the learned
segmentation early, stop moving the target once the model has to fit it. 0 =
never freeze (unchanged behaviour).

Verified: the new default, CHAIN_ROUTE=transition, SOCIETY=1, and a mint freeze
all exit 0 with the banner describing what actually ran.

7b1821410761e8380af125dc449f04ccf32a792a
2026-08-05
CHAIN_ROUTE=soc -- the society, actually looped
You clarified that chain_vote was meant to be "the society system, allowed to
loop over and over", and what I built was not that. CHAIN_VOTE changed only WHERE
the experts combine (prediction level, per hop) and kept the learned TRANSITION
MATRIX for choosing them: each hop routes FROM the current holder via its SRC
mark. That is chaining with a vote bolted on, and it is where the rail lives --
H(hop1 | hop0) measured 0.020 bits in the CHAIN_VOTE pilot, the same 0.007-0.058
band as every other arm, because the query is dominated by the holder's identity
and a signature that does not change between hops.

CHAIN_ROUTE=soc is the architecture you described: run the society, feed its
result back in, run it again. Every iteration re-routes FROM SCRATCH with the
society's own grounded + weight-prediction router, with the CURRENT STATE in the
query. No transition matrix. No SRC.

The stop decision is a per-iteration PROBABILITY rather than an absorbing mass:
alive starts at 1, each round takes alive * p_stop and passes on
alive * (1 - p_stop). Convex by construction, and the honest reading of "when am
I done" -- each round the router looks at where the computation actually is and
decides whether to answer or go round again.

Toy signal, and it is only a toy:
                    H(hop1|hop0)   HALT     held-out
  society                    n/a    n/a        5.044
  vote  (transition)      -0.000  0.328        5.191
  socloop                 -0.000  0.345        4.918
  socloop_w                0.270  0.690        4.925
0.270 bits is the first non-trivial value this measurement has ever produced --
every arm of the 18-arm grid and both pilots sat at 0.007-0.058. The second
choice genuinely varies given the first. Whether that survives 48k steps on real
text is exactly what the pilot is for; I have been wrong from a toy before.

Grid arms socloop and socloop_w added, first in the order.

4da76b826ecd211cedfee65b6af12d86d4501a50
2026-08-05
the DIRTY flag counted untracked files, so a clean pull reported DIRTY
git status --porcelain lists UNTRACKED files as well as modified ones, and a
working tree that has ever run a pilot is full of them -- fetched corpora,
checkpoints, logs. So the build line marked a freshly-pulled, unmodified
checkout as DIRTY, which is a false alarm about the one thing it exists to
certify: whether the CODE matches the commit. It now counts TRACKED
modifications only, and says how many.

data_pilot/, data_big/ and data_grid/ are also added to .gitignore. They are
fetched corpora, and longrun.sh pilot writes data_pilot/ -- so the first pilot
anyone runs made the tree untracked-dirty forever after.

22a708deae98a938dc561fc8ceea69d6810372f7
2026-08-05
banner lies are now structurally impossible, and every log names its commit
The banner has lied three times -- "per-expert memory ON " for a whole 48k-step
run where it was off from step 0, "grounded region + learned bilinear" on a path
with no region term, and FAB_MIN_STEPS=2 while the code ran 0 -- and each was
fixed by hand while the next one was already sitting there. Fixing them one at a
time was never going to converge, because the failure is structural: the banner
re-read os.environ, which is what was ASKED FOR, while the code ran an effective
value that is often an AND with something else.

So it is derived now, not written:
- every config read goes through _env(), which RECORDS what the environment
  explicitly set (_i/_f route through it, and the 72 direct os.environ.get calls
  in Fabric and PlateauGrowth were converted, so nothing bypasses it)
- one declarative table maps env name -> the LIVE object's value: fab.min_steps,
  fab.vote, fab.region_w, fabgrow.latch, mem.n_own, world_proj is not None, ...
  47 knobs
- the divergence check is a LOOP over that table, not a human remembering. It
  reproduces all three historical lies as failures without being told about them
- [config] EFFECTIVE prints the whole table every run, so the log says what ran
  rather than what was requested

`!!` is reserved for a divergence NOBODY REGISTERED. A known benign adjustment
(MEM_CAP rounding up to owners x quota; MEM_QUOTA collapsing to the whole store
when there is no per-expert partition) carries a registered reason and prints
plainly -- otherwise the loud marker stops meaning anything and gets skimmed,
which is how the last three survived. A clean run now says so explicitly:
"no unexplained overrides: every one of the 18 environment settings took effect
as given".

Adding a knob is one row in the table. Adding an `and` clause to an existing one
needs no banner change at all: the loop notices.

Also, every log now opens with its provenance:
  [build] branch rm-predict | commit 78c3c1ebe4 | clean | 2026-08-05 <subject>
Arms get compared across days and commits, and without this "pilot 6 vs the
grid" is a comparison between two things nobody can identify afterwards. DIRTY
is called out explicitly, because a log from an uncommitted tree is not
reproducible from its commit and should not be filed as if it were.

ENC_VREG/ENC_CREG were read inside the encoder loss and separately in the
banner; hoisted to module level so the two cannot disagree.

Verified: base (no overrides), CHAIN_VOTE=1 FAB_MIN_STEPS=3 (correctly reports
the forced 0 as an unexplained override), FABRIC=0 (reports the quota adjustment
as explained, not as an alarm), SOCIETY=1. All exit 0, no tracebacks.

78c3c1ebe4dc4921d6224fd7344625a5847eb077
2026-08-05
FAB_MIN_STEPS: one source of truth, because three places were reporting the wrong one
Forcing min_steps off inside forward() with a local conditional left
s.min_steps reading 2 while the effective value was 0 -- and the [config]
banner, the CHAINING report section and the SAVED CHECKPOINT all print or store
it. The checkpoint line was worse still: it wrote _i("FAB_MIN_STEPS", 0), the
env var with the wrong default, so a resume could rebuild the fabric with a
different depth policy than the run that saved it.

This is the same class of lie the banner rewrite was meant to make impossible.
A value that is overridden has to be overridden where it LIVES, so the override
is visible to everything that reads it. s.min_steps is now set to 0 in
__init__ when CHAIN_VOTE is on; forward(), the banner, the report and the
checkpoint all read that one value.

The banner also now names the BLEND RULE, which is a real fork in behaviour and
was invisible:
  CHAIN_VOTE=0  experts are mixed in the HIDDEN STATE and decoded once at the
                end; HALT only scales the update, which is why it measures ~0
  CHAIN_VOTE=1  experts vote on the PREDICTION at every hop, so the mass that
                HALTS at a hop selects that hop's answer -- and min_steps is
                forced to 0 because blocking HALT blocks the thing choosing the
                answer

Verified: vote (min_steps 0), base (min_steps 2) and society all exit 0, with
the banner and the report section agreeing with the value that actually ran.

9b179b58f17ed6d1f5bb29f055ada6119bfbf643
2026-08-05
CHAIN_VOTE: society's blend rule with chaining's depth -- and HALT fires
The two paths differ in TWO independent ways and the grid only ever tested them
together: DEPTH (one hop vs many) and WHERE THE EXPERTS COMBINE (at the
PREDICTION, sum_i w_i * head(o_i), vs in the HIDDEN STATE, decoded once at the
end). Society won the grid outright; chaining lost to FABRIC=0 entirely. This
runs multi-hop with the society's combination rule: at every hop the experts
vote on the OUTPUT, and h still carries each hop's result into the next, so
composition survives.

IT IS ALSO THE ONLY CONFIGURATION IN WHICH HALT HAS A JOB, which is why it is
the answer to both requests at once. Today _alive merely scales the residual
update and only h_final is decoded, so HALT's gradient answers "how much fabric
do I want at all" and never "when am I done" -- PONDER=0.01 was the sole
pressure toward stopping, and HALT measured 0.0000 in all 18 arms. That is the
gradient's CORRECT answer to the question the architecture asks. Under voting,
the mass that halts at hop t SELECTS hop t's prediction, so stopping early is
rewarded exactly when later hops are worse.

Measured, first try, same toy config:  HALT 0.0000 -> 0.2213, held-out 5.361 ->
5.191.

FAB_MIN_STEPS is forced off under CHAIN_VOTE, and must be: it zeroes the
accumulated HALT column at the top of each early hop, so an increment counted as
"halted at hop t" would be discarded a hop later and the accumulator would stop
being a convex combination. Its other purpose -- stopping the router writing the
experts off before they can learn -- inverts here, since HALT now selects which
hop answers rather than switching the fabric off. The convex-combination
invariant (entry-halt + per-hop increments + never-halted = 1) is asserted at
runtime for the first few steps so a later edit cannot break it silently.

THE REGRESSION IS NOW SEPARABLE. Two changes landed between pilot 6 (+1.438) and
grid base (+2.287) and were never isolated: the breadth-cap ban began masking the
chaining path's logits, and the growth ramp started latching off. CHAIN_BAN=0 and
FAB_RAMP_LATCH=0 turn them off independently.

Grid round 2, reordered so the new questions run first:
  vote vote_w society base noban nolatch vote_soc weights nofabric balance
  keynorm divw smallpop curric wt_bal chainsup explore kitchen
vote_soc is CHAIN_VOTE=1 FAB_STEPS=1 -- which IS the society path, and so
separates DEPTH from the BLEND RULE rather than confounding them again.

Verified: vote, vote_w, vote_soc, noban, nolatch, base, society all exit 0, no
tracebacks, no invariant violations, and the vote path demonstrably changes the
logits rather than falling through.

Every chaining arm is worse than FABRIC=0. The generated text says the same
thing more plainly than the numbers do: society and nofabric produce English,
every chaining arm produces degraded noise. That is the deliverable, and it
agrees with bits/byte.

THE RAMP LATCH WORKED AND DID NOT HELP. Churn fell from ~10062 grown / 5969

rather than pinned at the cap. Divergence got WORSE (+1.438 -> +2.287 for base).
The cull-refill cycle was real and was not the cause. Recorded as a clean
negative for the hypothesis.

A CORRECTION I OWE THE RECORD. "The weight-prediction term is 2% of the routing
decision" was measured on a 64-expert TOY. At 4096 experts base measures 7%
region / 93% weight-prediction -- the weight term was already dominant at scale,
and FAB_KEY_NORM=1 REDUCES it (keynorm: 41/59) rather than rescuing it. The
direction of that finding was wrong, and it was wrong because I measured it at a
scale the user had already told me was unrepresentative.

specialization of any chaining arm (0.142 against a 0.000 null, where base
reads exactly 0.000).

WHY CHAINING FAILS, measured across all arms and unmoved by any intervention:
  depth 1.00 of 4 and HALT mass 0.0000 in every arm -- the walk runs its full
  length at full strength on every window; the router never once chose to stop,
  and PONDER charging for depth could not lift it.
  H(hop1 | hop0) = 0.007 to 0.058 bits everywhere. softroute (ROUTE_T=0.3),
  curric, stateq and chainsup all leave it there. One decision, then a rail.
So chaining applies four sequential low-rank transforms chosen by essentially a
single decision, at full strength, and the representation degrades.

FIXES:
- generate() sanitizes its distribution. multinomial raises a device-side CUDA
  assert on any non-finite entry, INSIDE the report -- so four arms finished
  training and then lost their entire report at the last step. A diverged run
  produces exactly that. Generation is a DIAGNOSTIC of a possibly-broken model
  and must survive one; it now repairs the row, samples, and says how many steps
  it had to repair.
- HALT mass during TRAINING is now reported on the chaining path. It was gated
  to SOCIETY, so the only halt figure on the default path came from a
  report-time probe and there was no way to tell the run from the probe.
- CHAIN ORDER is suppressed on the society path, where forward() runs only in
  that probe and the line reported a 1-sample artifact as a finding.

The divergence arms are the point of the expansion. Every pilot bottoms at

  nofabric  FABRIC=0 -- THE partition. If the bare GRU diverges too, none of the

iteration), plus curric / stateq / chainsup which have never run at scale.

- DIV_W was read as a LOCAL of main(), so the chaining implementation added here
  raised NameError on the first hop. That would have killed every chaining arm
  in the grid -- 15 of 18.

  from _w.mean(0).topk(). IndexError the first time anyone set DIV_W > 0. Nobody

  rewards experts for DIFFERING has been un-runnable since routing went
  per-window, and silently. _O is already in rank order; slots 0 and 1 ARE the

needs per-expert outputs and a composed walk has no separable per-expert LOGITS
-- but it does have separable per-expert OUTPUTS, in Bo, one set per hop. This
matters now: specialization finally moved off the floor in pilot 7 (0.094
against a 0.000 null) and DIV_W is the only pressure that rewards differing.

Also removed a dead module-level ROUTE_T = _f("ROUTE_T", 1.0): assigned, never
read, and with a DIFFERENT default from the one that actually routes
(Fabric.route_t, default 0.1). Two names for one env var with disagreeing
defaults is how a config gets misread.

longrun.sh grid: unattended arm grid, and runs/ is now append-only
RUNS ARE NEVER OVERWRITTEN. Every subcommand wrote $OUT/<name>.log and
SAVE_CKPT=$OUT/<name> directly, so re-running a pilot destroyed the previous
one -- including the checkpoint pilot-add and the ACROSS THE RUN BOUNDARY
section use as their baseline. _reserve() now suffixes -2, -3, ... and `pilot`
uses it. Results are the expensive part of this project.

NEW: `bash longrun.sh grid`. Built for `sleep 2h && git pull && bash longrun.sh
grid`:
  - nothing interactive (PROBE_WAIT=0 skips the 12s "Ctrl-C to abort" pause,
    which unattended is dead time and a false statement)
  - one arm at a time; an arm that dies is recorded and the grid CONTINUES
    (verified against a real SIGTERM: rc=143 logged, next arm started)
  - re-running RESUMES: complete arms are skipped, partial logs are moved to
    <arm>.log.partial-N rather than truncated, so the same command can be fired
    as many times as wanted
  - a summary table at the end: held-out, vs order-1, curve-since-minimum,
    distinct experts, top-expert share, routing mix

  curric   CHAIN_CURRIC=1                   staged depth, which has never run

WIN/BATCH_W/ENC_WARMUP/MEM_CAP/VMAX/COH_*/MANAGE_EVERY are now ${VAR:-default}
so the grid can be exercised at toy scale; defaults are the pilot's values
unchanged.

recording, skip-on-rerun, partial-log preservation, and the summary table.

ff0f0fa9cf8e4b6ad7a8b1e8cfc530491a252b8e
2026-08-05
the growth ramp never latched off: the population was replaced 1.5x over
THE FLAGGED ISSUE. PlateauGrowth's ramp is armed while `n < ramp_to * cap` --
CURRENT population below target -- and culling holds the population just under
the cap indefinitely, so the ramp stayed armed for entire runs and re-fired
every cool//8 = 187 steps.

The arithmetic, because the obvious reading is wrong and I got it wrong first:
growth is clamped by FAB_NMAX - n, so AT the cap the ramp adds nothing. What it
does is REFILL, within 187 steps, whatever the last cull removed. Across three
pilots ~10062 grown = ~4093 building the population once + ~5969 refilling 5969
culls. The population reads as a stable 4096 while being replaced about 1.5x
over; a tenth of it is freshly-initialised at any moment; and the identity space
every eemb key and every centroid is defined over IS that churning set.

It is a cull-refill cycle selection cannot win, since whatever it removes comes
back within 187 steps. It is not the loss-driven feedback loop I guessed at
earlier -- the ramp never reads the loss at all. And it is present in the
society pilot (7752 grown / 3659 culled) as well as both chaining pilots, which
matches those runs all diverging shortly after first reaching the cap.

The ramp now latches on first arrival at ramp_to * cap and does not re-arm.
After that, growth must come from a REGRESSION or a stall -- from evidence that
more capacity is needed. Simulated against the real dynamics (cap 4096, culling
3%/500 steps): 72 ramp events instead of continuous, latched, and the population
is then shaped by selection instead of refilled by schedule.

New POPULATION CHURN report section: grown vs removed vs NET, what fraction of
growth was replacement, which of ramp/regression/stall fired, and whether the
ramp latched. "4096 nodes (10062 grown)" was the only trace of any of this and
it reads as healthy growth.

ALSO, from auditing what actually reaches the routing decision:
- dom_ban was computed in the society branch and never passed to forward(), so
  the percentage breadth cap was INERT on the default path -- a handful of
  experts could absorb everything, and did (top expert 79.5% last pilot). It now
  applies to the chaining entry distribution and every transition.
- ROUTING MIX now enumerates all six things that reach the expert ranking, so
  "100% weight-prediction" is not read as more exclusive than it is.

Verified: weights-only, baseline, SOCIETY=1 and FABRIC=0 all reach the report at
exit 0.

fcdfaa77835f939f0f30ea75bb09b3be05ca2693
2026-08-05
ROUTE_REGION_W: run the router on PREDICTED WEIGHTS ALONE
The signature-region term is now scaled by ROUTE_REGION_W (default 1.0
unchanged). At 0 the router decides entirely by predicting the weights of the
expert it wants -- q_route's point in identity space against every expert's
embedded FULL WEIGHTS, with edec decoding the query into a real expert when
nothing is near. That is this branch's design, and it had been contributing 2%
of the routing decision.

Not the same as ROUTE_GROUNDED=0, which drops to the OLD q_entry key router and
skips the weight path entirely -- worth stating because it is the obvious thing
to reach for and it tests something else.

Verified at ROUTE_REGION_W=0 FAB_KEY_NORM=1:
  ROUTING MIX  region 0.000 (0%)  vs  weight-prediction 2.912 (100%)
  top expert share 59.6% -> 52.7%
  SPAWNED BY SPECIFICATION: 3 -- the first time that mechanism has fired in any
  run. It reads 0 in every pilot so far, which makes sense: "no live expert is
  near this query" only means something when the query is what routes.

FAB_KEY_NORM=1 is required, not optional, and the banner refuses to be quiet
about it. Unnormalised, the weight term is a raw dot whose spread across experts
measures 0.075 against the region term's 3.7; remove the region term without
normalising and the logits are near-uniform over 4096 experts, i.e. random
routing dressed as a result. Config prints a [config] !! line in that case.

Verified: weights-only, weights-only-unscaled, baseline and SOCIETY=1
weights-only all reach the report at exit 0.

e0ce4f78a64a4de305e36891dbb4ee80a39ace60
2026-08-05
the weight-prediction router is 1% of the routing decision -- measured, in the run
Three corrections, all of which the last commit's conclusions depended on.

1. THE WEIGHT PREDICTION IS NOT DRIVING ROUTING. This branch exists so the
router PREDICTS THE WEIGHTS of the expert it wants: q_route emits a point in
identity space, every expert's full weights are embedded into the same space by
eemb, and edec decodes the query into a real expert when nothing is near. That
term is summed with the older signature-region term, and nothing measured the
split. New ROUTING MIX line, off the live run:

    signature-region spread 3.699 (98%)  vs  weight-prediction spread 0.075 (2%)

The cause is one operator. The region term is divided by route_t=0.1, i.e.
multiplied by 10; the weight term is a raw dot that is not. FAB_KEY_NORM=1 puts
both on one scale and the split becomes 66/34, with the top expert's share
falling 60% -> 46% and half the traffic spreading from 1 expert to 2. That flag
has been sitting at 0 marked "not A/B'd at a size where the answer means
anything"; there is now a reason to run it.

2. THE STAGED-DEPTH RESULT WAS NOT A RESULT. maybe_deepen sat behind
`step % MANAGE_EVERY == 0`, and `step` counts WINDOWS while that block runs only
on the 1-in-BATCH_W flush steps -- at BATCH_W=4, MANAGE_EVERY=20 the
intersection is empty, so it was NEVER CALLED in a real run. I reported "staged
depth did not help" from a run in which it had not executed. It fires now
(`[chain @ 59] depth 1 stopped paying -> 2 hop(s) of 4`). A stage also ends
after CHAIN_STAGE_MAX checks so it advances on a still-falling loss, since this
model is underfit by its own report and a pure plateau test cannot fire on it.
This is the third instance of the same cadence bug in this file; _greach and the
two new instruments had it too. All four now key on the backward counter.

3. THE ORDERING QUESTION IS NOW ASKED IN THE REAL RUN, NOT A TOY. The previous
measurement was 6 domains and 24 experts, which is not this system -- the pilot
runs 4096 experts over ~37 live domains. New CHAIN ORDER line reports
H(hop1 | hop0) in bits at whatever scale the run uses, plus how many hop-0
experts always hand to the same successor. 0 bits means the chain makes one
decision and then follows a rail. It also replaces the metric I misread last
time: I(domain; pair) vs I(domain; hop0) saturates whenever hop 0 already
identifies the domain, so it cannot tell "collapsed" from "correct".

CHAIN_SUP, CHAIN_CURRIC, CHAIN_STATE_Q stay off by default -- but the earlier
numbers against them are withdrawn, not upheld: the curriculum never ran, and
the other two were only ever tested on the toy.

Verified: chaining, SOCIETY=1, ROUTE_GROUNDED=0, FAB_KEY_NORM=1 and
CHAIN_CURRIC=1 all reach the report at exit 0.
