7e9612dd14c975b7962e8d92091e605724867e59
2026-08-05
three attempts at chain credit assignment, all measured, all defaulting OFF
Answering "was anything done for the backprop failure" -- honestly, no. The
last commit changed what the router SCORES with (the grounded region term). The
credit assignment through the chain was untouched. This commit attempts it, and
reports that the attempts did not work rather than shipping them.

The structural facts are not in doubt:
  - the only loss is one cross-entropy at the END of the walk, reaching hop t
    through D-t LayerNorms and mixtures
  - topk's INDICES are not differentiable, so the gradient can re-weight an
    expert already chosen but can never say "you should have gone elsewhere"
  - nothing evaluates whether hop t's choice was right GIVEN hop t-1's

Three interventions, on a task where each domain needs an ORDERED pair of
transforms (6 domains, 24 experts, depth 4, 3 seeds, uniform-guess loss 3.871):

  baseline                            loss 2.52 / 3.00 / 2.72
  CHAIN_SUP=0.3   per-hop deep supervision, so each hop and the router that
                  chose it get a local "did this move help"
                                      loss 3.17 / 3.50 / 3.64   WORSE, all seeds
  CHAIN_CURRIC=1  staged depth: start at 1 hop, add one when the loss plateaus,
                  so the order is learned one position at a time (D sequential
                  N-way choices instead of one N^D search)
                  depth rarely left 1; where it reached 2, worse
  CHAIN_STATE_Q=1 the transition query sees the CURRENT state, not just the
                  input signature + which expert holds it
                                      loss 2.79 / 2.71 / 2.74   neutral

All three off by default, kept as flags with the numbers recorded next to them,
so the next attempt starts from this rather than repeating it. Per-hop
spawn-by-specification is wired the same way (it only ever ran at entry, so a
mid-chain query with nothing near it could never create anything).

A CORRECTION, because I reported the diagnosis before testing it. What prompted
this was I(domain; (hop0,hop1)) equalling I(domain; hop0) to three decimals,
which I read as "the second hop carries zero information". That reading is
wrong. I(dom; pair) >= I(dom; hop0) always, and when hop0 already identifies the
domain at ~0.83 the metric is saturated -- equality is also what CORRECT
behaviour looks like. If the domain determines the right pair, hop1 being a
deterministic function of hop0 is the answer, not the failure.

What stands: the concentration is real and measured (25 distinct experts against
society's 487 in the pilot). What does NOT stand: that per-hop credit assignment
is its cause. These three results are evidence against it. The metric that would
settle it is H(hop1 | hop0, domain) -- can the chain vary its second move for the
same first move when the material calls for it -- and that is not yet measured.

Verified: chaining, SOCIETY=1, all three flags ON together, FABRIC=0 and a
prompt.py read-back all reach the report at exit 0.

a71820a8e417d0edcd514e307be3da21f0ea7f24
2026-08-05
the chaining path had a different, weaker router -- and the banner said otherwise
THE BANNER. Rewritten structurally rather than patched. It used to print at the
top of main(), before model/fab/mem existed, which forced it to re-read
os.environ for every value -- a PARALLEL DESCRIPTION of the system rather than a
reading of it, and a parallel description drifts. It is now built after
construction and reads the live objects (fab.cap, fab.chain_k, fab.min_steps,
fab.explore, fab.halt_on, fab.emb_every, mem.n_own, ...) or the computed
variable, never the environment. Where the two differ it prints an OVERRIDDEN
line naming the flag responsible, so "MEM_PER_EXPERT=1 was asked for but did
NOT run" appears instead of "per-expert memory ON " on a run where it was off
from step 0. That class of lie is now structurally impossible rather than fixed
case by case.

THE ROUTER. The rewritten banner immediately exposed a bigger one: it read
"grounded region + learned bilinear" on the chaining path, and that was false.
route_w (society) and Fabric.forward (chaining) each had their own entry router
and the two had drifted apart. Society scored a GROUNDED cosine to each
expert's owned region, plus a learned key term, and EMA'd the regions toward
the signatures they served. Chaining had `q_entry(gist) @ K.t()` -- free
learned keys, no region term, no centroid update anywhere in the path. That is
exactly the design this class's own comment describes as unable to specialize:
near-identical experts give near-identical keys, route_t=0.1 amplifies the
noise between them, whoever wins first collects the gradient and becomes more
distinct, and nothing gives anyone else a constituency.

Measured on a task where 8 domains each need their OWN map, with signatures
separable by construction so any failure is the router's:

  I(domain; chosen expert) / H(domain)      experts used   top share
  society   0.343 0.871 0.758 0.473          3-8           14-76%
  chaining  0.000 0.000  (before)            1             100%
  chaining  0.449 0.002 0.209 0.505 (after)  2-7           42-100%

The router could not learn where anything belonged. That is the explanation for
the pilot's 25 distinct experts against society's 487, for SPECIALIZATION 0.000
against a 0.000 null, and for the runaway growth: PlateauGrowth fires on
worsening, so a population that cannot specialize keeps failing, keeps growing,
and hits FAB_NMAX -- 9784 grow events against a 4096 cap.

entry_logits() and ground_update() are now single implementations called by
both paths. Duplication is what let them diverge; sharing them is the fix that
holds.

This closes most of the gap but NOT all of it -- chaining still concentrates
harder than society on the same task. The remaining suspect is the transition
matrix itself: iterating a sharp row-stochastic matrix is a power iteration and
converges on one dominant expert, and BAL_WARM decays the only counter-pressure
to zero by step 4000, which is where the pilot's loss turned. Not changed here;
it needs its own measurement.

Verified: chaining, SOCIETY=1, ROUTE_GROUNDED=0, FABRIC=0 WORLD_MODEL=0 and a
prompt.py read-back all reach the report at exit 0.

the training-curve line read its own sign backwards, and the banner lied twice
Read back against the 48k-step chaining pilot, which diverged: loss bottomed at

worse. The report printed "-0.059: still FALLING = more passes/steps will help".

_d8 is prev-minus-current, so negative means the loss went UP, and the text
asserted "still FALLING" regardless of sign. Two points also cannot see a
40k-step trend. It now measures against the MINIMUM of the whole curve, states
the direction in words, and prints a loud DIVERGING verdict with the list of
things that change on that timescale (fabric hitting FAB_NMAX -- growth fires
on worsening, so a rising loss grows the population, which is a feedback loop;

per-TOKEN loss mechanically as tokens lengthen; memory reaching MEM_CAP).

The society pilot has the same shape (3.61 @ 5903 -> 4.27 @ 47231), so this is
not a property of chaining. It has been happening on every long run and the
report has been reporting it as healthy.

Two banner flags printed the ENVIRONMENT value where the effective value is an
AND with something else. That 48k chaining run logged "per-expert memory ON "
for a run in which MEM_PER_EXPERT was `... and SOCIETY` and therefore off from
step 0. WORLD_GROW had the same shape against WORLD_MODEL. Both print what is
actually on now, with a note naming the flag that overrode them. This banner
exists so a log can be read back as "here is the system this measured"; a
banner that can lie is worse than no banner.

at exit 0, and the DIVERGING branch reproduces the pilot's own numbers.

8a1e3a7e639793f27cbbe272239f923d668b252c
2026-08-04
the router trains ON the experts' current weights -- make that channel honest
Answering "does the router learn too, on the updated weights?" -- yes, and
measuring it turned up three things.

FAB_EMB_EVERY defaulted to 50, and after the last commit it was live on the
chaining path (which passes step=) and dead on the society path (which did
not). That is not a cache cadence, it is a gradient switch: the routing keys
are eemb(A,B), so the identity channel is the ONLY one that reaches every
expert -- routing computes k of N, but eemb reads all N sets of weights. At 50
it throttled that channel to one step in 50 and routed on keys up to 50 steps
stale, and it silently did so on the default path only. Default is 1, both
paths pass step=, and the report says which regime ran.

_ids also conflated two kinds of reuse. Same step must return the LIVE tensors
-- it is called from route_w, forward and spawn_from, and any consumer handed
a detached copy trains nothing. A later step must return DETACHED copies or the
next backward walks a freed graph. Split.

halt_b was measured dead on the chaining path: allocated, optimized, decayed,
never gradiented -- on what is now the default path, added one commit ago. The
learned halt prior applies to the entry distribution and every transition now,
so HALT is one operator with one key and one prior on both paths.

New ROUTER LEARNING report line: which router parameters actually received
gradient this run, and which never did. This project has shipped a dead router
parameter more than once (keys/qproj/q_entry/nov/ctrl/halt_key were all
zero-gradient under grounded routing until it was noticed), and nothing else in
the report can distinguish an allocated-but-untrained parameter from a working
one. Under chaining nothing is dead; under SOCIETY=1 it correctly names
q_entry and ctrl, which that path does not use.

Verified: chaining, SOCIETY=1, FAB_EMB_EVERY=50 and a prompt.py read-back all
reach the report at exit 0.

7b28570a4be221c2ff3d9a58ec41099f0167dd02
2026-08-04
chaining is the default, and everything the old default was silently gating
SOCIETY now defaults to 0. Composition is the point of the design and it had
never once run in a training step -- the transition matrix, FAB_STEPS, PONDER
and PONDER_WARM were inert on every run this project has made, and the depth
figures in the report came from a report-time probe of a path nothing trained.
SOCIETY=1 restores the one-shot blend.

Flipping the flag alone would have silently disabled half the selection
machinery, which is the exact failure the [config] banner exists to prevent.
So the gates come with it. Fabric.forward now produces `_wrun`, the same (B,N)
"which expert served this window" table society() returns, integrated over
hops -- and on the strength of it these all work under chaining for the first
time: per-expert competence EMAs, the fast/slow error pair (so the
sustained-error cull route has inputs at all, instead of leaving utilization
under capacity pressure as the only way an expert could die), the domain
affiliation map and the breadth cap it feeds, per-expert memory ownership,
targeted birth at the current signature, and router-specified spawn.

Marginal contribution too, at the cost the path implies: there are no separable
per-expert logits to recombine, so the walk is re-walked without each candidate
(forward(ban1=...), no_grad, manage cadence -- 8 forwards per 500 steps).

Exploration was society-only. Chaining is worse off without it, not better:
measured on a 1024 population over 60 steps, the compute path reached 25% of
the experts under society and 8% under chaining, because mass CONCENTRATES as
it flows -- each hop's top-k is drawn from a distribution the previous hop
already sharpened. More hops did not mean more experts learning. Both paths now
swap a slot for a low-use expert on FAB_EXPLORE of windows.

New GRADIENT REACH report line: how many experts received a nonzero gradient on
a typical step, counted straight off fab.A.grad. Distinct from utilization --
`use` says who won a window sometime during the run, this says how many were
being trained at once. It is set by BATCH_W x k and does NOT grow with the
population, which is the ceiling on how fast a large population can
differentiate, and nothing was measuring it.

Three bugs found on the way:

- Fabric._ids cached graph-attached tensors, so a cache hit would backward
  through a freed graph. It never fired only because the training loop calls
  society() without step=, so the cadence test always failed and the embed was
  recomputed every single step -- the O(N * 2*d*r * hid) cost the cache exists
  to amortize was being paid in full at N=4096, 50x more often than intended.
  Cached detached; gradient reaches eemb on refresh steps and from ae_loss
  every step, which is what actually trains it.
- prompt.py's chaining branch did `_h = FAB(...)`, assigning the whole
  (h, depth, mass, bal) tuple and handing a tuple to model.head. A guaranteed
  TypeError the instant a chaining checkpoint was sampled. Both paths now go
  through fab_logits, which branches correctly -- the copied logic was wrong
  again, one level down from the copied Fabric class.
- prompt.py took the path from the environment, so a checkpoint trained as a
  society would be generated as a chain purely because the default changed
  since it was saved. The checkpoint decides now.

Verified: chaining (default), SOCIETY=1, FABRIC=0 and a checkpoint read-back
through prompt.py all reach the report at exit 0.

30e635d5b3ecd6500dbefc528169e3e6f94cb17a
2026-08-04
HALT on the society path: the router decides WHETHER the population answers
HALT existed only inside the chaining loop. On the society path it was
computed and thrown away -- the learned branch of route_w built a
distribution over N+1 operators and sliced off column N, and the grounded
branch (the default) had no HALT operator at all. So the router chose
WHICH experts answer but never whether they should, and the base model's
own head was unreachable except by an ablation flag.

HALT is now a real operator in both branches of route_w, scored from the
same query in identity space that the learned expert term uses so it sits
on one scale with what it competes against. Its mass is spent on
model.head(h) directly: on a one-shot path "halt" cannot mean stop
walking, it means no expert is needed for this window.

Routing weights over the experts are bit-for-bit unchanged -- renormalising
the expert slice of the N+1 softmax is identical to the old softmax over N.
The blend is the only behavioural change.

- FAB_HALT_MAX (0.9) is a barrier, not a preference: at halt=1 the experts
  get no gradient and can never become worth routing to.
- The leave-one-out contribution counterfactual blends the same way the
  forward pass did, or every contribution is offset by whatever HALT spent.
- Fabric state loads non-strict on resume and in prompt.py, and prints what
  did not match: a checkpoint predating halt_b would otherwise have thrown
  away the entire fabric over one freshly-initialised scalar.
- prompt.py honours the checkpoint's halt setting; absent means off.

Also fixes SUFFICIENCY, which has printed nothing since routing went per
window: int(_os[j]) was int() of a whole row and raised ValueError into the
section's own except. Rank slots are now labelled by modal holder, and the
section reports what the router actually emitted alongside experts-alone.

Verified: society, society+FAB_HALT=0 and chaining arms all reach the
report at exit 0.

d7994ea18abe4e8728840faf322f624142a950c4
2026-08-04
HALT now actually halts, and chaining was recording no utilization at all
Two asks, and both found something broken.

SOURCE WEIGHTS IN THE ROUTER INPUT: already there as of the embedder commit -- SRC[n] is a projection of
expert n FULL adapter weights through eemb, and the transition query is q_route(gist) + SRC[holder] + ctrl.
The report now states this explicitly instead of leaving it to be inferred.

THE ROUTER DECIDES COMPLETION: it did not. The residual step renormalised over the top-k and applied at FULL
strength however much mass had halted, so the loop ran its full depth and h kept changing after the router had
decided to stop. HALT accumulated mass and charged ponder cost while changing nothing about WHEN the
computation ended -- it answered how much, never when. The step is now scaled by the mass still routing, so as
HALT absorbs, updates shrink to zero and the state settles. Depth 0.50 -> 1.00 of 4.
SOCIETY=1 cannot satisfy this by construction: it is one-shot, experts compute once and go straight to the
head, and there is nothing to complete. The CHAINING section says so rather than implying otherwise.

AND THE ONE THAT MATTERS MORE: use[] was only written on the society path, so under SOCIETY=0 the utilization
table stayed EMPTY. Everything reading it ran blind -- culling ranks the bottom fraction by utilization (all
zero, so it culled arbitrarily), the breadth cap counts domains per expert, and discovery hands novel material
to the least-used expert. Every chaining run so far had three selection mechanisms silently disabled. It
surfaced as an IndexError in the new ROUTER SELECTION line, which is the only reason I looked.

UNGATED.

2026-08-04
the router was never broken -- I was reading a 32-window probe as if it were the run
ROUTER SELECTION over the whole run: 84 distinct experts won at least one window | top expert took 3.9% |
half the traffic went to 21 experts.

"1 of 4096 used" is measured on 32 EVAL windows. It answers "how many experts serve this small probe", not
"how many did the router ever choose". With 4096 experts and 32 probe windows a handful winning is close to
what healthy routing gives you -- and I read it as catastrophic concentration for ten turns and built four
failed fixes on it. fab.use had the real answer the whole time and nothing reported it.

Also corrected, one turn after I asserted it: identities are NOT collapsed. IDENTITY SPACE now measures
unconditionally -- 196 experts, nearest-neighbour median 0.0349, mean pairwise 0.8571, DISTINCT. The 0.000 I
diagnosed collapse from was a STALE VARIABLE: it was captured inside spawn_from, which only runs when the spawn
bar is MET, so the number meant to diagnose the collapse was recorded only on the path the collapse prevents.
A diagnostic that depends on the thing it diagnoses is not a diagnostic.

The two changes made on that false diagnosis stay, on their own merits: _var_cov on the identity embeddings
(every other encoder here has that protection and this one had none) and training the embedder EVERY step
rather than once per 50 (it was getting twelve weak updates per run; isolated, the same loss needs ~300 to
separate identities from 0.021 to 0.217).

Spawn criterion is now RELATIVE. `1 - near > 0.45` compares against the nearest of N identities, and that
distance shrinks as N grows -- so an absolute bar makes spawning impossible exactly when the population is
large. It now compares against the population's OWN typical nearest-neighbour distance.

What is actually unresolved is narrower than "the router is broken": SPECIALIZATION 0.002 against a null of
0.041 +/- 0.027. The router picks many different experts; the material each wins is not modelled differently
from a random split. That points at DIV_W=0, not at routing.

Gate: arms now run in parallel (nproc at a time, OMP_NUM_THREADS=1 each), 701 s -> 485 s. Fixed two bugs in
doing it: $JOBS used in the banner before it was defined (set -u, instant death), and `grep -c || echo 1`
appending to grep's own "0" so every arm compared unequal and reported FAIL at exit 0.

f4fc6c7ecc56b89ca37f27e430a65bbc9e636a27
2026-08-04
the router can now SPECIFY an expert that does not exist, and it gets built
Closing the loop opened by deriving identity from weights. eemb maps weights -> identity, so the router can
RECOGNISE an expert by what it is. edec is the inverse -- identity -> weights -- so the router can SPECIFY one.
q_route(gist) is already a point in identity space; read as "the expert I want for this material", it gives:

    nearest live identity within FAB_SPAWN_DIST -> route to it, as before
    nothing within it                           -> decode the query into weights, instantiate the expert asked for

Discovery stops being "hand odd material to whoever is idle" and becomes "build what was specified".

The part that makes it LEARNING rather than a heuristic: the newborn's weights ARE edec(query), so the LM loss
backpropagates through them into q_route. The router is trained on what it ASKED FOR, not only on what it chose
from what already existed. Verified differentiable end to end.

edec is tied by an autoencoder loss (weights -> identity -> weights, FAB_AE_W=0.05) because birth is rare and
the decoder would otherwise get a gradient signal far too sparse to shape it.

Unit-verified: a far query spawns (n_live 4 -> 5), a query an existing expert already serves does NOT.

NOT verified: it has never fired inside a training run -- a 120 kB test reached step ~200 without triggering, so
FAB_SPAWN_DIST=0.45 may be too strict in practice. And this stacks three coupled loops: weights -> identity ->
routing -> material -> weights, plus the router training on experts it created. Each is defensible alone;
together they are untested at scale and a toy cannot say whether they settle or oscillate.

59945e5b5d6b3897fc77268a5f6a784c0a180eef
2026-08-04
expert identity is now DERIVED from the expert's full weights, not a free parameter beside it
K and SRC were free parameters. They described an expert without being derived from it, so what an expert DOES
and where it is ROUTED could drift apart: an expert could learn something new and keep the key that sent it the
old material, or hold a key nothing matched while its weights were perfectly good. Nothing tied the two.

A dedicated embedder -- used ONLY for experts, never sees the stream, separate from the SigEncoder -- now takes
each expert's full adapter (A and B flattened, 2*d*r numbers, nothing summarised) and emits both identities:

    eemb: Linear(2*d*r -> hid) -> GELU -> Linear(hid -> 2*dk)   ->   (K_n, SRC_n)

Verified: mutating expert 3 alone moves expert 3's identity by 0.336 and every other expert's by exactly 0.

Three things stop being code and start being consequences. A replicated child lands near its parent in routing
space because its WEIGHTS are near -- the explicit SRC-copy-with-jitter is now redundant. An expert that learns
moves its own key. A culled slot cannot leave a stale identity behind, because there is no stored identity.

Cost is real: O(N * 2*d*r * hid) is not a per-step price at N=4096, d=768, so it is cached and refreshed every
FAB_EMB_EVERY=50 steps, which is also what gives the embedder its gradient. FAB_DERIVE_IDS=0 restores the free
parameters for an A/B.

FLAGGED, not resolved: routing identity now depends on weights that the routing itself trains -- an expert whose
weights shift changes where it is sent, which changes what it trains on, which shifts its weights. The old free
parameters had no such loop. The 50-step cadence damps it; whether it is stable at scale is not something a toy
run can tell me.

012a2e0eea794f5436258286bedd023fbd7e0c57
2026-08-04
restore per-source routing: I optimised away the thing that made the chain a chain
The chaining transition did not depend on which expert held the mass. Verified directly rather than by reading:
all mass on expert 0 and all mass on expert 4 produced the IDENTICAL next distribution. So
nxt = sum_n nm[n] * R[n] collapsed to (total routed mass) x (one global re-route), and the identity of the
current expert was discarded every hop. Composition still happened in h -- contributions accumulate and
gradient flows back through them -- but "expert A hands to expert B" was not a relation the router could
represent, let alone learn.

That was mine. The original carried a per-expert Linear(sig_d, dk), so R[n] genuinely differed by source. I
collapsed it to one shared q_route when tensorising for scale -- it was the O(N.sig_d.dk) term measured at
345 ms for N=65536 -- and did not notice what it removed, because the SOCIETY path never evaluates R at all.

Restored as an OUTGOING SIGNATURE per expert: K[m] is where a message may be sent, SRC[n] is the mark expert n
puts on a message it emits, and R[n -> m] = softmax((q_route(gist) + SRC[n] + ctrl(summ)) . K[m] / T). A
dk-vector each (32 floats) rather than a matrix each, so it is O(N.dk) and the 345 ms does not come back.
Verified: the same two-source test now differs (max 0.056).

R is also built only for the top-k sources actually holding mass. The full (B,N,N+1) transition is 1.07 GB at
N=4096 by itself -- a second OOM sitting behind the one that killed the chaining run.

A child inherits SRC from its parent with perturbation, so a lineage inherits WHERE IT SENDS as well as what it
computes. That is my choice, not a specified one.

2026-08-04
chaining OOM'd because every hop computed every expert -- and then chained nothing anyway
I recommended SOCIETY=0 having already written the comment saying the chaining path is O(N) and "a chained
fabric of 10,000 experts is not a thing you want". That was careless: it OOM'd at 972 experts.

Bo was (B,N,L,d) -- at N=972, B=16, L=256, d=768 that is 12 GB for ONE hop, times the depth budget, times the
autograd graph. Now only the top-k by CURRENT routing mass are computed per hop (FAB_CHAIN_K=8). The semantics
that matter are unchanged -- mass still flows expert -> expert through the transition, so an expert still builds
on another's output -- but a hop costs k experts instead of N. Everything outside the top-k contributed a weight
of ~0 to the mixture anyway: computed, multiplied by nothing, and kept alive for the backward pass.
Verified: 343 experts, SOCIETY=0, no OOM.

Then a second finding, worse than the first: with the OOM gone, mean routed depth was 0.00 of 4. HALT is
absorbing on the very first hop because FAB_MIN_STEPS defaulted to 0 -- chaining switched ON and nothing
chained. FAB_MIN_STEPS now defaults BY PATH: 0 under SOCIETY (where HALT is unused and 0 is correct), 2 under
chaining. Depth 0.00 -> 0.60 on the same config, and the CHAINING section says which applied.
A composition mechanism that is enabled but never entered is worse than one that is off, because it reads as
tested.

33355b229efd482128b2c14961de0ee2d42f5ac4
2026-08-04
the experts have never chained, and the ponder schedule I built the pilot around is inert
Asked whether experts chain via the router. They do not, and never have. The fabric has TWO forward paths:

  SOCIETY=1 (default)  society()  every expert maps the SAME h to its own output, blended at the end.
                                  Expert i never sees expert j. _dep is zeros.
  SOCIETY=0            forward()  mass flows expert -> expert through the learned transition matrix, HALT
                                  absorbing, depth adaptive and charged for.

Every run of this project used the first. The transition matrix, HALT, FAB_STEPS, PONDER and PONDER_WARM are
all present and all inert on the default path. The report now states which path ran, on every run.

TWO CORRECTIONS, BOTH MINE.
1. "the router HALTs 90%, mean routed depth 0.10 of 4" -- I quoted this for several rounds as evidence about
   the system. It comes from a report-time probe call to forward(), not from anything that trained. It
   describes a path the run did not use.
2. I built the case for the whole pilot on "PONDER_WARM=8000 never completes, so the fabric's schedule has
   never finished". On the society path the ponder cost is identically zero, so PONDER_WARM does nothing at
   all. That justification was wrong. Second time this session a justification of mine was about inert code.

WHY IT MATTERS FOR THE DESIGN: "no expert suffices alone, together they do" is currently only testable as a
VOTE, not a COMPOSITION. Averaging near-identical voters cannot beat the best voter, which is plausibly why
SUFFICIENCY keeps reporting that the best single member matches the blend. SOCIETY=0 enables composition and
runs clean; whether it is BETTER is an unmeasured A/B, not a claim.

Also this round, from the previous message: sustained-error culling (fast/slow EMA pair -- fast~=slow and both
above the population is incompetence and culls; fast >> slow is a distribution shift and is protected, which is
the old-news-changes case), a parent quota breaking the lineage monopoly (91 distinct parents, largest share 4%
against a 20% cap, where before every birth was the incumbent's child), and the pilot dropped to GRU-only since
the architecture question was answered twice.

245bc684c2ff587425af109f9052bd3b6e646066
2026-08-04
selection by SUSTAINED error, a parent quota, and the pilot drops to GRU only
Three things, and one result that isolates the remaining problem.

BACKPROP-DRIVEN SELECTION. Per-expert FAST and SLOW error EMAs, where the PAIR is what does the work:
  fast ~= slow and both above the population -> persistent incompetence -> cull
  fast >> slow                               -> a shift is in progress and the expert is ADAPTING -> protect
That second line is the old-news-changes case, and it is protected by construction rather than by a threshold:
a spike is precisely what makes fast exceed slow. This route also runs at ANY occupancy, unlike the utilization
cull which only fires under capacity pressure -- an expert that is called often AND bad was invisible to
selection before, because utilization only knows how OFTEN an expert was called, never whether it was any good.

PARENT QUOTA. The incumbent wins routing, so it is in every relevance shortlist AND it is the fittest, so every
birth is its child and the population becomes one lineage wearing 4096 hats. Diversity of a POPULATION is not
diversity of its ANCESTRY. Max 20% of recent births per parent, sliding window. Measured effect:
  SELECTION OUT: 152 culled, 72 of them for SUSTAINED error | 173 spared as load-bearing
  LINEAGE: 91 distinct parents in the recent-birth window, largest share 4% (cap 20%)

Existing culling verified working, as asked: culled 4-5 / spared 11-12 per manage, both routes firing.

PILOT IS GRU ONLY. The architecture question was answered twice -- GRU 2.064/2.200 vs transformer 2.130/2.184 on
bits/byte and 0.17 vs 0.02 on coherence. PILOT_ARCH="gru transformer" re-opens it if the base model changes.

STILL 1 OF 256 USED, and that is now a clean isolation rather than a mystery. Ancestry is diverse, selection
removes both the unused and the failing, discovery/exploration/crossover fire thousands of times, and the
encoder demonstrably separates the material (mean pairwise distance 0.871, 50 distinct nearest-centroids).
Every INPUT to the problem is healthy and the output is one expert -- so the fault is in the routing arithmetic
between "nearest centroid" and "what top-k selects". The next step is to instrument that gap (log the logit
decomposition: grounded term vs learned term vs novelty) rather than propose a fifth fix for it.

b8f78375ced98e04b9759cfc15ebbb3b5f8b2a3f
2026-08-04
put the routing diagnosis IN the report, and stop shipping unvalidated router fixes
I asked for a probe to be run on a checkpoint instead of measuring in the run that produced the routing, and
then the session closed and the checkpoint went with it. The diagnosis belongs in the report. It does now, and
it immediately falsified the hypothesis I had been reasoning from:

    200 held-back windows | mean pairwise cosine distance 0.871 | effective dimensions 14.5 of 64
    distinct nearest-experts 50 of 128 live
    >> SEPARABLE: concentration of routing is the ROUTER's doing rather than the representation's

The signature space is NOT a blob. The encoder separates the material, and 50 different experts are the NEAREST
CENTROID for some window -- while 1-3 are actually used. That gap is the entire problem and it sits in the
routing arithmetic, not the encoder and not the material.

Prime suspect found and NOT shipped as a fix. The learned term was a raw unbounded dot product of two
unconstrained trained vectors, added to a bounded cosine:

    logits  = cos(gist, centroid)/T          # bounded, +/-10 at T=0.1
    logits += q_route(gist) @ K.t()          # UNBOUNDED

so an expert whose key norm grows scores high for every input with any positive projection regardless of its
region, and gradient descent grows one key because that lowers loss fastest early. Normalising both terms onto
one scale is the principled form -- and measured on a 100 kB toy it went the WRONG way, 3 used experts to 1. At
that size the number is 1-vs-3 out of 32 windows, which is noise, and this would have been the fourth
unvalidated router change in a row. It is FAB_KEY_NORM, default 0 (original behaviour), so the pilot can A/B it
at a size where the answer means something.

e44b5b019250fab49336e3c67bed488c674b88a6
2026-08-04
route PER WINDOW, not per batch -- which is why discovery, crossover and exploration changed nothing
The pilot fired every mechanism I added and moved none of them: 3081 novelty handoffs, 436 off-policy
routings, 1770 crossover births -- and still 3 of 4096 experts used, still INTERCHANGEABLE (0.035 against a
null of 0.087 +/- 0.063), and bits/byte WORSE, 2.064 -> 2.200 on the GRU. Paid 0.14 bits for nothing.

They could not have worked. Routing was at BATCH granularity:

    idx = w.mean(0).topk(kk).indices     # one expert set for all BATCH_W windows
    _wk = _w[:, _oid[_ki]].mean(0)       # one weight vector for all of them

All 16 windows in a batch were served by the same experts at the same weights, so an expert could never come to
own a KIND of text -- only a batch average that happened to contain it. Discovery, crossover and exploration
all change WHICH expert is picked; none of them changes that everyone shares the pick.

Now per window, at no cost: einsum('bld,kdr->bklr') already computed every b x k pair, so this is the same
arithmetic with a batch dimension on the gather. Four dependent sites moved to per-row gathers: fab_logits, the
leave-one-out contribution, the independence loss, and the sufficiency probe.

prompt.py broke on it, and the read-back gate arm caught that -- the first time that arm has earned its keep on
something I introduced. It imports the model CLASSES now but still carried its own copy of the ensemble LOGIC
(`_w[:, _oid]`, the batch-level form), which threw the moment idx became (B,k). Importing the classes is not
enough while the logic using them is copied; it calls the shared fab_logits now.

HONEST STATE: this fixed a real defect and did NOT fix the concentration -- a 256-node toy still routes all 32
eval windows to one node, and eval routing was already per-window, so that was never the binding constraint
there. Best remaining explanation, held loosely: on a SINGLE English corpus the signature encoder may produce
one dense region, in which case no routing rule spreads traffic and the lever is the encoder (ENC_CREG, still
off) or the material, not ROUTE_T. probe_ckpt_geometry.py on the pilot checkpoint decides which.

2026-08-03
open a path in: discovery was structurally impossible, not merely tuned badly
The router could not discover an expert, and the reason was one line:

    j = int(w.mean(0).argmax())          # ONLY the single global winner
    s.cent[j] = EMA(s.cent[j], gist)

Only the argmax winner's centroid ever moved. The winner therefore drifted toward every region it won and
became closer still, while every other centroid stayed frozen at its initialisation. A newcomer cannot win
because its region never moved, and its region never moves because it never wins. Rich-get-richer with no path
in -- which is why 4096 experts produced ONE used node, and why that was never going to be fixed by breeding or
by culling. Three changes:

SHARED CENTROID UPDATES. The top-FAB_CENT_TOPK (8) experts by routing mass each move toward the signature they
served, weighted by share. Several experts now track the regions they actually serve.

NOVELTY -> DISCOVERY. A signature further than FAB_DISCOVER (0.35 cosine) from EVERY centroid is material nobody
owns; it is handed to the LEAST-USED expert rather than the nearest incumbent. That is the mechanism by which new
material recruits new capacity instead of being absorbed by whoever is already largest. Fired 871 times on a
150 kB test.

EXPLORATION. top-k is on-policy: only experts the router already prefers are COMPUTED, so only they receive
gradient. An expert outside the top-k is not unused, it is FROZEN, and cannot improve into contention. One slot
is swapped for a low-use expert on FAB_EXPLORE (15%) of steps. A population, not a leaderboard.

CROSSOVER, per the request for newborns to take connected sections of other experts. A low-rank expert is a sum
of r rank-1 maps A[:,i] (x) B[i,:], so a rank slice is a self-contained piece of function -- the natural
connected section to inherit. A newborn takes whole slices from several parents on the same relevance shortlist,
then mutates on top. Mutation explores AROUND a parent; crossover reaches BETWEEN them.

First run with all four: SPECIALIZATION 0.206 against a shuffled null of 0.054 +/- 0.055 -> SPECIALIZED. Every
previous run in this project said INTERCHANGEABLE.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 <noreply@anthropic.com>
Claude-Session: https://claude.ai/code/session_01PksNp6ECrYm34zjV2rbm4x

---
85652468db485558f64fce501c9e26adc43b6f2e
2026-08-03
breed by RELEVANCE not global fitness, and mutate by enough to matter
Two corrections to the replication rule, both from the same observation: a population that clones its global
champion with negligible perturbation is not exploring anything, it is copying one lineage.

PARENT = RELEVANT FIRST, FIT SECOND, AND SAMPLED. Growth is triggered BY A REGION -- the birth signature -- so
the expert that matters there is whichever already serves it, which may be a niche expert with low global
utilization and high local value. A global argmax hands every birth to the same incumbent. Now: shortlist the
FAB_PARENT_K=8 experts whose centroids are nearest the birth signature, then SAMPLE within that shortlist with
probability proportional to fitness. The sampling carries as much weight as the shortlist -- an argmax over the
shortlist would still let one local incumbent monopolise every birth in its region. Negative-contribution
parents get a floor rather than zero so the tail can still breed occasionally, which is the point of not using
an argmax at all.

MUTATION SCALED TO THE PARENT, WITH A HEAVY TAIL. FAB_CLONE_JITTER=0.02 was an absolute number against a weight
whose own scale is unknown, so a "clone" was an exact copy in practice. Now 25% of the parent's own std, and 10%
of births take a 6x jump. Most offspring stay near the parent; a few reach somewhere the lineage has not been.
Without the tail a population converges on its founder however many members it has.

Also fixed a banner line that printed "competence protection" twice, from an edit that left the tail of the
string it replaced.

HONEST RESULT: on a 256-node toy the used-node count did NOT improve -- still 1 of 256. Breeding decides what a
newborn IS; it does not decide what the router SENDS it. The concentration is a routing problem (ROUTE_T=0.1
over thousands of near-equal logits, DIV_W=0 so nothing rewards differing). Replication and mutation make the
population CAPABLE of specialising; they do not make the router explore. Those two knobs remain untouched, and
now have a real breeding mechanism to act on.

e71453112763840cbd95a87f9ef07243f33f63bc
2026-08-03
grow by REPLICATING the fittest -- a blank newborn can never earn its way in
The pilot put 75% of the routing mass on ONE node of 4096. The growth rule is why, and it was a design error
rather than a tuning problem.

Every newborn was B=0 -- an exact identity. Identity birth was chosen so that adding a node could never disrupt
what already works, and that reasoning is sound in isolation. But an identity computes NOTHING, so it has no
competence, so it attracts no routing mass, so it never gets traffic, so it can never ACQUIRE competence. It is a
trap with no exit, and 4095 blank identities losing to one trained node is exactly what it predicts.

Selection needs variation of something that WORKS. Growth now clones the fittest expert -- marginal contribution
where measured, utilization otherwise -- plus jitter, so a newborn starts competent and differentiates from
there. The world model already did this ("cloned from the fittest") and ExpertBank had it in the dead legacy
path; the fabric alone never replicated anything. FAB_REPLICATE=0 restores blank birth as the ablation.
On a 256-node test, used nodes went 1 -> 4 with real spread (66% / 22% / 9%).

IS IT COMPOSING? -- a new section, because "it produced words" deserves a measurement rather than an argument. A
tokenizer that minted whole words would let the model emit one token and look like it had spelled something.
TOKENS PER GENERATED WORD separates those: >1 means the model chose a sequence of pieces, ~1.0 means the
vocabulary did the spelling. Reported beside the fraction of generated words that exist in the training text,
which separates composition from recall. First run: 2.29 tokens/word, 77% real words.

2a262a2ed66c3dab3edf2105ab711ad93d46603f
2026-08-03
the fabric had no culling at all -- the expert population was grow-only
Two observations, and together they hid a hole in the design.

THERE WAS NO EXPERT CULLING. router.manage() -- create/replicate/cull -- is gated on EXPERTS, which is mutually
exclusive with FABRIC and therefore 0 in every default run. The only other fab.remove() is inside the
EXPERT INDEPENDENCE test, which restores immediately after. So the fabric ramped to its cap and nothing ever
removed a node. A population that only grows is not under selection whatever its growth rule is.

That also explains something I had been reporting as a puzzle for several rounds: "COMPETENCE PROTECTION spared
0", every single run. The protection was wired into router.manage -- a code path that never executes with the
default config. I said the pilot would give it a live path. It would not have.

fab.manage() now exists and mirrors the domain manager: cull only under CAPACITY PRESSURE, only the bottom
FAB_CULL_FRAC by utilization, never a newborn (FAB_GRACE), and never a node that earns its place -- positive
marginal contribution (the system is measurably worse without it), or failing that a competence better than the
population's on its own material. That last clause is the protection for the useful-but-rare: rarely called is
the bottom of a utilization ranking, and it is also what a niche expert looks like.
First run of it: "[experts @ 1600] culled 9 spared 8 -> 206 live". Protection has never fired before.

THE BANNER WAS LYING. It printed "experts off" while the expert population was ON: the EXPERTS flag names the
legacy ExpertBank, and the live population is the fabric. Saying "experts off" about a run with thousands of
routed experts is worse than saying nothing. It now states that the fabric IS the expert population and that the
legacy flag being 0 is correct rather than a missing subsystem.

Held from push until the gate is green -- I have pushed two things this session that broke the pilot.

34406343de7dd9bdba3b0ab4e92797b26d014bc1
2026-08-03
print what the run actually is, and stop the signature encoder ending at 62% coverage
CONFIG BANNER on every run. The largest error in this project's history was never a bug: six subsystems silently
defaulted OFF, so every result described a system missing its routing fabric, world model, expanding tokenizer,
per-expert memory and non-stationary stream -- and nothing in the output said so. A run that cannot be read back
as "here is the system this measured" gets misfiled later. Three lines now state subsystems, selection rules and
the deliberate OFFs, plus a note that PHASED with one corpus is stationary and the non-stationarity that matters
comes from ADDING an area.

SIG_WIN=614 in the pilot and the long run. The signature width is FIXED for a run (it has to be -- centroids are
means of encoded windows, and a width that moves mid-run makes signatures before and after incomparable, which
is what crashed both pilot arms). But the loop STRIDE grows with the tokenizer: 384 B at WIN=256 early, ~614 B
once the vocabulary compresses. At the default the encoder therefore starts at 100% coverage and ends near 62%,
labelling material it never read -- and domain assembly, expert routing and the coherence reference all consume
that signature. 614 covers the stride throughout.
The cost is real: early in the run 614 B is wider than one loop step, so consecutive signatures overlap and
boundary detection is slightly smoothed. Full coverage of the material being labelled is the better end of it,
but it is a trade rather than a free fix.

dec9fb352d83c535ee187beb0cbc5f6a1e58a8f5
2026-08-03
the optimizer moments were a one-line bug, not the inherent limit I called it
I described the moment loss on resume as inherent -- growth adds param groups, remapping across a different
flattening would attach moments to the wrong tensors, the transient is safer than the fix. That was wrong, and
I had not looked closely enough to say it.

The actual cause: since the population became preallocated tensors, fab.grow() returns [] -- the rows are
already in the optimizer. The caller still ran om.add_param_group on that return value, appending an EMPTY
param group on every growth event. A checkpoint after 60 growths carried 60 phantom groups, load_state_dict
refused the count mismatch, and every Adam moment was discarded on every resume. One line, plus replaying the
world model's genuine growth groups in their original order so the structure matches exactly.
Verified: resume now restores moments with zero warnings.

prompt.py now has ZERO duplicated model classes. MiniLM, TinyTransformer, SigEncoder and Fabric are all imported
from the file that trains them. What had forced the copies was that those constructors size themselves from
module globals, so a loader could not size them from a CHECKPOINT -- build_lm(nv=) and an nv= argument on the
three classes fixes that at the root. The duplicated Fabric is what left this file dead for several commits;
the remaining three had simply not drifted yet, which is luck, not safety.

Gate 12/12 (11 arms + read-back).

4554d6b106fdc5b283b381ee91e6423164b6a072
2026-08-03
the gate never read a checkpoint back, and one of its arms tested nothing
Three explicitly-broken things, all of them in the thing that is supposed to catch broken things.

READ-BACK. Nothing in the pipeline ever loaded a checkpoint after writing one, which is exactly how prompt.py --
the tool GENERATIONS are read with, i.e. the deliverable -- sat completely dead for several commits without
anyone noticing. The gate now trains a tiny run with SAVE_CKPT, prompts the result, and requires exit 0. Had this
existed the breakage would have surfaced the hour it was introduced instead of during an audit.

no_experts WAS VACUOUS. EXPERTS is mutually exclusive with FABRIC, which wins the forward pass's elif chain, so
with FABRIC on (the default) that arm changed nothing and passed in every gate run since it was added -- it has
been reporting coverage it never had. Replaced with expert_bank:EXPERTS=1 FABRIC=0, which exercises the
ExpertBank/ExpertRouter path that was otherwise never run at all.

Also removed a dead `SigEncoder as _SO_SigEncoder` alias left in prompt.py when it switched to importing Fabric.

Looked at and deliberately left: prompt.py still duplicates MiniLM/TinyTransformer/SigEncoder. Same failure mode
as Fabric had, but latent -- those classes have not changed, and prompt.py's SigEncoder takes vocab size
explicitly, which is MORE robust for loading arbitrary checkpoints than the trainer's global. The read-back gate
now catches them if they drift, which is the protection that matters.

Still unfixed and still reported rather than silent: optimizer MOMENTS do not restore after growth, because
add_param_group during training gives the checkpoint more param groups than a rebuilt optimizer. Costs a ~1000
step Adam re-warm per resume. Remapping moments across a different flattening would attach them to the wrong
tensors, which is worse than the transient.

Gate 12/12 (11 arms + read-back).

763e9f2001463e678e4f72938e4936aa380bd149
2026-08-03
breadth cap, empty-domain culling -- and prompt.py had been dead since the refactor
Two asks, and a sweep that found the suspicion was right.

BREADTH CAP. An expert may serve at most EXP_DOM_FRAC (10%) of the live domain population, floor EXP_DOM_MIN=4.
Percentage rather than a count because the domain population is itself grown and culled -- a fixed ceiling would
be permissive early and crushing later. Enforced at ROUTING time: an expert at its share is masked out of the
softmax for domains it does not already serve, so the cap shapes the population instead of reporting on it after
the fact. Live: "an expert may serve <= 4 domains (10% of 18, floor 4). widest 4 | 40 at the cap | median 2".

EMPTY-DOMAIN CULLING. The existing rule needs `act < min_size AND unseen > stale` -- a conjunction an empty
domain can fail forever, since act decays toward zero without reaching it and `last` only moves when the domain
is fed. That is why the pilot logged zero culls against 5-8 merges per manage: domains consolidated but were
never selected OUT. Empty is now literal (no memory carries its provenance, no sample windows) and needs no
staleness argument.

THE SWEEP.
1. A stale `fab.keys` left by the tensor refactor raised inside a try/except that printed a one-line message, and
   the ENTIRE EXPERTS and SUFFICIENCY output disappeared from the report with no sign it had been attempted.
   Fixed, and that handler now prints the full traceback -- the message alone would not have located it.
2. prompt.py was COMPLETELY DEAD and had been since the refactor. It carried its own duplicated copy of Fabric,
   which went stale the moment the population became tensors: load_state_dict failed on ~300 missing keys. That
   is the tool used to read GENERATIONS -- the deliverable -- and it would have failed silently until someone
   tried it. 69 lines of duplicated model code deleted; it imports the real Fabric now, so the class of bug
   cannot recur. Verified generating.

Standing observation, no longer speculative: at 236 experts only THREE win any eval window, specialization 0.086
against a shuffled null of 0.089 +/- 0.038. ROUTE_T=0.1 was tuned when N was 64 and its own comment reasons about
N=64; at scale it concentrates nearly all mass on a handful. The breadth cap limits how many DOMAINS an expert
takes, not how much TRAFFIC, so it does not address this.

Gate 11/11.

cc04c21d6819f42a9a84e14cc4d04bbf33501d35
2026-08-03
experts as tensors: 64 was three ceilings, and the two populations were 15,625x apart
"If we only had 64 experts, how were there hundreds of domains?" -- because MAX_DOMAINS defaults to 64 with the
comment "hard cap, MIRRORING the expert bank's fixed slot pool", and every launcher then set MAX_DOMAINS=1000000
while leaving FAB_NMAX at 64. The two populations designed as duals ran four orders of magnitude apart. Hundreds
of domains routed through 64 experts means expert granularity was coarser than domain granularity by more than
100x, so "experts competing within a domain" could not happen at all. MAX_DOMAINS now defaults to FAB_NMAX and
the launcher overrides are gone.

64 itself was three separate ceilings, none of them a decision:
  PARAMETERS  FabricNode was a full residual MLP d->2d->d: 2.36M params each at d=768, so 1000 experts is 2.36B
              (9.5 GB) and a million is 9.4 TB. Now the low-rank d->r->d form ExpertBank already used: 12.3k at
              r=8, so a million is 12.3B (49 GB). Still born an exact identity (B zero-init).
  PYTHON      keys was a ParameterList and qproj a ModuleList, so every step ran torch.stack(list(s.keys)) and
              [q(gist) for q in s.qproj] -- O(N) Python object iteration. Single tensors now.
  SLOTS       growth appended, which reallocates, which invalidates the optimizer's parameter references.
              Preallocating to FAB_NMAX removes that: tensors never change identity, only n_live grows, and
              unused rows are exact identities. remove() is swap-with-last, O(1).

A fourth only measurement found: parameters scaled but TIME did not (345 ms at N=65536). Every expert had its own
sig_d x dk query matrix, making scoring O(N*sig_d*dk) -- attention with a per-expert query projection, which is
the wrong shape. One shared query projection with per-expert keys took it to 15 ms, and 8192 experts now cost the
same as 64. It also cut per-expert parameters by a third.

    N=       64  params   0.3M   society(top-4)  1.6 ms
    N=    8,192  params  33.8M   society(top-4)  1.5 ms
    N=   65,536  params 270.5M   society(top-4) 15.2 ms

GROWTH: +3 per event cannot build a population of thousands. The ramp window expires at step 4000 and afterwards
growth needs a plateau or a regression, which are rare -- so the old rule reaches 243 experts by step 60,000 and
stops. Growth is now a FRACTION of the population and the ramp ends on POPULATION SIZE rather than a step number:
3 -> 4096 in 63 events by step 3101. Measured live: grew 17 -> 196, 19 -> 215, 21 -> 236.

Also: MEM_OWNERS decouples the memory partition from FAB_NMAX (sizing owners to 4096 would have given each expert
48 store entries), and expert ids fold into the owner count since an owner is an eviction bucket, not an identity.

2e3a464780e88aa71f57411144cd4967fe9393cc
2026-08-03
the expert population was capped at 64 by three accidents and a wrong routing shape
Selection over tens is not selection -- the pilot saturated 64/64 at step 1295 and everything after was merge/cull
churn over a full bank. None of what capped it was a design decision.

PARAMETERS. A FabricNode was a full residual MLP d -> 2d -> d: 2.36M parameters at d=768, so a thousand experts
is 2.36B (9.5 GB fp32) and a million is 9.4 TB. Now the low-rank d -> r -> d form ExpertBank already used
(FAB_RANK=8): 12.3k each, so a million is 12.3B / 49 GB -- reachable. Still born an exact identity (B zero-init),
so adding one never disturbs what already works. Each expert is individually much weaker, which is the intent:
no single one is meant to suffice.

PYTHON. keys was a ParameterList and qproj a ModuleList, so every step ran torch.stack(list(s.keys)) and
[q(gist) for q in s.qproj] -- O(N) Python object iteration per step. Invisible at 64, dominant at 10,000. Single
tensors now.

SLOTS. Growth appended, which reallocates, which invalidates the optimizer's parameter references. Preallocating
to FAB_NMAX removes the problem: the tensors never change identity, only n_live grows, and unused rows are exact
identities. remove() is swap-with-last, O(1), and carries the centroid with its expert.

And a fourth found only by measuring, which the other three would have hidden. Parameters scaled and TIME did not:

    N=64  1.7 ms   N=1,024  2.2 ms   N=8,192  26.2 ms   N=65,536  345.2 ms

Every expert had its OWN sig_d x dk query matrix, making the routing score O(N*sig_d*dk) -- attention with a
per-expert query projection, which is the wrong shape. One SHARED query projection with per-expert keys, which is
what attention actually is:

    N=64  1.6 ms   N=1,024  1.2 ms   N=8,192   1.5 ms   N=65,536  15.2 ms

8,192 experts now cost what 64 did, and per-expert parameters drop by a third.

FAB_NMAX default 64 -> 4096. Memory is 2*NMAX*d*r floats: 0.2 GB at 4k, 0.5 GB at 10k, 49 GB at 1M (a GH200 and
nothing smaller). Two consequences handled: the per-expert memory partition derived its size from FAB_NMAX and
would have given 48 entries per expert, so owners are capped separately (MEM_OWNERS=64) and expert ids fold into
them -- an owner is an eviction bucket, not an identity. And the CHAINING path still computes all N, so only
SOCIETY (the default) is viable at scale; that is now stated in the code rather than discovered later.

Verified: full run + resume clean at the new default, growth 63/4096 instead of saturating 64/64.

2a682d7daab68c8b952596c1e3171bb277d1add4
2026-08-02
the signature width must NOT move mid-run -- it killed both pilot arms
My own regression, one commit old. Closing the 42%-coverage gap by recomputing _sigw live as the tokenizer grew
crashed both arms of the pilot at the first rekey:

    ValueError: expected sequence of length 384 at dim 1 (got 426)

asm.wins holds sample windows captured at the OLD width and rekey concatenates them into one batch, so a width
that changes produces a ragged batch. The crash is the lesser problem. Domain centroids ARE means of encoded
windows, so changing the width mid-run makes signatures taken before and after incomparable -- every centroid,
radius and boundary test would straddle two different measurements, silently. Live update was wrong in
principle, not just in implementation.

Now fixed for the lifetime of the run. rekey normalises a ragged batch rather than dying, since one bad window
should never end a multi-day run. And because a fixed width cannot track a growing stride, the coverage line
reports the PROJECTED figure too: "covers 100% now, ~62% once the vocabulary has grown" with the SIG_WIN value
that would cover it throughout, and an explicit note that doing so makes consecutive signature windows overlap
-- a real trade, not a free fix.

THE GATE PASSED THIS, which is the more important failure. Every smoke arm ran 12 kB, where the vocabulary
barely moves, the stride stays put, and asm.wins never holds two widths -- so the gate certified a change that
killed both pilot arms in under two minutes. Added a `vocab_growth` arm (200 kB, VMAX=1024, GROW_EVERY=20,
REKEY_EVERY=200) which reproduces the failure and now guards it. A gate only covers what it exercises.

Verified under the conditions that crashed: vocab 512 -> 1024 with repeated rekeys, clean exit.

98e3301ed3ee5d6881464b7dba989e2f985a7803
2026-08-02
the signature encoder was reading 42% of the stream
Found in the pilot's own output while readying it:

    [signature] space=bytes | window 256 B | loop stride 384 B (256 tok x 1.50) -> covers 67% of the stream

SIG_WIN=0 meant "use WIN" -- 256 BYTES -- while the loop advances WIN TOKENS. Early in a run one token is about
one byte and the two agree, which is why this survived: it is correct at the start of every run and drifts wrong
as the tokenizer earns its compression. By the time the vocabulary reaches ~2.4 bytes/token the loop strides 614
bytes and the signature encoder is characterising the first 256 of them, so the domain encoder reads 42% of the
stream. Nothing downstream could detect it, because every window still produced A signature -- just one computed
from the opening fragment of the material it claims to describe.

Now recomputed live from the tokenizer rather than pinned at config time, since the stride is not constant across
a run, and refreshed on the rate cadence. Verified: coverage 67% -> 100%. SIG_WIN= still overrides outright.

Every domain result this project has produced was measured through this, at whatever coverage that run's vocabulary
happened to imply.

54e55a2ac80caf2a307851529044d4f381e74e4b
2026-07-31
competence by COUNTERFACTUAL, not correlation -- and sufficiency measured on the outcome
Answering how competence was actually computed, and fixing it. The version shipped an hour ago was an EMA of the
per-window loss on the windows a unit WINS, against the population's EMA over ALL material. That has a flaw that
matters for a rule deciding who lives: a node that happens to win EASY windows scores well even if any node would
do as well on them. It measures the material as much as the expert.

The counterfactual does not have that problem -- drop the expert, recombine the blend, and ask what the system's
loss does. It is also immune to the failure you named: a contribution-MAGNITUDE signal rewards a loud or noisy
expert, but a noisy expert makes the blend WORSE when present, so removing it IMPROVES the loss and its
contribution goes NEGATIVE. Only usefulness scores; volume does not.

Nearly free, and only because society() returns per-expert outputs separately rather than a blended hidden state:
every _hd[j] is already computed for the forward pass, so leave-one-out is a re-weighted sum of tensors already
in hand instead of k extra forward passes. Run on the manage cadence -> one extra cross_entropy per node per
MANAGE_EVERY steps. Culling now checks contribution first and falls back to the cheap EMA only where no
counterfactual has been measured yet.

SUFFICIENCY, measured on the outcome as you put it: the ensemble's bits/byte against the best that any SINGLE
expert manages on the same windows. This is the design claim -- no member suffices alone, together they do --
and nothing had ever tested it. First run:

    population (2 experts blended) 3.079 bits/byte | best single expert (node 48) 3.082 | population buys +0.004
    >> NOT AGGREGATE: the best single expert does as well as the whole blend

which is the expected reading while the nodes are still INTERCHANGEABLE, and is now a number rather than an
assumption. It should be the first thing checked after the pilot, since it is the claim the whole selective
structure rests on.

Experts stay decoupled from domains, as you said is fine -- they draw on domain signatures for routing and are
free to stretch across them.

Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5 <noreply@anthropic.com>
Claude-Session: https://claude.ai/code/session_01PksNp6ECrYm34zjV2rbm4x

bcd3fd5823b4652817fcc156eee809e48c5174da
2026-07-31
selection gains a COMPETENCE term, so useful-but-rare survives; pilot runs both architectures
Every cull rule ranked on UTILIZATION alone -- fabric soft_cull on routing mass, ExpertRouter on
use-per-unit-time, domains on decayed `act`. Utilization is the right resource, but on its own it cannot tell a
niche expert that is excellent when called from a dead one: both are called rarely. The protections that existed
were all TIME-based (grace for the newborn, the AND-clause on staleness, bounded rank turnover, culling only
under capacity pressure) -- they protect the NEW and bound the RATE of death, never the USEFUL-BUT-RARE.

Competence is now tracked online and free: cross_entropy already computes per-window losses before reducing, so
taking reduction='none' and .mean() is identical arithmetic that leaves the per-window numbers available. Those
are attributed to the domain the window was assigned to and to the fabric node it leans on most, as EMAs against
the population's own EMA. Both cull rules then spare any unit that models its own material better than the
population manages, however seldom it is called. COMP_PROTECT=0 restores pure-utilization selection as the
ablation, and the run REPORTS how many units were spared -- a selection change that reports nothing cannot be
audited, and this one deliberately keeps units the ranking wanted dead.

First run: "11 of 19 live domains beat the population EMA (4.426 bits/window) on their own material".

Fixed while wiring it: _w is indexed by GLOBAL node id (the code below reads _w[:, _oid[rank]]), so _w.argmax(-1)
is already a node id -- indexing _oid with it treated a global id as a rank and went out of bounds.

Architecture, asked and answered by reading rather than recalling: the base LM is a GRU by default and every
number this project has produced is a GRU number. MODEL=transformer exists (4 layers, 8 heads, causal,
norm_first) and had never been run. Attention appears in exactly two places and neither is self-attention over
the sequence: the fabric's routing softmax over MODULES, and the memory read (cosine -> top-k -> softmax, no
learned projections). Fabric nodes are residual MLPs; experts are low-rank adapters; the world model is MLPs;
the SigEncoder is a GRU regardless of MODEL.

So the pilot now runs BOTH and prints them side by side, since a 1-layer GRU may be the ceiling rather than the
system, and the GPT-2 reference I have been quoting is a transformer.

9d90416e34f6bd27c2c1125eefe6c19233d227a8
2026-07-31
measure the EXPERTS, and put output and retention above the domain scores
The stated goal is the output, continual learning without exorbitant forgetting, and old capacities surviving.
Domain counts are a diagnostic for those and I have been reporting them as if they were the result. Two changes.

1. EXPERTS: a new section, because nothing measured whether the population is good at anything. The fabric block
   reports node MASS -- how routing load is spread -- and load is not competence. A population can spread mass
   perfectly with every node doing the same undifferentiated job, which is exactly what DIV_W=0 permits once
   BAL_WARM has decayed the only other pressure to 0 by step 4000.
   It asks instead: is the material a node WINS modelled differently by that node than by the population? Against
   a null, because per-node bits/byte is mostly material difficulty -- the same windows re-scored with the node
   assignment SHUFFLED, 20 times, with the spread reported. First run of it, on a toy stream:

       64 nodes, 5 of them win at least one of 32 held-back windows
       SPECIALIZATION (mean |node - population|)  0.179
       shuffled-assignment null                   0.161 +/- 0.054
       >> INTERCHANGEABLE
       (5 of 64 nodes used: unused nodes are capacity the router never calls on)

   The population grew to its 64-node cap and the router calls on five of them, which are not distinguishable
   from a random split of the same windows. That is a far more useful thing to know than a domain count.

2. Reading order in both launchers now states what the project is FOR: 1. THE OUTPUT (generation first -- it IS
   the deliverable, everything else is a proxy), 2. CONTINUAL LEARNING WITHOUT EXORBITANT FORGETTING, 3. the
   machinery, only insofar as it moves 1 and 2. Domain counts, purity, silhouette, V-measure and CAN A DOMAIN
   PREDICT are labelled DIAGNOSTICS, NOT TARGETS, with a note that a domain count going up is not a result and
   that a diagnostic disagreeing with 1 and 2 is the diagnostic's problem.

Also retracted a stale claim still in rerun.sh's header: "FABRIC alone is worth +0.709 bits/byte". That was the
eval-time knockout, not the retrained ablation, which says 3.089 vs 3.090.

98f19fa0fe99d7be82e2f9108413d5307df50b5c
2026-07-31
read one corpus CONTIGUOUSLY: more than half of English's domains were our seek points
Domains stay -- they are expected to appear within English, and they do. But seg_from ALWAYS drew from a random
offset, so a single-corpus stream jumped elsewhere in the same corpus every SEG_MIN..SEG_MAX bytes. Those
discontinuities are ours, at a spacing we chose, and the assembler duly found domains at them. eng_only
reporting 71 domains at SEG_MIN=700 was partly a count of our own splices.

A/B, same seed, same everything, only the read mode differing:

    SEG_CONTIG=0 (random offset)   31 LIVE domains   order-1 3.500 | MODEL 3.349
    SEG_CONTIG=1 (contiguous)      13 LIVE domains   order-1 3.498 | MODEL 3.513

More than half the domains were seek artefacts. 13 is the number that is about English.

Contiguous is also the HARDER stream -- bits/byte is worse -- which is the expected direction: reading in order
means fresh text rather than re-sampled spans, and the boundaries left are document ends, topic changes and
register shifts that are actually in the text.

Default: contiguous when there is ONE corpus, random when there are several. The multi-corpus splice IS the
random-offset experiment, and flipping it would silently invalidate every earlier comparison; SEG_CONTIG= forces
either. The cursor persists across epochs, so epoch N+1 continues where N stopped rather than re-reading the
head -- which is also how a 20 GB corpus gets streamed in order instead of seek-sampled.

a3ed1a949eddad37d00dc6d62a1b5c15bb15e97a
2026-07-31
english is ONE corpus, and the phase schedule is generated rather than tabulated
Two corrections, both of the same kind: I kept putting our partition into material that has none.

1. English-only means ONE corpus. Splitting it into `eng` (fineweb-edu) and `web` (openwebtext) was a splice WE
   invented so that PHASED would have two processes to fade between -- and then every clustering and coherence
   score would have been measured against our invented split. The pilot and the long run now take a single
   English corpus and let the ASSEMBLER find whatever structure is in it. Nothing tells it where boundaries are.
   A single corpus does make the phase schedule stationary. That is the honest state of affairs: the
   non-stationarity worth measuring is not one we manufactured by interleaving two web scrapes, it is a genuinely
   new area ARRIVING, which is what `add` does to an already-trained system.

2. _phases was a lookup table keyed on n -- which replaced a single hard-coded 4-process list, i.e. I fixed
   arbitrary-constant with arbitrary-constants. It is now a rule: a sliding window of PHASE_W processes over n,
   across PHASES phases, every process entering and fading, and never all-active in the last phase (that is what
   `faded` is computed from). Applies at any n:
       n=2 -> [0] [0] [1] [1]        n=4 -> [0,1] [1,2] [1,2] [2,3]
       n=6 -> [0,1,2] [1,2,3] [2,3,4] [3,4,5]
   PHASE_SCHED="0|0,1|0,1|1" overrides it outright, and a malformed value fails at startup instead of silently
   becoming a different experiment.

Consequence that had to be handled: COHERENCE required >= 2 spliced corpora and SKIPPED otherwise -- so an
English-only run would have lost the one metric that speaks to "is this proper language". It now falls back to
the SELF-ASSEMBLED domains and asks whether a continuation stays in the domain the system itself put its seed in.
Labelled [SELF-ASSEMBLED reference] and explicitly called weaker evidence, because the partition being scored is
the system's own -- a tidy score there could mean the encoder is self-consistent rather than that the text is
coherent. Verified on a single-corpus run: floor 1/21 domains, ceiling 0.38, model 0.07.

c411ac70c2486c32e1cfd39c215c66d3ee916685
2026-07-31
the phase schedule was hard-coded for four processes, and English-first uses two
Running multi-epoch for the first time (it works: fresh resample per epoch, step keeps counting, probe survives)
surfaced a bug that lands squarely on the configuration we just chose. PHASE_SCHED was the fixed
[[0,1],[0,1,2],[1,2,3],[2,3]], filtered to existing processes ONLY inside build_stream, with an
`or list(range(NP))` fallback when the filter emptied it. At NP=2 that gives:

    [0,1] -> [0,1] -> [1] -> []  ->  ALL

so the final "fade" phase was the LEAST non-stationary of the four and process 0 never faded at all.

Five other places read PHASE_SCHED raw, unfiltered:
  - the phase banner printed "active processes [2, 3]" for processes that do not exist -- confirmed in a run
  - the learning curve's was_active flag was wrong for every NP < 4
  - UNLEARN computes faded = [p for p in labels if p not in PHASE_SCHED[-1]], which at NP=2 marks EVERY
    process as faded

Derived from NP once, so every reader sees the same schedule. NP=2 is [0] -> [0,1] -> [0,1] -> [1]: ends with 0
GONE rather than with everything back on, because the last phase is what `faded` is computed from and a schedule
ending [0,1] leaves nothing faded -- which is how the unlearn-a-faded-process test came to report as passing
without ever running. That shape is also the experiment being asked for: learn one thing, add a second, take the
first away, measure what survived. Verified: PHASE 3 now shows [1], and the faded-process unlearn fires.

longrun.sh gains `pilot` and `pilot-add`: 2 x 30 MB, 4 MB/epoch x 8 epochs = 32 MB, ~52k steps, ~15-20 min. That
step count matters -- it is the first configuration in this project to pass PONDER_WARM=8000 and BAL_WARM=4000,
so the pilot tests the fabric schedule as well as the plumbing. Banner reports the ACTUAL settings rather than
the defaults, since a banner that lies when overridden is how a run gets filed under the wrong description.

471318cd2ec75a5e280ef28edcd276ae40c75faf
2026-07-31
english first, then ADD -- and the measurement that makes adding an area meaningful
Dropping wikipedia and starting on English changes what the run is for, and exposed that the thing it needs was
missing. Every retention figure in this file is computed on the CURRENT stream:

    _at = [a for a in range(...) if labels[a] == _p]      # RETENTION picks windows from `stream`

So the moment a new domain is introduced, the only question that matters -- did adding it damage what was already
known? -- is unanswerable. Old and new material are both in the new stream and both were just trained on. Nothing
survived a run boundary.

Added a held-out probe keyed by domain NAME (not index: adding a domain shifts every index after it, so an
index-keyed probe would silently compare `eng` against `py`). Window draw is seeded from the name with a
deterministic hash, because Python's hash() is salted per process and would redraw the probe every run. Stored in
every checkpoint; on the next run the same held-out text is scored again and the delta reported per domain, with
domains that did not exist before marked NEW rather than folded into a mean.

Per-window rather than pooled, so it carries a standard error and says HELD when a change is inside it. Pooling
would have produced one figure with no way to tell forgetting from sampling noise -- which is exactly how the
coherence metric went wrong one commit ago, and there is no excuse for repeating it in the next section down.

Verified end to end: train on eng,py -> resume with eng,py,num ->
    eng   was 4.263 -> now 4.486   +0.223 +/- 0.047  WORSE (forgetting)
    py    was 4.314 -> now 4.006   -0.308 +/- 0.141  better
    num   3.637 +/- 0.040   NEW this run
    mean +/- 0.074 -- inside the noise, do not read this as forgetting

longrun.sh: fetch is now 10 GB fineweb-edu + 10 GB openwebtext, two registers of English and nothing else. New
`add` subcommand pulls a new area, resumes from the trained checkpoint with the domain appended, and writes to a
SEPARATE checkpoint so the English one stays intact if the addition goes badly.

10842e8ea4a40b50f1410ff5454342220ccc69c3
2026-07-31
balance the fetch: the stream samples domains UNIFORMLY, so corpus size buys repetition not attention
build_stream picks each segment with random.choice(act) -- uniform over the ACTIVE domains, never weighted by
how much text a domain has. So every domain contributes the same stream volume regardless of corpus size, and an
unbalanced pull does not give the big domain more attention: it gives the SMALL one more REPETITION.

The sizes I shipped an hour ago were 20/8/10/1 GB. Over a 40 GB stream that is one HALF-pass of fineweb against
roughly a hundred passes of oasst1 -- the dialogue domain memorised while the web domain was barely read, on a
run whose whole point is continual learning rather than memorisation.

Now 10/10/10/10, and oasst1 is dropped for the same reason: it is far too small to hold up a quarter of the
stream whatever --gb says. openwebtext takes the fourth slot, so the four registers are curated web /
encyclopedic / mixed-incl-code / raw web. Dialogue is worth having, but only at a size comparable to the others.

prep the multi-day run: resume was broken, and the corpus glob ate the fetch manifest
Tested RESUME end-to-end for the first time instead of assuming it. It crashed:

    File "self_organize.py", line 1027, in _absorb
      s.last[a] = max(...); s.born[a] = min(s.born[a], s.born[b])
  KeyError: 0

asm.born and asm.act were the two assembler fields nothing saved. _absorb reads s.born[a] with NO default, so
the first domain merge after ANY resume died -- meaning every resumed run crashed within DOM_MANAGE_EVERY steps.
That is the entire recovery path for a run measured in days. act is the DECAYED use that drives culling; empty on
restore, every domain looks unused and the first manage() invites a mass cull. Both now saved and restored, with
defaults so older checkpoints still load. Verified: resume -> merge at step 400 -> exit 0, no traceback.

The optimizer-moment restore still fails after growth (add_param_group during training gives the checkpoint more
param groups than a freshly built optimizer). The parameter SET matches and only the grouping differs, but
remapping moments across a different flattening would attach them to the wrong tensors -- worse than restarting
them. Left restarting, with the message now saying so and quantifying it: Adam re-warms in ~1000 steps, ~20 s at
the observed rate, and the first [rate] line after a resume shows the transient.

Also: open_corpus globbed `*`, which picks up fetch_big.py's _fetch_manifest.json and splices it into the corpus
as training text. Harmless at 300 bytes and undetectable by anything downstream, but a 40 GB pull writes one per
domain. Filtered, plus a clear error when DOMAINS names a domain with no data at all.

longrun.sh: fetch (4 distinct sources, resumable) | run | resume | watch. Four sources rather than 40 GB of
fineweb into one domain, because PHASED needs >= 2 genuinely different processes -- a single-corpus run
degenerates to stationary and the forgetting test becomes vacuous. PONDER_WARM and FAB_MIN_STEPS are left alone
deliberately: the point of the run is that step finally passes 8000 and the designed schedule completes.

6f24bed306496fcf27011965ceab45fa39e2b5fd
2026-07-31
coherence was a four-sample statistic and I read it as a finding, twice
Every coherence number this project has printed landed exactly on 0.25/0.50/0.75/1.00. That is not a
coincidence, it is the signature of a mean over four samples: the metric scored the GEN_PROCS=4 printed
generations, and a ~200-token continuation at WIN=256 with stride WIN//2 is about TWO windows. Standard
error of a four-sample mean here is 0.25, so every difference anyone has quoted off this line was one
sample flipping:

  "memory now HELPS coherence (0.50 -> 0.75)"        -- me, last run
  "the fabric buys coherence (0.75 vs 0.50)"         -- me, used to defend the FABRIC default
  "memory HURTS coherence (0.75 -> 0.50)"            -- the same measurement, next run, opposite sign

Coherence is the metric closest to "is this proper language", which is the one thing that is supposed to
matter, and it had no resolution at all.

Now measured on its OWN sample -- COH_N=16 seeds x COH_LEN=384 tokens, ~10x the decisions -- with the
standard error PRINTED and the HELPS/HURTS verdict gated at 2 sigma. First run of the fixed version:
model ALONE 0.47 +/- 0.10 | model+MEMORY 0.44 +/- 0.09 | memory is NEUTRAL (-0.03 +/- 0.14, inside the
noise). The printed generations still exist to be read by eye; they are no longer the sample.

Also fixed the ablation table's +mem column, which matched bare "MEMORY [0-9.]+" and so picked up the
COHERENCE line's "model+MEMORY 0.50" for five arms and the FABRIC line's bits/byte for the sixth -- two
different quantities in one unlabelled column.

And re-pinned the gate: the new defaults are 32 autoregressive generations per arm, which took a smoke arm
from 25 s to 3.3 min. The gate asks "does this arm reach the report"; COH_N=2 COH_LEN=96 answers that.
Resolution is the measurement's job, and the measurement runs on the GPU.
